<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Short directional Hilbert transform** ([source](#intro:section)): For $0<\epsilon<a$, define the short directional Hilbert transform by $$H_{v,a}^{\epsilon}f(x) =\int_{\epsilon<\|t\|<a}f(x-tv(x))\,\frac{\mathrm dt}{t}.$$
- **Tile** ([source](#stmt-red-packets)): A tile $s$ has a length $\ell_s\in\mathcal L$, a bounded slope $\theta_s$, a center $z_s=(x_s,y_s)$, and precision $d_s=w/\ell_s$.
- **Tile box and t-box** ([source](#stmt-red-packets)): Its box is $$R_s=\left\{(x,y): \|x-x_s\|\le\frac{\ell_s}{2},\quad \|(y-y_s)-\theta_s(x-x_s)\|\le\frac W2\right\}, \qquad \|R_s\|=\ell_s W.$$
- **Vertical band cutoff** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A vertical band cutoff at length $w>0$ is a multiplier $P_w=p(wD_y)$, where $p$ is smooth and supported in a fixed compact annulus $\{r:c_\eta\le\|r\|\le C_\eta\}$, with $0<c_\eta<C_\eta<\infty$ absolute.
- **Box weight** ([source](#tile:eq-density-weight)): For a box $R$ of length $l$, width $W$, slope $t$, and center $z_R=(x_R,y_R)$, put $$\label{tile:eq-density-weight} B_R(x,y)= \left(1+\frac{\|x-x_R\|}{l} +\frac{\|(y-y_R)-t(x-x_R)\|}{W}\right)^{-D_0}.$$
- **Tile density** ([source](#tile:eq-density)): For a box $R$ of length $l$, width $W$, slope $t$, and center $z_R=(x_R,y_R)$, put $$\label{tile:eq-density-weight} B_R(x,y)= \left(1+\frac{\|x-x_R\|}{l} +\frac{\|(y-y_R)-t(x-x_R)\|}{W}\right)^{-D_0}.$$
- **Size of a coefficient family** ([source](#tile:eq-size)): $$\label{tile:eq-size} \operatorname{size}(v)= \sup_T \left(\frac{1}{\|R_T\|} \sum_{\substack{s\in T\\\|t-\theta_s\|\geq c_1d_s}} \|v_s\|^2\|R_s\|\right)^{1/2}.$$
- **Test tree** ([source](#tile:eq-tree)): A test tree has parameters $T=(z_T,t,l)$, where $l\leq\tau$ is dyadic, and a box $R_T$ of length $l$, width $W$, and slope $t$.
- **Top mask** ([source](#eq:src-8)): Let $A>2$ be a fixed exponent to be chosen sufficiently large after the unmasked top estimates in <a href="#eq:src-11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-11">[eq:src-11]</a>.
- **Eligible mass** ([source](#hier:eligible-mass)): $$\mathcal T(v) =\{T:s_T\in v,\ d_T\le d(i)/B^2\}, \qquad n_v(z)=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T(v)}h_T(z). \label{hier:eligible-mass}$$ We call these tops eligible at $v$.
- **Analytic amplitude** ([source](#eq:src-15)): For a nonempty node $v$ at level $h$, define the $\mathcal H$-valued analytic amplitude by $$A_v(\zeta,z) =\sum_{\substack{s:s_{T(s)}\in v\\ k_s\ge h}} q_{T(s)}(z)\phi_s(z)\, n_{v_{k_s}(T(s))}(z)^{-\mu/K} \prod_{i=h}^{k_s-1}w_{v_{i+1}(T(s))}(\zeta,z). \label{eq:src-15}$$ The condition $k_s\ge h$ already ensures eligibility of $T(s)$ at $v$.
- **Interval-sum 3-variation** ([source](#conc:variation-definition)): For a finite sequence $(F_k)$ of functions with values in $\mathcal H$, define its interval-sum variation by $$\label{conc:variation-definition} \mathcal V_3(F_k)(x) =\sup_{\mathcal I} \left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal I} \left\\|\sum_{k\in I}F_k(x)\right\\|_{\mathcal H}^{3}\right)^{1/3},$$ where $\mathcal I$ ranges over finite families of disjoint integer intervals.
- **Phase exponents** ([source](#phase:parameters)): Fix the absolute constants $$\epsilon_0=\frac14, \qquad \kappa_0=2^{-10}, \qquad c_{\rm ph}=2^{-60}. \label{phase:absolute-constants}$$ For the bound $D\ge1$ from the preceding section and for $p\ge4$, put $$\alpha=2+\frac{c_{\rm ph}}{\log(2D)}, \qquad \mu=\frac{\alpha-1}{p}, \qquad r_* = \frac{1}{1-\mu}. \label{phase:parameters}$$ In particular, $2<\alpha<7/3$, $0<\mu<1/3$, and $1<r_*\le3/2$.
- **Last eligible levels** ([source](#hier:stopping-levels)): $$k_s=\max\{i\ge0:d_s\le d(i)/B^2\}, \qquad k_T=\max\{i\ge0:d_T\le d(i)/B^2\}. \label{hier:stopping-levels}$$ The initial precision choice makes these sets nonempty.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-outer-length-constant | absolute outer length constant a_* | < | $1/2$ | absolute constant, independent of the field |
| q-kakeya-weak-norm | weak L2 norm of the popularity maximal operator | ≤ | $C\delta^{-1/2}$ | constant independent of the field and delta |
| q-restricted-log-decay | restricted band form decay factor | ≤ | $C\bigl(1+\left\|\log(\|F\|/\|E\|)\right\|\bigr)^{-3}$ | uniform in band scale and inner truncation |
| q-short-time-cutoff | short-time cutoff tau | ≤ | $e^{-L^{0.97}}$ | L at least the absolute threshold |
| q-bin-number | bin parameter m | ≥ | $e^{L^{0.9}}$ | L at least the absolute threshold |
| q-bin-error | total commutation error of bin diagonalization | ≤ | $C m^5\tau\left\\|f\right\\|_2\left\\|g\right\\|_2$ | large L, fixed exponents |
| q-improved-model-form | improved packet form bound | ≤ | $C L^{-20}\sqrt{\|F\|\|E\|}$ | log of the measure ratio between L/2 and 2L |
| q-reverse-disparity-exponent | reverse-disparity power gain exponent | = | $9/20$ | all subcollections |
| q-separated-count | size of a separated list of significant tops | ≤ | $D/100$ | D a fixed power of L |
| q-fourier-exponent | improved coefficient exponent r_* | = | $\frac{1}{1-\mu}$ | D and p as stated |
| q-popular-adjoint-saving | integral of the popular adjoint sum over F | ≤ | $C L^{-20}N\|F\|$ | L sufficiently large |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-fixed-time** [claim](#stmt-red-fixed-time) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-fixed-time): change of variables for a bi-Lipschitz vertical map, then Minkowski on endpoint differences -> uniform Lp control of shifted truncations
- **s-kakeya-input** [claim](#stmt-red-kakeya) / [proof](#stmt-red-kakeya): external maximal theorem used as input -> weak L2 bound for popularity maximal operator
- **s-carleson** [claim](#stmt-red-carleson) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-carleson): Carleson measure condition plus maximal theorem -> Carleson embedding
- **s-commutator** [claim](#stmt-red-commutator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-commutator): dyadic frequency decomposition of the Lipschitz function and kernel moments -> commutator bound
- **s-composition** [claim](#stmt-red-composition-commutator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-composition-commutator): differentiate conjugated family in the time parameter and integrate -> commutator with composition operator
- **s-restricted-strong** [claim](#stmt-red-restricted-strong) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-restricted-strong): amplitude level decomposition and Young inequality -> strong L2 bilinear bound
- **s-band-implies-main** [claim](#stmt-red-band-implies-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-band-implies-main): random-sign square function, commutator reassembly, Fatou and Chebyshev -> main theorem
- **s-time-reduction** [claim](#stmt-red-time-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-time-reduction): integrate translation bounds against dt/\|t\| and use derivative of the band kernel -> removal of long and very short times
- **s-scale-truncation** [claim](#stmt-red-scale-truncation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-scale-truncation): geometric sums of dyadic kernel tails -> finite scale sum for the truncation
- **s-bin-levels** [claim](#stmt-red-bin-levels) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-bin-levels): dyadic measure classes with Cauchy-Schwarz and geometric tails -> summable amplitude weights
- **s-packet-representation** [claim](#stmt-red-packet-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-packet-representation): Fourier series in normalized coordinates, lattice averaging, adjacent grids -> average of packet forms
- **s-model-suffices** [claim](#stmt-red-model-suffices) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-model-suffices): combine time, scale and bin errors with the three model bounds -> band estimate
- **s-synthesis** [claim](#stmt-tile-lem-synthesis) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tile-lem-synthesis): Schur test at fixed length and orthogonality across lengths -> weighted synthesis bounds
- **s-size** [claim](#stmt-tile-lem-size) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tile-lem-size): stopping-time selection of high-energy trees -> size reduction with area budget
- **s-density** [claim](#stmt-tile-lem-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tile-lem-density): greedy selection of witnesses in decreasing length -> keeper boxes
- **s-tree** [claim](#stmt-tile-lem-tree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tile-lem-tree): Whitney decomposition of the horizontal line and cell suprema -> tree bound
- **s-ordinary-model** [claim](#stmt-tile-prop-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tile-lem-tree-2): dyadic thresholds alternating density and size removal, then layer cake for vectors -> ordinary model estimates
- **s-top-count** [claim](#stmt-forest-prop-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-forest-prop-count): enclose dilated boxes, apply popularity maximal bounds and layer cake -> weak L2 top count
- **s-mask** [claim](#stmt-forest-lem-mask) / [proof](#proof-stmt-forest-lem-mask): Fourier support of sinc powers and pointwise decay -> mask properties
- **s-single-scale** [claim](#stmt-conc-single-scale) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-single-scale): lattice sums of unequal-exponent envelopes -> single-scale envelope bounds
- **s-coefficient-carleson** [claim](#stmt-conc-coefficient-carleson) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-coefficient-carleson): duality with weighted maximal synthesis -> Carleson packing of main coefficients
- **s-annular-variation** [claim](#stmt-conc-annular-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-annular-variation): compare smooth truncations with dyadic conditional expectations, then martingale variation inequality -> variation bound for annular sums
- **s-john-nirenberg** [claim](#stmt-conc-john-nirenberg) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-john-nirenberg): dyadic stopping and exponential distribution -> normalized Lb bound
- **s-line-moments** [claim](#stmt-conc-line-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-line-moments): local mean and oscillation bounds on lines, then John-Nirenberg -> every-line moment bounds
- **s-top-good** [claim](#stmt-conc-top-good) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-top-good): weighted moments, vertical reproduction kernel and Markov inequality -> uniform top bounds off a small set
- **s-mask-removal** [claim](#stmt-conc-mask-removal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-conc-mask-removal): mask closeness near the top, far-region decay and the top count -> masked sum and count threshold
- **s-line-lemma** [claim](#stmt-count-line-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-count-line-lemma): adjacent dyadic grids, Carleson packing and exponential moment -> exponential tail for the count
- **s-simultaneous-count** [claim](#stmt-count-simultaneous) / [proof](#stmt-count-simultaneous): transfer witnesses to the longest top's reference line and apply the counting lemma -> separated significant direction count
- **s-quantile-gap** [claim](#stmt-phase-quantile-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-phase-quantile-gap): Hellinger affinity and quantile-separated events -> fourth-moment deficit
- **s-finite-fourier** [claim](#stmt-phase-finite-fourier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-phase-quantile-gap-2): dominant versus non-dominant coefficient split, then interpolation with constant one -> improved exponent bound
- **s-weight-variation** [claim](#stmt-hier-weight-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-weight-variation): telescoping masses across shifts and exponential increments -> variation of path weights
- **s-amplitudes** [claim](#stmt-hier-amplitudes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-amplitudes): summation by parts with top bounds and weight variation -> amplitude bounds
- **s-mass-motion** [claim](#stmt-hier-mass-motion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-mass-motion): logarithmic derivative bounds of positive sums of top weights -> slow motion of masses
- **s-moving-weights** [claim](#stmt-hier-moving-weights) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-moving-weights): bounded variation product rule and snapshot budget -> moving weight bounds
- **s-amplitude-motion** [claim](#stmt-hier-amplitude-motion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-amplitude-motion): vertical and horizontal derivative bounds then summation by parts -> predictable amplitude motion
- **s-good-paths** [claim](#stmt-hier-good-paths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-good-paths): high moment bounds on lines and Markov inequality -> good set
- **s-path-expansion** [claim](#stmt-hier-path-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-path-expansion): exact branching expansion read as path probabilities -> path expansion dominated by good paths
- **s-marking** [claim](#stmt-hier-marking) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hier-marking): telescope active drops and relative mass motion on cells -> marked cells and drop budget
- **s-fourier-average** [claim](#stmt-pred-fourier-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pred-fourier-average): clock selection of separated children and the finite Fourier inequality -> averaging of the prediction
- **s-marked-comparison** [claim](#stmt-pred-marked-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pred-fourier-average-2): denominator shrinkage, blending and normalization of step suprema -> averaging factors on marked cells
- **s-block-square** [claim](#stmt-res-block-square) / [proof](#proof-stmt-res-block-square): disjoint length ranges per block and goodness of the path -> square sum of residual blocks
- **s-common-weight** [claim](#stmt-res-common-weight-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-res-common-weight-variation): logarithmic increments with the full-root variation bound -> variation of common residual coefficients
- **s-path-product** [claim](#stmt-res-path-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-res-path-product): pointwise domination of the product by mesh step functions and iterated averaging -> integrated path product bound
- **s-vertices** [claim](#stmt-assembly-vertices) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-vertices): path expansion and integrated path product -> vertex Lp bound
- **s-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-assembly-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-interpolation): damped analytic family and three-lines lemma on the simplex -> central parameter bound
- **s-boundary-loss** [claim](#stmt-assembly-boundary-loss) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-boundary-loss): expectation over the common grid translation with an eligibility telescope -> expected boundary error
- **s-improved-model** [claim](#stmt-assembly-improved-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-improved-model): decompose into exceptional sets, mask error, boundary error and the central term -> improved popular model bound
- **s-all-model-estimates** [claim](#stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates): ordinary estimates for the first and third items, improved popular model for the second -> three model estimates
- **s-band-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-band-restricted) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates-2): apply the model-suffices proposition to the three model estimates -> restricted band estimate
- **s-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates-2): band estimate implies main theorem; principal value by Fatou, weak bound by Chebyshev -> uniform L2 and weak (2,2) bound

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-quartic-interaction-sum-at-most-one**: For a separated finite frequency list and nonnegative weights summing to one, the fourth-moment interaction sum over quadruples with an approximate additive relation is at most one.
- **inv-strict-deficit-without-dominant-weight**: If no single weight exceeds one minus kappa, the same interaction sum is strictly below one by an explicit quantitative deficit depending only on kappa.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-dominant-coefficient**: Use the strict deficit when one weight is close to the total mass. -> The deficit bound is only asserted when the largest weight stays below one minus kappa; with a dominant weight the sum can equal its ordinary bound and no gain exists.
- **anti-unseparated-frequencies**: Apply the interaction-sum bound to an arbitrary frequency list without separation. -> Unique completion of three indices to an admissible quadruple fails, so the unit bound on the sum is not available.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The interaction sum of the finite Fourier-gain lemma is a finite exact computation over a finite frequency list; the harness evaluates it exactly in rational arithmetic on fourth-power weights and checks the bound by one and the stated deficit. The analytic main theorem is not decided by this harness.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin tokens: d Delta n kn kd, then d integer frequencies, then d integer a_i with sum a_i^4 = n^4 (weights x_i = a_i^4/n^4); kappa = kn/kd
# PRECONDITIONS: frequencies pairwise Delta-separated, weights nonnegative integers satisfying the fourth-power sum identity, 0 < kappa < 1
# OUTPUTS: three lines: Q=<exact fraction>, LE1=<0|1>, GAP=<0|1|NA>
# TIME: O(d^4) exact rational operations
# SPACE: O(d) integers plus one running fraction
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic, no floating point; the exponent three-fourths becomes a cube because weights are fourth powers
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and exits nonzero
import sys
from fractions import Fraction


def main():
    tok = sys.stdin.read().split()
    d, delta, n = int(tok[0]), int(tok[1]), int(tok[2])
    kap = Fraction(int(tok[3]), int(tok[4]))
    lam = [int(t) for t in tok[5:5 + d]]
    a = [int(t) for t in tok[5 + d:5 + 2 * d]]
    q = Fraction(0)
    for i in range(d):
        for j in range(d):
            for k in range(d):
                for l in range(d):
                    if 4 * abs(lam[i] + lam[j] - lam[k] - lam[l]) < delta:
                        q += Fraction((a[i] * a[j] * a[k] * a[l]) ** 3, n ** 12)
    print("Q=%s" % q)
    le1 = 1 if q <= 1 else 0
    print("LE1=%d" % le1)
    mx = Fraction(max(a) ** 4, n ** 4)
    if mx <= 1 - kap:
        dk = kap ** 4 * (1 - kap / 2) ** 2 / 4096
        print("GAP=%d" % (1 if q <= 1 - dk else 0))
    else:
        print("GAP=NA")


main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an analytic operator bound for a directional singular integral on the plane. It states no program, interface or data format a software library could implement. Constants are only asserted to exist. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The estimate concerns an operator whose integration line depends on the output point. It does not describe a time-stepping, collision or rendering scheme, so it gives no stability guarantee for a game or physics simulation. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no tool, checker or workflow. Its constants are absolute but unspecified, so no tool can read off a number from the statement. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The reductions, stopping-time selections and path expansions are proof devices over finite collections whose estimates are independent of truncation. They are not stated as a procedure with a computable output or complexity. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Tiles, box lattices and angular grids are analytic bookkeeping in the proof. They are not offered as a storage layout or index with access costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The vertical bins form a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space with dimension-free constants, but no arithmetic, precision or memory model is given. No accelerator mapping is supported. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns adversaries, hardness or keys. The operator samples a straight line through each output point in a frozen direction, which has no security meaning. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only link is the Hilbert-space-valued bin vectors, which are finite-dimensional components of a proof. No learning problem, loss or generalisation claim appears. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or solver appears. The stopping and selection steps are analytic arguments, and the paper stresses that an unspecified constant would destroy the finite Fourier step. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There are no nodes, messages or consistency requirements. Uniformity of constants in the number of packets and levels is a mathematical statement, not a scalability claim for a system. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result belongs to pure harmonic analysis: a uniform bound controlled by the Lipschitz seminorm alone. It has no direct engineering reading, and the conjecture settled is a mathematical question. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-lipschitz-unit-field** (hypothesis): The uniform bound is stated for unit-length fields on the plane that are Lipschitz with seminorm at most one. It is not asserted for non-Lipschitz fields or fields with values outside the circle. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to discontinuous or merely measurable direction fields.', 'Applying the bound to vector fields on spaces other than the plane.']
- **limit-short-outer-length** (formal_scope): The estimate is a short-scale assertion, with the outer length tied to the reciprocal Lipschitz seminorm. It is distinguished from an estimate at arbitrary outer lengths for varying fields. Blocked use: ['Using the bound for full-line directional Hilbert transforms of a varying field.', 'Using an outer length larger than an absolute multiple of the reciprocal Lipschitz seminorm.']
- **limit-sub-lipschitz-regularity** (counterexample_scope): The source recalls an obstruction showing that Hölder regularity below Lipschitz does not by itself give a uniform weak estimate for the short positive averaging maximal operator. Lipschitz control is therefore essential and cannot be relaxed to a weaker modulus. Blocked use: ['Replacing the Lipschitz hypothesis by Hölder regularity of exponent below one.']
- **limit-unspecified-constants** (nonconstructive): The constants are asserted only to exist as absolute constants. The paper gives no numerical values for them, and the argument is an existence proof with fixed but unspecified exponents. Blocked use: ['Reading a concrete numerical bound for the operator norm off the theorem.', 'Using the theorem to certify a specific numerical error bound for a discretization.']
- **limit-band-strip-localization** (hypothesis): The restricted band estimate is proved for sets inside a horizontal strip of bounded length. Passing to the full plane relies on localization and square-function reassembly, not on a direct global restricted estimate. Blocked use: ['Using the restricted band bound on sets that are not contained in a bounded-length strip.']
- **limit-large-disparity-threshold** (edge_case): The packet parameters and the logarithmic improvement are set up only after a sufficiently large absolute threshold on the logarithm of the measure ratio. Below the threshold other fixed choices are made, so the improved decay is not claimed uniformly. Blocked use: ['Quoting the power saving in the logarithm for moderate or small measure ratios.']
- **limit-external-popularity-cost** (approximation): The maximal theorem used as external input has a weak L2 norm that grows like a negative power of the popularity. Any use of popular tops pays this cost, so the number of tops is controlled only through the stated polynomial losses. Blocked use: ['Treating the maximal operator bound as independent of the popularity parameter.']
- **limit-principal-value-schwartz** (edge_case): The principal-value assertion is stated on Schwartz functions and extended to L2 by density. Pointwise existence of the principal value for arbitrary L2 inputs is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Claiming pointwise existence of the principal value for general L2 functions.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 083 (`lean/docs/083.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A uniform Hilbert transform estimate for Lipschitz directions](../../preprints/A-uniform-Hilbert-transform-estimate-for-Lipschitz-directions-September-25-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves uniform $L^2$ bounds for short Hilbert transforms along Lipschitz unit vector fields in the plane, including fields depending on both coordinates. For Lipschitz constant $K>0$, every hard truncation with $0<\varepsilon\le R\le1/(10^6K)$ has one universal $L^2$ bound on Schwartz functions, independent of the inner cutoff.
>
> At a fixed short scale for $1$-Lipschitz fields, the formalization also gives principal-value and weak-$(2,2)$ estimates and bounded extensions to all of $L^2$. These results establish the stated short-scale form of Stein's question.

Comparator source `LipschitzHilbert.lean` (3544 bytes, sha256 `0f52347474ceb515213f6a8a05f46dc0d34001c8c7edea5af371b41752834c39`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

/- Use the normed-field structure on ℂ for real scalar multiplication. -/
attribute [-instance] instCommCStarAlgebraComplex

open MeasureTheory Set
open scoped ENNReal NNReal

namespace LipschitzHilbert

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)
abbrev DirectionPlane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

def sample (v : DirectionPlane → DirectionPlane) (t : ℝ) (x : DirectionPlane) :
    DirectionPlane := x - t • v x

/-- The symmetrized form of the hard-truncated directional integral. -/
def shortTransform (v : DirectionPlane → DirectionPlane) (ε a : ℝ)
    (f : DirectionPlane → ℂ) (x : DirectionPlane) : ℂ :=
  ∫ t in ε..a, t⁻¹ • (f (sample v t x) - f (sample v (-t) x))

def directionalKernel (v : DirectionPlane → DirectionPlane)
    (f : DirectionPlane → ℂ) (x : DirectionPlane) (t : ℝ) : ℂ :=
  t⁻¹ • f (sample v t x)

/-- The two hard-cutoff intervals in the directional Hilbert transform. -/
def twoSidedTruncation (v : DirectionPlane → DirectionPlane) (ε a : ℝ)
    (f : DirectionPlane → ℂ) (x : DirectionPlane) : ℂ :=
  (∫ t in -a..-ε, directionalKernel v f x t) +
    ∫ t in ε..a, directionalKernel v f x t

/-- The symmetric quotient extended continuously at zero for smooth inputs. -/
def smoothPrincipalValue (v : DirectionPlane → DirectionPlane) (a : ℝ)
    (f : DirectionPlane → ℂ) (x : DirectionPlane) : ℂ :=
  let g := fun t : ℝ => f (sample v t x)-f (sample v (-t) x)
  ∫ t in (0:ℝ)..a, Function.update (fun t => t⁻¹ • g t) 0 (deriv g 0) t

attribute [instance] instCommCStarAlgebraComplex

def ShortHilbertConclusion (a : ℝ) (C : ℝ≥0) : Prop :=
  ∀ v : Plane → Plane, LipschitzWith 1 v → (∀ x, ‖v x‖=1) →
    (∀ epsilon : ℝ, 0<epsilon → epsilon<a → ∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ,
      eLpNorm (twoSidedTruncation v epsilon a f) 2 volume≤C*eLpNorm f 2 volume) ∧
    (∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ,
      eLpNorm (smoothPrincipalValue v a f) 2 volume≤C*eLpNorm f 2 volume) ∧
    (∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ, ∀ level : ℝ≥0∞, level≠0 → level≠∞ →
      volume {x | level<‖smoothPrincipalValue v a f x‖ₑ}≤
        level⁻¹^2*((C:ℝ≥0∞)*eLpNorm f 2 volume)^2) ∧
    (∀ epsilon : ℝ, 0<epsilon → epsilon<a →
      ∃ T : Lp ℂ 2 (volume : Measure Plane) →L[ℂ] Lp ℂ 2 volume,
        ‖T‖≤C ∧ ∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ,
          (T (f.toLp 2 volume) : Plane → ℂ)=ᵐ[volume] twoSidedTruncation v epsilon a f) ∧
    (∃ T : Lp ℂ 2 (volume : Measure Plane) →L[ℂ] Lp ℂ 2 volume,
      ‖T‖≤C ∧ ∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ,
        (T (f.toLp 2 volume) : Plane → ℂ)=ᵐ[volume] smoothPrincipalValue v a f)

/-- Uniform hard truncation, principal-value and weak-(2,2) estimates,
with bounded L² extensions for all Lipschitz unit vector fields. -/
theorem main :
    ∃ a : ℝ, a∈Ioo 0 (1/2) ∧ ∃ C : ℝ≥0, 0<C ∧ ShortHilbertConclusion a C := by
  sorry

/-- The same uniform estimate at the reciprocal-Lipschitz truncation scale. -/
theorem uniform_truncation :
    ∃ C : ℝ, 0<C ∧ ∀ K : ℝ≥0, 0<K → ∀ epsilon R : ℝ,
      0<epsilon → epsilon≤R → R≤1/(1000000*(K:ℝ)) →
      ∀ v : Plane → Plane, LipschitzWith K v → (∀ x, ‖v x‖=1) →
      ∀ f : SchwartzMap Plane ℂ,
        eLpNorm (shortTransform v epsilon R f) 2 volume≤ENNReal.ofReal (C*‖f.toLp 2 volume‖) := by
  sorry

end LipschitzHilbert

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove a uniform <i>L</i><sup>2</sup> bound for the Hilbert transform along a Lipschitz unit vector field in the plane, with integration restricted to a fixed absolute multiple of the reciprocal Lipschitz seminorm. The bound is uniform in the inner truncation and holds for fields depending on both coordinates. This gives an affirmative answer to Stein's weak-$`(2,2)`$ conjecture.

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Short transforms and band reduction

Let $v:\mathbb R^2\to S^1$ be a Lipschitz unit vector field. For $0<\epsilon<a$, define the short directional Hilbert transform by $$H_{v,a}^{\epsilon}f(x)
 =\int_{\epsilon<|t|<a}f(x-tv(x))\,\frac{\mathrm dt}{t}.$$ The direction is held fixed at the output point $x$: the integral samples the straight line through $x$ in direction $v(x)$. For $f\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^2)$, the symmetric principal value $H_{v,a}f=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}H_{v,a}^{\epsilon}f$ exists at every point, since $f(x-tv(x))-f(x+tv(x))=O_x(|t|)$ near zero.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are absolute constants $a_*\in(0,1/2)$ and $C_*<\infty$ such that every Lipschitz field $v:\mathbb R^2\to S^1$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)\le1$ satisfies $$\sup_{0<\epsilon<a_*}
 \left\|H_{v,a_*}^{\epsilon}f\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}
 \le C_*\left\|f\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2)}
 \qquad(f\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^2)).$$ Its symmetric principal value therefore satisfies $$\left\|H_{v,a_*}f\right\|_2\le C_*\left\|f\right\|_2,
 \qquad
 \big|\{x:|H_{v,a_*}f(x)|>\lambda\}\big|
 \le C_*^2\lambda^{-2}\left\|f\right\|_2^2
 \quad(\lambda>0).$$*

</div>

Both coordinates may enter the field arbitrarily subject to the Lipschitz bound. The supremum in the theorem is outside the norm, so its first assertion is a uniform operator bound for each inner truncation. The principal-value assertion is stated on Schwartz functions and gives an $L^2$-bounded extension by density.

The outer length has a natural scaling. If $L_v=\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)>0$, put $\widetilde v(X)=v(X/L_v)$ and $\widetilde f(X)=f(X/L_v)$. Then $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\widetilde v)=1$, and changing variables $s=L_vt$ gives $$H_{v,a_*/L_v}^{\epsilon}f(x)
 =H_{\widetilde v,a_*}^{L_v\epsilon}\widetilde f(L_vx),
 \qquad \left\|\widetilde f\right\|_2=L_v\left\|f\right\|_2.$$ The theorem thus has the same constant at outer length $a_*/L_v$. If $L_v=0$, the field is constant. Rotation and Fubini reduce its transform, for any finite outer length, to the one-dimensional Hilbert transform. These conventions distinguish the short-scale assertion from an estimate at arbitrary outer lengths for varying fields.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and its predecessors

Stein’s question places Lipschitz regularity at the center of the planar directional singular-integral problem. In the formulation (M. Lacey and Li 2010, Conjecture 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[LLStein](#ref-LLStein)], it asks for a uniform weak-$(2,2)$ bound with the outer length controlled by the reciprocal Lipschitz seminorm. [thm:main](#thm:main) gives an affirmative answer with a strong $L^2$ bound. The scale restriction limits the turning of the field, but it does not make the operator a Fourier multiplier: the line used by the integral still varies with the output point.

Lacey and Li trace Stein’s question to Zygmund’s conjecture on almost-everywhere differentiation by averages over shrinking straight segments in direction $v(x)$. They also recall a Besicovitch obstruction showing that Hölder regularity of any exponent below one does not by itself give the corresponding uniform weak-$(2,2)$ estimate for the short positive averaging maximal operator (M. Lacey and Li 2010, Preface, Conjecture 1.2 and Chapter 2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LLStein](#ref-LLStein)] <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Bourgain1989](#ref-Bourgain1989)]. Analytic fields admit additional structure: Bourgain proved an $L^2$ theorem for their averaging maximal operator (Bourgain 1989), and Stein and Street proved local $L^p$, $1<p<\infty$, estimates for single-parameter singular Radon transforms associated with real-analytic maps (Stein and Street 2012) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[SteinStreet2012](#ref-SteinStreet2012)]. Their singular-integral theorem includes analytic frozen-direction Hilbert transforms, with localization depending on the analytic map. The uniform Lipschitz estimate considered here asks for constants controlled by the Lipschitz seminorm alone.

Several distinct advances explain the remaining difficulty. Lacey and Li proved an annular weak-$(2,2)$ estimate, together with strong $L^p$ estimates for $p>2$, for directional Hilbert transforms with measurable directions (M. T. Lacey and Li 2006, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[LLAnnular](#ref-LLAnnular)]. An annular restriction isolates a frequency scale; it does not give a uniform strong $L^2$ bound for the complete transform. For Lipschitz unit fields, Guo obtained annular weak-$(2,2)$ and strong $L^p$, $p>2$, bounds, uniformly in the annulus scale, for the $r$-variation, $r>2$, of smooth dyadic Hilbert-transform partial sums over the short scale range determined by the reciprocal Lipschitz length; the varying dyadic cutoff is distinct from the hard inner time cutoff in (Guo 2017b, Theorem 1.2 and (1.6)–(1.8) of the author version) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GuoVariation2017](#ref-GuoVariation2017)]. Lacey and Li’s Lipschitz Kakeya theorem controls averages over short rectangles on which the field has a prescribed popular direction (M. Lacey and Li 2006, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[LLKakeya](#ref-LLKakeya)]. For popularity $\delta$, its weak-$L^2$ norm costs $C\delta^{-1/2}$. We use precisely this estimate.

The Lacey–Li memoir develops the relation between those maximal operators and the Hilbert-transform problem (M. Lacey and Li 2010) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[LLStein](#ref-LLStein)]. Its Theorem 1.18 assumes a stronger maximal estimate below $L^2$ for the strong annular conclusion; its full-transform conclusion also requires $C^{1+\eta}$ regularity. These are conditional results with hypotheses different from those of [thm:main](#thm:main). For bounded measurable slopes depending on one coordinate, Bateman proved single-annulus $L^p$ estimates for $1<p<\infty$ (Bateman 2013) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Bateman2013](#ref-Bateman2013)]. Bateman and Thiele proved strong $L^p$ bounds for the full-line principal-value transform when $3/2<p<\infty$, for every nonvanishing measurable field depending on one coordinate (Bateman and Thiele 2013, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BatemanThiele2013](#ref-BatemanThiele2013)]. The one-coordinate $L^2$ case is already connected to Carleson’s theorem by a partial Fourier transform (Carleson 1966; Bateman and Thiele 2013) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Carleson1966](#ref-Carleson1966), [BatemanThiele2013](#ref-BatemanThiele2013)]; Bateman and Thiele obtain the stated wider $p$-range. The one-coordinate hypothesis remains a substantive restriction on the direction field. Guo extended the one-coordinate setting to fields constant along suitable Lipschitz foliations, with a uniform transversality assumption (Guo 2015, 2017a) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Guo2015](#ref-Guo2015), [Guo2017](#ref-Guo2017)].

A different family of results combines Lipschitz control with lacunary directions. Guo and Thiele proved strong $L^p$ bounds without a frequency restriction for short principal-value transforms associated with dyadic roundings of a positive Lipschitz slope (Guo and Thiele 2017) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GuoThiele2017](#ref-GuoThiele2017)]; Di Plinio and Parissis extended this framework to finite lacunary order for fields generated by separated dyadic roundings of Lipschitz angular functions (Di Plinio and Parissis 2018) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DiPlinioParissis2018](#ref-DiPlinioParissis2018)]. The rounded direction field can itself be discontinuous. These results exploit directional structure beyond that assumed in the present theorem.

A further reduction separates frequency reassembly from the main single-band estimate. Di Plinio, Guo, Thiele and Zorin-Kranich established that uniform strong $L^2$ bounds on vertical frequency bands imply the full short principal-value bound under a small uniform Lipschitz bound in the vertical variable (Di Plinio et al. 2018, Corollaries 1.3–1.4 of the author version) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[DiPlinioGuoThieleZorinKranich2018](#ref-DiPlinioGuoThieleZorinKranich2018)]. We give a commutator proof adapted to the two truncation endpoints and to the narrow frequency bins used later. The new estimate to be proved is the logarithmically improved restricted bound on a band; the principle of band-to-full reassembly is already present in that work.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The analytic and geometric reductions

We describe the proof before fixing its technical parameters. Localization and rotation write a short piece of the operator in slope form $$H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}h(x,y)
 =\int_{\epsilon<|t|<a}
 h(x-t,y-tu(x,y))\,\frac{\mathrm dt}{t},
 \qquad |u|\le1,\quad \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C,$$ with both input and test supported in a short horizontal strip. Writing $\eta$ for the frequency dual to $y$, a vertical frequency band at scale $w$ means that $|\eta|$ is comparable to $w^{-1}$. For its smooth projection $P_w$, the central restricted estimate is $$|\langle H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}P_w f,g\rangle|
 \lesssim
 \bigl(1+|\log(|F|/|E|)|\bigr)^{-3}\sqrt{|F||E|},
 \qquad |f|\le\mathbf 1_F,\quad |g|\le\mathbf 1_E.$$ Here $F,E$ are sets of positive finite measure in the horizontal strip. Its constants are uniform in $w$ and $\epsilon$. The logarithmic factor makes amplitude-level summation converge. A Lipschitz commutator then permits Littlewood–Paley reassembly across vertical bands. These implications, including all endpoint errors, are proved in this section before the restricted estimate itself is used.

The difficult regime is $|E|\gg|F|$. Write $L=\log\sqrt{|E|/|F|}$. Positive operator bounds discard all but very short integration times. The vertical band is then divided into narrow intervals centered at frequencies $\eta_j$, with common width comparable to $W^{-1}$, where $W=mw$ and $m$ grows subexponentially in $L$. The components form one finite-dimensional Hilbert space. A wave packet has horizontal length $\ell$, transverse width $W$, and a direction label with precision $d=w/\ell$. Smooth packet forms retain the original field only through scalar selectors of its direction.

Ordinary size and density estimates remove the small-density packets. Each remaining packet is assigned to a top box whose direction is popular on a measured subset of the test set. The popularity witnesses are disjoint. Lacey–Li’s theorem controls a decaying weighted count of the tops, after every thin parallelogram has been enclosed by an admissible orthogonal rectangle. This step is geometric: it supplies a polynomial cost in $L$, and does not provide the final oscillatory saving.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The phase gain and its iteration

Two statements make the oscillation usable. First, on every reference line the packet sums have polynomial moment bounds, square-function bounds and $3$-variation bounds. The variation argument combines Lépingle’s martingale inequality with the comparison between smooth Fourier truncations and conditional expectations developed by Jones, Seeger and Wright (Lépingle 1976; Jones, Seeger, and Wright 2008) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Lepingle1976](#ref-Lepingle1976), [JSW2008](#ref-JSW2008)]. All three packet estimates hold for every fixed deterministic subcollection. Popularity and Lipschitz control then produce one exceptional set outside which each separated list of significant top directions in the required angular cluster and precision range has at most $D=L^C$ members, simultaneously over angular resolutions.

Second, a finite Fourier sum with at most $D$ separated real frequencies has a strict improvement over the usual coefficient exponent. For suitable band-limited probability measures $\nu$, we prove $$\left\|\sum_{i=1}^d a_i e^{i\lambda_i t}\right\|_{L^p(\nu)}
 \le \left(\sum_{i=1}^d |a_i|^{r_*}\right)^{1/r_*},
 \qquad
 \frac1{r_*}=1-\frac{1+c/\log(2D)}p,
 \quad p\ge4.$$ The operator constant is exactly one, for complex coefficients and for Hilbert-valued coefficients acted on by diagonal phases. The proof separates the case of one dominant coefficient from its complement. In the latter case, separated quantiles give a strict fourth-moment deficit, which survives a small improvement of the coefficient exponent. An unspecified constant in this inequality would accumulate at every angular level and destroy the argument.

The iteration organizes directions into nested angular intervals. Each interval carries the positive mass of the tops whose direction precision is sufficiently fine for that interval; these are its eligible tops. It also carries an oscillatory amplitude assembled from its packets. The significant-direction count enters phase averaging through a weighted selection of separated block children, whose analytic weights cancel the probability cost of retaining them. We compare mass-normalized $p$-energies after adding the mass itself as a baseline. On an appropriate spatial interval, a finer amplitude can be predicted from one good point by diagonal phase motion. The finite Fourier estimate therefore controls the average of the predicted energy factor. The comparison errors are proportional to the relative mass lost on the path, so their sum is controlled by a logarithmic mass telescope.

Packets that stop between two angular endpoints create residual terms. Square summation handles blocks with a definite mass drop. Across nearly unary portions of a path, the energies telescope and the remaining residual sums are estimated by $3$-variation of one fixed sequence for that path. The sequence is chosen before the spatial point or mass stop is selected. Normalized factors on nested spatial cells can then be integrated without any cost proportional to the angular depth.

Finally we interpolate among shifted groupings of angular levels. At the central parameter the packet coefficients contain boundary weights. Averaging one common angular-grid translation removes these weights, using eligibility as well as angular proximity in the boundary telescope. With $p=L^{1/2}$, the strict phase gain leaves a decrease of order $(\sqrt L\log L)^{-1}$ in the exponent of $\sqrt{|E|/|F|}$. This dominates all fixed polynomial losses and supplies the logarithmic restricted estimate above.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization, inputs and uniformity

Section <a href="#intro:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:section">1</a> gives the analytic reduction and the precise packet estimates needed for the band theorem. Section <a href="#tile:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tile:section">2</a> proves the ordinary tile estimates and constructs the popular forest. Section <a href="#conc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conc:section">3</a> establishes every-line concentration and the simultaneous count of significant directions. Section <a href="#phase:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="phase:section">4</a> proves the constant-one finite Fourier inequality. Section <a href="#hier:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="hier:section">5</a> constructs the angular hierarchy and the predicted factors. Section <a href="#res:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="res:section">6</a> controls residuals and integrates path products. Section <a href="#assembly:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:section">7</a> performs interpolation, angular-grid averaging and the final strong-type assembly.

There are two specialized external inputs: Lacey–Li’s short Lipschitz-popularity maximal theorem, stated in [red:kakeya](#red:kakeya), and the unweighted scalar martingale $3$-variation inequality of Lépingle, used in the form (Zorin-Kranich 2020, Theorem 1.1, $p=2$, $r=3$, weight one) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[ZorinKranich2020](#ref-ZorinKranich2020)]. The latter enters the locally proved Fourier/martingale comparison in [conc:annular-variation](#conc:annular-variation); we credit its origin to (Lépingle 1976) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Lepingle1976](#ref-Lepingle1976)]. Standard one-dimensional Hilbert-transform, maximal-function and interpolation facts are recalled where used. The commutator, tile, concentration, finite-phase and hierarchy arguments are included in full.

All packet collections, scale ranges and angular depths are finite until estimates independent of those truncations have been proved. The bin Hilbert spaces are finite-dimensional and complex, and every constant is independent of their dimension. Pairings are linear in the first variable. A factor $L^C$ always has a fixed exponent; it never conceals the number of packets, scales or hierarchy levels.

<a id="red:section"></a>
#### Fourier conventions and the local reduction

We use the Fourier convention $$\widehat f(\xi,\eta)
   =\int_{\mathbb R^2}f(x,y)e^{-i(x\xi+y\eta)}\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dy,
 \qquad
 f(x,y)=\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\int_{\mathbb R^2}
       \widehat f(\xi,\eta)e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\,\mathrm d\xi\,\mathrm d\eta.$$ The same convention is used in one dimension. Pairings are linear in the first entry. A constant depending on a fixed finite list of smooth cutoffs or on their prescribed seminorms is an absolute constant. No such list will depend on a frequency scale, an inner truncation, the number of packets, or the dimension of the bin Hilbert space.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A maximal theorem and elementary analytic tools

We first record precisely the specialized maximal theorem used later. If an orthogonal rectangle $R$ has long side $L(R)$, short side $W(R)$, and long-side direction $e_R\in S^1$, let $\operatorname{EX}(R)$ be the arc centered at $e_R$ of total length $W(R)/L(R)$.

<a id="stmt-red-kakeya"></a>
<div id="red:kakeya" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Lacey–Li). *Let $v:\mathbb R^2\to S^1$ be Lipschitz and $0<\delta<1$. Restrict to rectangles satisfying $$L(R)\le \frac1{100\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)},\qquad
 \left|R\cap v^{-1}(\operatorname{EX}(R))\right|\ge\delta\left|R\right|,$$ where the first restriction is void when $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)=0$. Then $$\mathcal M_{v,\delta}h(z)
   =\sup_R\frac{\mathbf 1_R(z)}{\left|R\right|}\int_R\left|h\right|
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad
 \left\|\mathcal M_{v,\delta}h\right\|_{L^{2,\infty}}
       \le C\delta^{-1/2}\left\|h\right\|_2.$$ Here the supremum is tested at every point of $R$, and the constant is independent of $v$ and $\delta$.*

</div>

This is (M. Lacey and Li 2006, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[LLKakeya](#ref-LLKakeya)]. Requiring the field to lie in a smaller fixed subarc only restricts the collection of admissible rectangles. In the bounded slope range, the map $t\mapsto(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^2}$ changes slope distances into arc distances by comparable absolute factors. These observations explain the slope version of [red:kakeya](#red:kakeya) used below. When an intermediate box is a parallelogram, the application of the theorem will explicitly enclose it by a comparable orthogonal rectangle of admissible length.

<a id="red:horizontal-hilbert"></a>
We also use the following familiar one-dimensional estimates, with their indicated uniformities. The Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator is bounded on $L^p$, $1<p<\infty$, and $$\label{red:horizontal-hilbert}
 \sup_{0<r<R<\infty}
 \left\|\int_{r<|t|<R}h(\,\cdot-t\,)\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{t}\right\|_p
   \le C_p\left\|h\right\|_p.$$ The supremum in <a href="#red:horizontal-hilbert" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:horizontal-hilbert">[red:horizontal-hilbert]</a> is outside the norm; the usual maximal truncation theorem gives this assertion as well. See (Hajłasz 2012, Theorems 1.1 and 7.6 and the proof of (7.7)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Hajlasz2012](#ref-Hajlasz2012)]. Fubini gives the corresponding horizontal estimates on $\mathbb R^2$. The $L^2$ square-function identities used here are consequences of Plancherel and finite overlap of smooth frequency annuli. The standard smooth Littlewood–Paley and random-sign multiplier formulations are also recorded in (Tao 2006, Proposition 5.3, Corollary 5.4, and Remark 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Tao2006Notes4](#ref-Tao2006Notes4)]. We give the particular Carleson embedding and commutator arguments needed for our reduction below. The John–Nirenberg and martingale variation estimates are stated in the sections where their local forms are used.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The local slope operator and its endpoints

<a id="red:local-operator"></a>
Fix an absolute bound $C_{\rm Lip}$ large enough for the slope fields obtained in the following localization; for example, a bound of ten suffices after taking the squares small enough. Choose an absolute horizontal length $b_0>0$ with $b_0 C_{\rm Lip}<1/10$, and subsequently choose $0<a_*<b_0/100$. These constants may be decreased to accommodate the fixed geometric enlargements in later applications of [red:kakeya](#red:kakeya). All such enlargements are chosen absolutely. For the remainder of the local argument write $a=a_*$, and put $$\label{red:local-operator}
 H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}h(x,y)
   =\int_{\epsilon<|t|<a}
      h(x-t,y-tu(x,y))\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{t},
 \qquad 0<\epsilon<a,$$ where $\left|u\right|\le1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C_{\rm Lip}$. The local inputs and tests will be supported in $I\times\mathbb R$, where $I$ is any interval of length at most $b_0$.

<a id="stmt-red-fixed-time"></a>
<div id="red:fixed-time" class="lemma">

<a id="red:translate-bound"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *For $\left|t\right|\,C_{\rm Lip}<1$, define $$A_t h(x,y)=h(x-t,y-tu(x,y)).$$ For $1\le p<\infty$, $$\label{red:translate-bound}
 \left\|A_t h\right\|_p
   \le (1-\left|t\right|\,C_{\rm Lip})^{-1/p}\left\|h\right\|_p,$$ and $A_t$ is a contraction on $L^\infty$. Consequently, replacing either symmetric endpoint at scale $r$ by a measurable endpoint between $c r$ and $C r$, with $c,C>0$ fixed and $\max(1,C)r C_{\rm Lip}<1/2$, costs a bounded $L^p$ operator, independently of $r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-fixed-time"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-fixed-time</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed $x$, the map $\varphi_{x,t}(y)=y-tu(x,y)$ is increasing, onto, and satisfies $$(1-\left|t\right|\,C_{\rm Lip})\left|y-y'\right|
 \le\left|\varphi_{x,t}(y)-\varphi_{x,t}(y')\right|
 \le(1+\left|t\right|\,C_{\rm Lip})\left|y-y'\right|.$$ The change-of-variables formula for a one-dimensional bi-Lipschitz map, followed by translation in $x$, proves <a href="#red:translate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:translate-bound">[red:translate-bound]</a>. A difference of endpoint truncations is pointwise dominated by $$\int_{c' r<|t|<C' r}\left|A_t h\right|\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{|t|}$$ with $c'=\min(1,c)$ and $C'=\max(1,C)$. Minkowski and <a href="#red:translate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:translate-bound">[red:translate-bound]</a> bound this by $C_p\log(C'/c')\left\|h\right\|_p$. The same proof applies to inner and outer endpoints, and permits endpoints depending on $(x,y)$. ◻

</details>

We explain the localization of the original unit-field operator. Partition the plane into squares $Q$ of a sufficiently small fixed side length. For output in $Q$, only a fixed enlargement $Q^+$ of that square contributes to a transform of length $a_*$. Rotate coordinates so that the vector at the center of $Q$ points along the positive first coordinate axis. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)\le1$, on the rotated output square we have $$v=(v_1,v_2),\qquad v_1\ge c>0,
 \qquad u=v_2/v_1,\qquad \left|u\right|\le1,
 \qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C_{\rm Lip}.$$ The last inequality follows by subtracting the two ratios and using the lower bound for $v_1$. Extend $u$ to the plane with the same Lipschitz bound, using $\inf_{z\in Q}(u(z)+C_{\rm Lip}|\,\cdot-z|)$, and then clip the extension to $[-1,1]$. The infimum extension agrees with $u$ on $Q$, and clipping preserves both agreement and the Lipschitz bound.

At an output point, the substitution $s=t v_1(x,y)$ changes $\,\mathrm dt/t$ to $\,\mathrm ds/s$ and changes the two endpoints to $\epsilon v_1(x,y)$ and $a_*v_1(x,y)$. By [red:fixed-time](#red:fixed-time), each endpoint can be replaced by $\epsilon$ or $a_*$, respectively, at a uniform $L^2$ cost. The rotated sets $Q$ and $Q^+$ fit in a common horizontal interval of length at most $b_0$, after choosing the square side and $a_*$ in that order. Thus a uniform bound for $\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}$ implies the required bound on this output square. The output squares are disjoint, the sets $Q^+$ have bounded overlap, and rotations preserve area. Squaring the local estimates and summing over $Q$ therefore proves the global estimate. Notice that both endpoint errors are uniform in the inner radius $\epsilon$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The band theorem and summation of amplitude levels

A vertical band cutoff at length $w>0$ is a multiplier $P_w=p(wD_y)$, where $p$ is smooth and supported in a fixed compact annulus $\{r:c_\eta\le|r|\le C_\eta\}$, with $0<c_\eta<C_\eta<\infty$ absolute. We allow a fixed bounded family of such cutoffs, including slightly enlarged cutoffs equal to one on the supports of others. All constants below are uniform when the required finitely many cutoff seminorms are bounded. The dyadic inverse frequency scale $w$ belongs to $2^{\mathbb Z}$.

<a id="stmt-thm-band-restricted"></a>
<div id="thm:band-restricted" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:src-1"></a>
**Theorem 4** (Restricted estimate on a vertical band). *For the fixed absolute constants in <a href="#red:local-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:local-operator">[red:local-operator]</a>, there is an absolute $C$ such that, for every field $u$ with $\left|u\right|\le1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C_{\rm Lip}$, every $0<\epsilon<a$, every dyadic $w$, and every vertical band cutoff $P_w$, $$\label{eq:src-1}
 \left|\langle H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}P_w f,g\rangle\right|
 \le C\bigl(1+\left|\log(|F|/|E|)\right|\bigr)^{-3}
       \sqrt{|F||E|}$$ whenever $F,E\subset I\times\mathbb R$ have positive finite measure, $\left|f\right|\le\mathbf 1_F$, $\left|g\right|\le\mathbf 1_E$, and $\left|I\right|\le b_0$.*

</div>

The proof of [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted) will be completed after the packet estimates and their iteration have been established. The next results show, without using that theorem as a premise elsewhere in its proof, that it implies [thm:main](#thm:main). The last part of this section then gives explicit sufficient packet estimates for [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted).

<a id="stmt-red-restricted-strong"></a>
<div id="red:restricted-strong" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *Suppose a bilinear form $\mathcal B$ satisfies $$\left|\mathcal B(f,g)\right|
 \le C_0(1+|\log(|F|/|E|)|)^{-3}\sqrt{|F||E|}
 \quad (|f|\le\mathbf 1_F,\ |g|\le\mathbf 1_E)$$ on finite-measure sets in a fixed measurable domain. Then $$\left|\mathcal B(f,g)\right|\le C C_0\left\|f\right\|_2\left\|g\right\|_2$$ for finite amplitude decompositions, and hence for all $L^2$ inputs whenever the form is obtained by an $L^2$-continuous truncation or by completion from those inputs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-restricted-strong"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-restricted-strong</summary>

*Proof.* Use disjoint amplitude sets $F_i=\{2^{i-1}<|f|\le2^i\}$ and write $f=\sum_i2^i f_i$, where $|f_i|\le\mathbf 1_{F_i}$. For the moment all sums are finite. Put $a_i=2^i\sqrt{|F_i|}$, so $\sum_i a_i^2\le4\left\|f\right\|_2^2$. Group the indices into measure classes $$\mathcal I_k=\{i:2^k\le|F_i|<2^{k+1}\},\qquad
 A_k=\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_k}a_i^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ If $i_0$ is the largest index in a nonempty class, then $$\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_k}a_i
 \le 2^{(k+1)/2}\sum_{i\le i_0}2^i
 \le 2\sqrt2\,a_{i_0}\le2\sqrt2\,A_k.$$ Make the analogous decomposition $g=\sum_j2^j g_j$, with measure classes $\mathcal J_l$ and square sums $B_l$. For $i\in\mathcal I_k$, $j\in\mathcal J_l$, $$(1+|\log(|F_i|/|E_j|)|)^{-3}
       \le C(1+|k-l|)^{-3}.$$ The assumed bound, followed by the within-class estimates, gives $$\left|\mathcal B(f,g)\right|
   \le C C_0\sum_{k,l}(1+|k-l|)^{-3}A_kB_l
   \le C C_0\left\|A\right\|_{\ell^2}\left\|B\right\|_{\ell^2}.$$ The final step is Young’s inequality for the summable sequence $(1+|k|)^{-3}$. This proves the assertion. Truncating the amplitude decompositions and using the asserted continuity or completion gives the last statement, with no dependence on the number of classes. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A Lipschitz commutator

<a id="red:lp-square"></a>
The estimate below belongs to the Lipschitz commutator theory initiated by Calderón and developed by Coifman and Meyer (Calderón 1965; Coifman and Meyer 1978) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Calderon1965](#ref-Calderon1965), [CoifmanMeyer1978](#ref-CoifmanMeyer1978)]. We give a dyadic proof with the precise multiplier seminorms needed for Littlewood–Paley reassembly and for the later narrow-bin reduction. The argument separates low output frequencies from the annular interactions, because the constant must remain uniform as the number of frequency scales increases. Let $\chi$ be a real even smooth cutoff equal to one on $[-1,1]$ and supported in $[-2,2]$, and define $$S_k=\chi(2^{-k}D),\qquad \Delta_k=S_k-S_{k-1}.$$ Thus $\Delta_k$ is supported in $\{2^{k-1}\le|\zeta|\le2^{k+1}\}$, and Plancherel gives $$\label{red:lp-square}
 \left\|h\right\|_2^2\asymp\sum_k\left\|\Delta_kh\right\|_2^2.$$

<a id="stmt-red-carleson"></a>
<div id="red:carleson" class="lemma">

<a id="red:carleson-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 6** (The required Carleson embedding). *For every fixed integer $c$, every $q\in L^\infty(\mathbb R)$, and every $h\in L^2(\mathbb R)$, $$\label{red:carleson-estimate}
 \sum_k\left\|(\Delta_kq)S_{k+c}h\right\|_2^2
      \le C_c\left\|q\right\|_\infty^2\left\|h\right\|_2^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-carleson"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-carleson</summary>

<a id="red:carleson-measure"></a>
*Proof.* For a dyadic interval $J$ of length $2^{-j}$, split $q=q\mathbf 1_{3J}+q\mathbf 1_{(3J)^c}$. By <a href="#red:lp-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:lp-square">[red:lp-square]</a>, the first part satisfies $$\sum_{k\ge j}\int_J
       |\Delta_k(q\mathbf 1_{3J})|^2
       \le C\left\|q\right\|_\infty^2|J|.$$ The kernels of $\Delta_k$ have Schwartz decay at scale $2^{-k}$. For $x\in J$ and $k\ge j$, this gives $$|\Delta_k(q\mathbf 1_{(3J)^c})(x)|
     \le C\left\|q\right\|_\infty 2^{j-k}.$$ After summing the squares, we obtain $$\label{red:carleson-measure}
 \sum_{k\ge j}\int_J|\Delta_kq|^2
          \le C\left\|q\right\|_\infty^2|J|.$$ For each dyadic interval $I$ of length $2^{-k}$, set $\alpha_I=\int_I|\Delta_kq|^2$. Then <a href="#red:carleson-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:carleson-measure">[red:carleson-measure]</a> says $\sum_{I\subset J}\alpha_I\le C\left\|q\right\|_\infty^2|J|$.

Write $M$ for the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator. Kernel decay, summed on dyadic annuli about a point of $I$, gives $$\sup_{x\in I}|S_{k+c}h(x)|
       \le C_c\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_{y\in I}Mh(y).$$ The shift $c$ is fixed, so moving the kernel center across $I$ changes the bound by only a fixed factor. If $F\ge0$ belongs to $L^2$, the dyadic Carleson condition implies $$\sum_I\alpha_I
     \bigl(\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_I F\bigr)^2
       \le C\left\|q\right\|_\infty^2\left\|F\right\|_2^2.$$ Indeed, for each $t>0$, sum over the maximal dyadic intervals on which $\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_I F>t$, apply the Carleson condition on each such interval, and integrate against $2t\,\mathrm dt$. Their union lies, up to null sets, in $\{F>t\}$. Apply this assertion with $F=Mh$, and use the $L^2$ maximal theorem. This proves <a href="#red:carleson-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:carleson-estimate">[red:carleson-estimate]</a>. This is also the usual continuous Littlewood–Paley Carleson embedding; compare the proof of (Tao 2007, Proposition 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Tao2007Notes6](#ref-Tao2007Notes6)]. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-red-commutator"></a>
<div id="red:commutator" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *Let $b\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb R)$, and let $T=\mathfrak m(D)$, where $\mathfrak m$ is smooth away from zero and $$A(\mathfrak m)
   =\max_{0\le r\le4}\sup_{\zeta\ne0}
        |\zeta|^r|\mathfrak m^{(r)}(\zeta)|<\infty.$$ Then <a id="red:commutator-estimate"></a>$$\label{red:commutator-estimate}
 \left\|[b,T]\partial h\right\|_2
    \le C A(\mathfrak m)\left\|b'\right\|_\infty\left\|h\right\|_2.$$ The commutator is initially defined on the Sobolev space $W^{1,2}(\mathbb R)$; the estimate gives its bounded extension to $L^2$. Its constant is independent of $\left\|b\right\|_\infty$ and of all frequency truncations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-commutator</summary>

<a id="red:b-band"></a>
<a id="red:b-reconstruction"></a>
*Proof.* Put $L_b=\left\|b'\right\|_\infty$ and $b_k=\Delta_kb$. Cancellation and the first absolute moment of the smooth kernels give $$\label{red:b-band}
 \left\|b_k\right\|_\infty\le C2^{-k}L_b,
 \qquad \left\|\partial S_kb\right\|_\infty\le C L_b,
 \qquad b_k'=\Delta_kb'.$$ Also $\left\|S_Nb-b\right\|_\infty\le C2^{-N}L_b$. Consequently, for each fixed $j$, the exact identity $$\label{red:b-reconstruction}
 b=S_{j-4}b+\sum_{k\ge j-3}b_k$$ has a uniformly convergent high-frequency sum. This identity retains the constant and low-frequency part of $b$; no homogeneous reconstruction of a bounded function at frequency zero is needed.

<a id="red:kernel-first-moment"></a>
For any fixed smooth annular cutoff $\rho$, let $K_j$ be the kernel of $\mathfrak m(D)\rho(2^{-j}D)$. Rescaling and three integrations by parts on its fixed compact frequency support show $$\label{red:kernel-first-moment}
 \int_\mathbb R|z|\,|K_j(z)|\,\mathrm dz
       \le C A(\mathfrak m)2^{-j}.$$ In fact the rescaled kernel is bounded by $C A(\mathfrak m)(1+|z|)^{-3}$, which proves the assertion.

First suppose that $\widehat h$ is smooth and compactly supported away from zero. Put $h_j=\Delta_jh$; only finitely many $h_j$ are nonzero. Apply <a href="#red:b-reconstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:b-reconstruction">[red:b-reconstruction]</a> to each of them. There are three interactions.

For the low frequencies of $b$, put $B_j=S_{j-4}b$. The supports of both $h_j$ and $B_jh_j$ lie in a fixed annulus of radius $2^j$. A slightly enlarged annular multiplier $T_j$ therefore satisfies $[B_j,T]\partial h_j=[B_j,T_j]\partial h_j$. The kernel formula and <a href="#red:kernel-first-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:kernel-first-moment">[red:kernel-first-moment]</a> give $$\left\|[B_j,T_j]\partial h_j\right\|_2
 \le \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(B_j)\int |z||K_j(z)|\,\mathrm dz\,
             \left\|\partial h_j\right\|_2
 \le C A(\mathfrak m)L_b\left\|h_j\right\|_2.$$ The outputs remain in fixed annuli of radius $2^j$. Finite frequency overlap and <a href="#red:lp-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:lp-square">[red:lp-square]</a> bound their sum by $C A(\mathfrak m)L_b\left\|h\right\|_2$.

For $k\ge j+4$, the output of $[b_k,T]\partial h_j$ lies in a fixed annulus of radius $2^k$. Plancherel, Bernstein’s inequality, and <a href="#red:b-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:b-band">[red:b-band]</a> yield $$\left\|[b_k,T]\partial h_j\right\|_2
       \le C A(\mathfrak m)L_b 2^{j-k}\left\|h_j\right\|_2.$$ Group by $k$, use frequency overlap, and apply Young’s inequality on $\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$ to the summable sequence $2^{-n}\mathbf 1_{n\ge4}$. This bounds the high–low sum by the same quantity and proves its convergence in $L^2$.

<a id="red:commutator-high-high"></a>
It remains to consider $|k-j|\le3$. Fix one such difference and write $H_k=h_{k+r}$, $|r|\le3$. The exact identity $$\label{red:commutator-high-high}
 [b_k,T]\partial H_k
  =\partial([b_k,T]H_k)-b_k'TH_k+T(b_k'H_k)$$ treats the possible low output frequencies. Indeed, $\partial([b_k,T]H_k)$ has spectrum in a ball of radius $C2^k$. Its $\Delta_i$ projection vanishes for $i>k+c_0$, with $c_0$ fixed, and otherwise $$\left\|\Delta_i\partial([b_k,T]H_k)\right\|_2
    \le C A(\mathfrak m)L_b2^{i-k}\left\|H_k\right\|_2.$$ Another application of <a href="#red:lp-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:lp-square">[red:lp-square]</a> and Young’s inequality to $2^{-n}\mathbf 1_{n\ge-c_0}$ bounds the sum of these derivative terms. Thus arbitrarily low output frequencies cause no logarithmic loss.

Choose a fixed $c_1$ so that $S_{k+c_1}$ equals one on the spectrum of every product $b_k'H_k$. For $g\in L^2$, $$\begin{split}
 \left|\left\langle\sum_k b_k'H_k,g\right\rangle\right|
 &\le
 \left(\sum_k\left\|H_k\right\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\sum_k\left\|(\Delta_kb')S_{k+c_1}g\right\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\le C L_b
       \left(\sum_k\left\|H_k\right\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}\left\|g\right\|_2,
 \end{split}$$ by [red:carleson](#red:carleson). Since $\left\|T\right\|_{2\to2}\le A(\mathfrak m)$, this bounds $T\sum_k b_k'H_k$. Replacing $H_k$ by $TH_k$ in the same calculation bounds $\sum_k b_k'TH_k$: the frequency supports are unchanged and $\sum_k\left\|TH_k\right\|_2^2\le A(\mathfrak m)^2
\sum_k\left\|H_k\right\|_2^2$. Equation <a href="#red:commutator-high-high" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:commutator-high-high">[red:commutator-high-high]</a>, summed over its seven possible differences, completes the proof on the chosen dense class.

For $h\in W^{1,2}$, approximate by $(S_N-S_{-N})h$, followed if necessary by smooth compact Fourier approximations away from zero. The approximation converges in $W^{1,2}$. Since $b$ is bounded and $T$ is $L^2$-bounded, the defining commutators converge in $L^2$. The estimate passes to the limit, and then defines a bounded operator on all of $L^2$. ◻

</details>

For a vertical multiplier $T$, set $$C_t h(x,y)=h(x,y-tu(x,y)).$$

<a id="stmt-red-composition-commutator"></a>
<div id="red:composition-commutator" class="corollary">

<a id="red:composition-bound"></a>
**Corollary 8**. *For $\left|t\right|\le b_0$ and $T$ as in [red:commutator](#red:commutator), $$\label{red:composition-bound}
 \left\|TC_t h-C_tTh\right\|_2
      \le C A(\mathfrak m)\left|t\right|\,\left\|h\right\|_2.$$ The constant is uniform over $\left|u\right|\le1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C_{\rm Lip}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-composition-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-composition-commutator</summary>

<a id="red:conjugation-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Work first with a smooth field and on one fixed $x$-fiber. Let $\varphi_s(y)=y-su(x,y)$. Direct differentiation gives $$C_s^{-1}C_s'=b_s\partial_y,
 \qquad b_s=-u(x,\cdot)\circ\varphi_s^{-1},$$ and hence, since $T$ commutes with $\partial_y$, $$\label{red:conjugation-derivative}
 \frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm ds}(C_sTC_s^{-1})
       =C_s[b_s,T]\partial_y C_s^{-1}.$$ The bi-Lipschitz estimates in [red:fixed-time](#red:fixed-time) imply $$\left\|C_s\right\|_{2\to2}+\left\|C_s^{-1}\right\|_{2\to2}\le C,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(b_s)\le
    \frac{C_{\rm Lip}}{1-|s|C_{\rm Lip}}\le C.$$ Apply [red:commutator](#red:commutator) inside <a href="#red:conjugation-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:conjugation-derivative">[red:conjugation-derivative]</a>, integrate from $0$ to $t$, and multiply by $C_t$. The estimate is uniform in the fiber, so integration in $x$ proves <a href="#red:composition-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:composition-bound">[red:composition-bound]</a>.

A bounded Lipschitz field is approximated by convolutions with smooth approximate identities, preserving the bounds on its size and Lipschitz seminorm. For each fixed small $s$, the corresponding composition operators converge strongly on $L^2$: this follows first for continuous compactly supported inputs by uniform convergence of the maps, and then by density and their uniform operator bounds. Passing to the limit proves the assertion for the original field. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Square-function reassembly

The preceding composition estimate pays a factor of $|t|$, which cancels the singular measure $\,\mathrm dt/|t|$ when commutators are integrated. This is the mechanism that permits the band estimates to be assembled. Di Plinio, Guo, Thiele and Zorin-Kranich established this band-to-full reduction under Lipschitz regularity (Di Plinio et al. 2018, Corollaries 1.3–1.4 of the author version) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[DiPlinioGuoThieleZorinKranich2018](#ref-DiPlinioGuoThieleZorinKranich2018)]. We retain the argument here to track the fixed outer endpoint, arbitrary inner endpoint and horizontal localization used throughout this paper.

<a id="red:integrated-commutator"></a>
Choose a real smooth annular function $p$ so that $\sum_{w\in2^{\mathbb Z}}p(w\eta)^2=1$ for $\eta\ne0$. Such a function is obtained by dividing an ordinary smooth dyadic partition by the square root of its sum of squares. Set $P_w=p(wD_y)$. For every finite set $\mathcal W$ of scales and every choice of signs $\varepsilon_w\in\{-1,1\}$, the multiplier $T=\sum_{w\in\mathcal W}\varepsilon_wP_w$ satisfies $A(\mathfrak m_T)\le C$, by finite overlap of the annuli. Writing $S_t^x h(x,y)=h(x-t,y)$, we have $$H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}
      =\int_{\epsilon<|t|<a} C_t S_t^x\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{t}.$$ The vertical multiplier $T$ commutes with $S_t^x$. Thus [red:composition-commutator](#red:composition-commutator) implies $$\label{red:integrated-commutator}
 \left\|[T,H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}]\right\|_{2\to2}
       \le C\int_{\epsilon<|t|<a}|t|\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{|t|}
       \le C a.$$ All these operators are already bounded on $L^2$ for fixed $\epsilon>0$, with the elementary bound $C\log(a/\epsilon)$; no conclusion of [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted) is needed to form their square functions.

<a id="red:reassembly"></a>
Randomize the signs independently and use their orthogonality. For $f=\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}f$, Equation <a href="#red:integrated-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:integrated-commutator">[red:integrated-commutator]</a> and the triangle inequality in the random-sign $L^2$ space give $$\label{red:reassembly}
 \left(\sum_{w\in\mathcal W}
       \left\|\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}P_w H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}f\right\|_2^2
       \right)^{1/2}
 \le C\left\|f\right\|_2+
 \left(\sum_{w\in\mathcal W}
       \left\|\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}P_w f\right\|_2^2
       \right)^{1/2}.$$ The horizontal restriction commutes with every vertical multiplier.

<a id="stmt-red-band-implies-main"></a>
<div id="red:band-implies-main" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9**. *The uniform restricted estimate in [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted) implies [thm:main](#thm:main).*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-band-implies-main"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-band-implies-main</summary>

*Proof.* Apply [red:restricted-strong](#red:restricted-strong) to the localized band form. It gives a uniform local $L^2$ bound for $H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}\widetilde P_w$, where $\widetilde P_w$ is an enlarged cutoff equal to one on the spectrum of $P_w$. Since $P_w f$ retains the same horizontal support, $$\left\|\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}P_w f\right\|_2
 =\left\|\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}
                      \widetilde P_w(P_w f)\right\|_2
 \le C\left\|P_w f\right\|_2.$$ Insert this in <a href="#red:reassembly" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:reassembly">[red:reassembly]</a> and let $\mathcal W$ increase to all dyadic scales. Plancherel and the square partition give $\left\|\mathbf 1_{I\times\mathbb R}H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}f\right\|_2
\le C\left\|f\right\|_2$, uniformly in $\epsilon$. The localization already proved above gives the asserted global bound for the unit field. Finally, for a Schwartz input the symmetric principal value exists pointwise; Fatou gives its $L^2$ bound, and Chebyshev gives the weak estimate in [thm:main](#thm:main). ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Removing long and very short times

<a id="red:small-time-parameters"></a>
We now reduce [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted) to packet estimates. In this part only, abbreviate $H_{u,a}^{\epsilon}$ to $H$, and put $$L=\log\sqrt{|E|/|F|}.$$ Fix a sufficiently large absolute threshold $L_0$, which can be increased after the fixed constants in the argument are chosen. When $L\ge L_0$, choose dyadic numbers satisfying $$\label{red:small-time-parameters}
 \tfrac12 e^{-L^{0.97}}<\tau\le e^{-L^{0.97}},\qquad
 e^{L^{0.9}}\le m<2e^{L^{0.9}},\qquad W=mw.$$ Here $\tau$ is the short-time cutoff. The parameter $m$ sets the number of vertical frequency bins: their width will be comparable to $W^{-1}$, and $W=mw$ is the corresponding spatial width. For every fixed $C$, the product $m^C\tau$ tends to zero as $L\to\infty$; this pays for the binning errors. We take $L_0$ large enough that $\tau<a$. For all other values of $L$, let $m=1$, $W=w$, and let $\tau\in(0,a)$ be a fixed small dyadic constant.

<a id="stmt-red-time-reduction"></a>
<div id="red:time-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *For any fixed $1<p<\infty$, the contribution of $\max(\epsilon,\tau)<|t|<a$ to $HP_w$ has $L^p$ norm at most $C_p(1+L)$ if $L\ge L_0$, and at most a fixed $C_p$ otherwise. On the remaining times below $w$, replacing $u$ by zero costs a uniformly bounded $L^p$ operator. The resulting horizontal symmetric truncation is uniformly bounded on $L^p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-time-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-time-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* The first assertion follows from [red:fixed-time](#red:fixed-time), the uniform $L^1$ norm of the convolution kernel of $P_w$, and $$\int_{\tau<|t|<a}\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{|t|}
       =2\log(a/\tau)\le C(1+L^{0.97}).$$ For the second, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives $$\begin{split}
 &(P_wh)(x-t,y-tu(x,y))-(P_wh)(x-t,y)\\
 &\qquad=-t u(x,y)\int_0^1
    (\partial_yP_wh)(x-t,y-\theta t u(x,y))\,\mathrm d\theta.
 \end{split}$$ The intermediate maps satisfy [red:fixed-time](#red:fixed-time), and the kernel of $\partial_yP_w$ has $L^1$ norm $O(w^{-1})$. Consequently, <a id="red:short-time-error"></a>$$\label{red:short-time-error}
 \left\|(A_t-S_t^x)P_wh\right\|_p
        \le C_p |t|w^{-1}\left\|h\right\|_p.$$ Integrating over any subinterval of $0<|t|<w$ against $\,\mathrm dt/|t|$ gives a constant. Equation <a href="#red:horizontal-hilbert" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:horizontal-hilbert">[red:horizontal-hilbert]</a> handles the horizontal part, uniformly in its inner and outer endpoints. ◻

</details>

<a id="red:restricted-error"></a>
Every error with $L^p$ norm $B_p$ contributes at most $$\label{red:restricted-error}
 B_p|F|^{1/p}|E|^{1-1/p}
   =B_p\sqrt{|F||E|}
           \left(\frac{|F|}{|E|}\right)^{1/p-1/2}$$ to the restricted form. In the regime $L\ge L_0$, choose one fixed $p<2$, for example $p=3/2$; the normalized error is then at most $C(1+L)e^{-L/3}$. In the opposite regime $|F|\ge|E|$, the fixed choice $p=4$ gives the power $(|E|/|F|)^{1/4}$ for each uniformly bounded error. On a bounded range of $L$, fixed operator bounds suffice. These choices are made once and do not approach the endpoint $p=2$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### From a spatial truncation to a finite scale sum

Choose an even smooth cutoff $\chi_0$, equal to one near zero and compactly supported, and set $$\vartheta(r)=\chi_0(r)-\chi_0(2r),\qquad
 \psi(r)=-i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(r)\vartheta(r).$$ The function $\psi$ is smooth and supported in a fixed compact annulus. Its inverse Fourier transform $k$ is Schwartz, and $k_\ell(t)=\ell^{-1}k(t/\ell)$ has multiplier $\psi(\ell\zeta)$. Since $\sum_{\ell\in2^{\mathbb Z}}\vartheta(\ell\zeta)=1$ for $\zeta\ne0$, $$\sum_{\ell\in2^{\mathbb Z}}k_\ell(t)=\frac1t\qquad(t\ne0).$$ The identity follows by Fourier inversion in tempered distributions; away from zero the kernel sum is locally uniformly convergent by Schwartz decay for small $\ell$ and the bound $C/\ell$ for large $\ell$.

<a id="stmt-red-scale-truncation"></a>
<div id="red:scale-truncation" class="lemma">

<a id="red:scale-kernel-error"></a>
<a id="red:scale-first-moment"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *For dyadic $0<r\le R$, $$\label{red:scale-kernel-error}
 \int_\mathbb R\left|
    \frac{\mathbf 1_{\{r<|t|<R\}}}{t}
           -\sum_{r\le\ell\le R}k_\ell(t)
       \right|\,\mathrm dt\le C.$$ Also, $$\label{red:scale-first-moment}
 \int_\mathbb R|t|\left|\sum_{r\le\ell\le R}k_\ell(t)\right|
        \,\mathrm dt\le C R.$$ Both constants are independent of the number of scales. Changing either spatial endpoint by a fixed factor preserves the first bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-scale-truncation"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-scale-truncation</summary>

*Proof.* For $r<|t|<R$, the omitted scales below $r$ have sum bounded by $C r/|t|^2$, using $\left|k_\ell(t)\right|\le C\ell/|t|^2$ and a geometric sum. The omitted scales above $R$ have sum at most $C/R$. The integrals of these bounds over the middle interval are bounded. For $|t|<r$, the retained sum is at most $C\sum_{\ell\ge r}\ell^{-1}\le C/r$; for $|t|>R$, it is at most $C R/|t|^2$. These are integrable on their respective regions and prove <a href="#red:scale-kernel-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:scale-kernel-error">[red:scale-kernel-error]</a>. For the moment bound, use $$\int |t||k_\ell(t)|\,\mathrm dt
          =\ell\int |s||k(s)|\,\mathrm ds$$ and $\sum_{\ell\le R}\ell\le2R$. Finally, changing an endpoint by a fixed factor changes the spatial kernel only on an annulus having bounded $\,\mathrm dt/|t|$ measure. ◻

</details>

After [red:time-reduction](#red:time-reduction), the only possible remaining times are between $r=\max(w,\epsilon)$ and $\tau$. Round the lower endpoint to a comparable dyadic number; the resulting annular error has a uniform $L^p$ bound. If this lower endpoint exceeds $\tau$, there is no remaining model. Otherwise [red:scale-truncation](#red:scale-truncation) reduces the multiplier, with $u$ frozen at the output point, to <a id="eq:src-2"></a>$$\label{eq:src-2}
 \mathfrak a_{u,\mathcal L}(x,y;\xi,\eta)
    =\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}
           \psi\bigl(\ell(\xi+\eta u(x,y))\bigr),
 \qquad
 \mathcal L\subset\{\ell\in2^{\mathbb Z}:w\le\ell\le\tau\}.$$ Here $\mathcal L$ is the consecutive set of retained scales; when $\epsilon\le w$ it is the whole displayed range. Denote the associated operator by $\mathcal A_{u,\mathcal L}$, or simply $\mathcal A$. Its spatial representation is $$\mathcal A h(x,y)
   =\int_\mathbb RK_{\mathcal L}(t)
                  h(x-t,y-tu(x,y))\,\mathrm dt,
 \qquad K_{\mathcal L}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}k_\ell.$$

The kernels in this representation are not compactly supported in $t$.
