          \left(\sum_\xi|a_{i,\xi}|^2\right)^{r_*/2}
            \right)^{1/r_*}.
\end{aligned}$$ The first inequality uses $p\ge2$, and the last uses $r_*\le2$. Each has constant one and is independent of the Hilbert dimension. Only the frequency list within an individual component enters the scalar estimate, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="hier:section"></a>
### Angular hierarchy and predicted factors

We continue with the finite popular forest and the set $Z_0$ from [count:simultaneous](#count:simultaneous), on which the top estimates, the bound <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>, and the separated-top count hold. The parameters $p,G,K,B$ are defined in <a href="#forest:eq-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="forest:eq-parameters">[forest:eq-parameters]</a>, and $\alpha,\mu,r_*$ in <a href="#phase:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="phase:parameters">[phase:parameters]</a>. We use $i$ for individual angular levels and $j$ for blocks of levels. Each shift chooses different block endpoints. The analytic family constructed below is shared by all $K$ groupings. Its block identities explain how the phase estimate will compare a parent amplitude with its children, while its equal-coordinate value leaves the boundary weights removed in the final angular-grid average. We then prove the estimates that predict amplitudes at a finer angular level, up to a small error, from one good point on a much longer horizontal interval. No mass cutoff is used in those estimates.

<a id="hier:top-weight-basic"></a>
All sums below are finite. We retain the strictly positive spatial weights $h_T$ from <a href="#eq:src-5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-5">[eq:src-5]</a>; in particular, $$0<h_T\le C_3,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}h_T\,\,\mathrm dz\le L^C |R_T|.
 \label{hier:top-weight-basic}$$ The integral follows by the change of variables to the normalized coordinates of $R_T$, since the decay exponent in that definition is greater than two. Its constant includes the fixed polynomial dilation $K_0^2$. Constants denoted by $L^C$ in this section are independent of the number of tiles, tops, levels, and Hilbert-space components.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Eligible masses and shifted blocks

Choose a dyadic number $d(0)$ such that $B^2\le d(0)<2B^2$, enlarging it by a fixed absolute factor if necessary to cover the initial precision range. For a translation parameter $\vartheta\in[0,d(0))$, let <a id="hier:angular-grid"></a>$$d(i)=d(0)G^{-i},
 \qquad
 \mathcal G_i^\vartheta
 =\bigl\{[\vartheta+q d(i),\vartheta+(q+1)d(i)):q\in\mathbb Z\bigr\}.
 \label{hier:angular-grid}$$ Since $G$ is an integer dyadic power, these grids are nested. We fix $\vartheta$ for now. The top slopes lie in a bounded interval, so only an absolute number of intervals in $\mathcal G_0^\vartheta$ contain a top slope. These intervals are the roots. The half-open convention assigns each top $T$ a unique interval $v_i(T)$ at every level $i$.

For $v\in\mathcal G_i^\vartheta$ put <a id="hier:eligible-mass"></a>$$\mathcal T(v)
 =\{T:s_T\in v,\ d_T\le d(i)/B^2\},
 \qquad
 n_v(z)=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T(v)}h_T(z).
 \label{hier:eligible-mass}$$ We call these tops eligible at $v$. A node is nonempty if $\mathcal T(v)\ne\varnothing$. For such a node $n_v(z)>0$ at every spatial point. An empty node has mass and amplitude zero and is omitted from every expression involving a negative power or logarithm of its mass. Thus none of the logarithms below requires a spatial lower cutoff.

<a id="hier:mass-loss"></a>
If $v_1,\ldots,v_G$ are the immediate children of $b$, then their eligible top sets are disjoint subsets of $\mathcal T(b)$. More precisely, $$n_b-\sum_{v\text{ child of }b}n_v
 =\sum_{\substack{T\in\mathcal T(b)\\d_T>d(i+1)/B^2}}h_T\ge0,
 \qquad b\in\mathcal G_i^\vartheta.
 \label{hier:mass-loss}$$ The same assertion holds between any two levels, with the corresponding eligibility cutoff. In particular, every positive mass difference used below is itself a sum of the same positive functions $h_T$.

For a packet $s$ and a top $T$, define their last eligible levels by <a id="hier:stopping-levels"></a>$$k_s=\max\{i\ge0:d_s\le d(i)/B^2\},
 \qquad
 k_T=\max\{i\ge0:d_T\le d(i)/B^2\}.
 \label{hier:stopping-levels}$$ The initial precision choice makes these sets nonempty. The assigned-top relation $\ell_s\le l_{T(s)}$ gives $d_{T(s)}\le d_s$, and therefore $k_s\le k_{T(s)}$. Thus a packet is stopped only while its assigned top is still eligible. Since the collection is finite and all precisions are positive, the maximum of the $k_T$ is finite. The grids may be defined at every level, but no amplitude requires a level beyond this maximum. We also keep the first block endpoint past this maximum, so that terminal blocks have the same notation as other blocks.

<a id="eq:src-14"></a>
<a id="hier:boundary-mass-bounds"></a>
For each $\sigma\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ use the endpoints <a id="hier:shifted-blocks"></a>$$i_0^\sigma=0,
 \qquad i_j^\sigma=1+\sigma+(j-1)K\quad(j\ge1).
 \label{hier:shifted-blocks}$$ The first block has between one and $K$ transitions; all subsequent blocks have $K$. The transition $i\to i+1$ is the final transition of a block for precisely the shift $\sigma\equiv i\pmod K$. For this shift, let $a(i)$ be the starting level of that block. Given an immediate child $v\in\mathcal G_{i+1}^\vartheta$ with immediate parent $b\in\mathcal G_i^\vartheta$, denote its ancestor at level $a(i)$ by $J(i,v)$. Define $$m_v=n_b+
 \sum_{\substack{v'\in\mathcal G_{i+1}^\vartheta,
                    \ v'\subset J(i,v),\ v'\not\subset b\\
                    \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(v,v')<G^{-1/4}d(i)}}n_{v'}.
 \label{eq:src-14}$$ Here the distance is the distance between the angular intervals. The summands outside $b$ have disjoint top sets, and these sets are disjoint from $\mathcal T(b)$. They all consist of tops eligible at $J(i,v)$. Consequently, $$n_v\le n_b\le m_v\le n_{J(i,v)}.
 \label{hier:boundary-mass-bounds}$$ In particular, $m_v-n_v$ is a sum of positive top weights. The use of the larger block ancestor in <a href="#eq:src-14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-14">[eq:src-14]</a> never changes the radius of the neighbor test: that radius is always tied to the immediate transition scale $d(i)$. [fig:shifted-blocks](#fig:shifted-blocks) shows the shifted block endpoints and the top sets entering $m_v$.

<figure id="fig:shifted-blocks">

<figcaption>Shifted blocks and boundary masses, schematically. The upper diagram uses $K = 3$. Each shift starts with $1 + σ$ transitions and then uses blocks of $K$ transitions; colored arrows are the final transitions for that shift. At a final transition $i → i + 1$, the mass $m_{v}$ includes all of $n_{b}$ and the masses of neighboring children outside $b$ but inside $J$. The neighbor radius is determined by the immediate scale $d(i)$, even when $J$ begins much earlier. Neither diagram is to scale.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-shifted-blocks"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:shifted-blocks`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=.94cm,y=.74cm,>=Stealth,font=\small]
 \foreach \i in {0,...,9}
   \node[font=\scriptsize] at (\i,.62) {\(\i\)};
 \node[anchor=east,font=\scriptsize] at (-.55,.62) {level};
 \foreach \sig/\col in {0/blue!65!black,1/green!45!black,2/orange!80!black}{
   \pgfmathsetmacro{\yy}{-1.18*\sig}
   \node[anchor=east] at (-.5,\yy) {\(\sigma=\sig\)};
   \draw[gray!50] (0,\yy)--(9,\yy);
   \foreach \i in {0,...,8}{
     \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\rr}{mod(\i,3)}
     \ifnum\rr=\sig\relax
       \draw[->,very thick,\col] (\i+.1,\yy)--(\i+.89,\yy);
     \else
       \draw[->,gray!45] (\i+.14,\yy)--(\i+.86,\yy);
     \fi
   }
   \foreach \i in {0,...,9}
     \fill[gray!65] (\i,\yy) circle (1.35pt);
   \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\first}{1+\sig}
   \draw[\col,thick] (0,\yy+.13)--(0,\yy+.32)
     --(\first,\yy+.32)--(\first,\yy+.13);
   \foreach \b in {0,1}{
     \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\aa}{\first+3*\b}
     \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\bb}{\aa+3}
     \draw[\col,thick] (\aa,\yy+.13)--(\aa,\yy+.32)
       --(\bb,\yy+.32)--(\bb,\yy+.13);
   }
 }
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{tikzpicture}[x=.94cm,y=1cm,font=\small,>=Stealth]
 \draw[thick] (0,0)--(9,0);
 \draw[thick] (0,-.12)--(0,.12) (9,-.12)--(9,.12);
 \node[above] at (4.5,.13) {block ancestor \(J\)};
 \fill[blue!12] (2.3,-1.35) rectangle (6,-.7);
 \draw[blue!65!black,thick] (2.3,-1.35) rectangle (6,-.7);
 \node at (3.85,-1.025) {immediate parent \(b\)};
 \fill[blue!65!black] (5.48,-1.35) rectangle (5.86,-.7);
 \node[below] at (5.67,-1.4) {\(v\)};
 \foreach \aa in {6.06,6.48}{
   \fill[orange!35] (\aa,-1.35) rectangle (\aa+.34,-.7);
   \draw[orange!80!black] (\aa,-1.35) rectangle (\aa+.34,-.7);
 }
 \node[anchor=west] at (7.15,-1.02) {neighbors \(v'\)};
 \draw[thin] (6.84,-1.02)--(7.08,-1.02);
 \draw[dashed] (5.48,-1.7)--(5.48,-2.4);
 \draw[dashed] (6.95,-1.5)--(6.95,-2.4);
 \draw[<->] (5.48,-2.27)--(6.95,-2.27);

```

Visual witness description of PDF page 53 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
The upper diagram illustrates discrete levels numbered 0 through 9 along horizontal axes for three shifts $\sigma = 0$, $\sigma = 1$, and $\sigma = 2$. Each shift consists of sequences of transitions grouped into blocks of length $K=3$ (preceded by $1+\sigma$ initial transitions), with final transitions within each block marked by colored arrows (blue for $\sigma = 0$, green for $\sigma = 1$, orange for $\sigma = 2$) and gray arrows for non-final transitions. The lower diagram shows interval containment and boundary neighbors: a broad horizontal interval labeled "block ancestor $J$" contains an interval labeled "immediate parent $b$" (shaded in blue with vertical subdivisions), which contains an element $v$ indicated by an upward arrow. Adjacent to $b$ are interval segments labeled "neighbors $v'$" (shaded in orange with vertical subdivisions). A horizontal bidirectional arrow beneath indicates the "local neighbor radius $\sim G^{-1/4}d(i)$".
```


For $\zeta=(\zeta_0,\ldots,\zeta_{K-1})\in\mathbb C^K$ and nonempty $v$ at level $i+1$, put <a id="hier:analytic-transition"></a>$$w_v(\zeta,z)
 =\exp\!\left(\zeta_{i\bmod K}\log\frac{n_v(z)}{m_v(z)}\right).
 \label{hier:analytic-transition}$$ All logarithms in this formula are real logarithms of positive numbers. Thus the weights are entire functions of $\zeta$ and have modulus at most one when all $\Re\zeta_\sigma\ge0$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### The analytic family and its block comparisons

<a id="eq:src-15"></a>
For a nonempty node $v$ at level $h$, define the $\mathcal H$-valued analytic amplitude by $$A_v(\zeta,z)
 =\sum_{\substack{s:s_{T(s)}\in v\\ k_s\ge h}}
 q_{T(s)}(z)\phi_s(z)\,
 n_{v_{k_s}(T(s))}(z)^{-\mu/K}
 \prod_{i=h}^{k_s-1}w_{v_{i+1}(T(s))}(\zeta,z).
 \label{eq:src-15}$$ The condition $k_s\ge h$ already ensures eligibility of $T(s)$ at $v$. The empty product is one. This single definition is used for every shift. At fixed $z$, it is a finite sum of entire $\mathcal H$-valued functions of $\zeta$.

<a id="hier:vertex-parameters"></a>
<a id="hier:block-ratio"></a>
We first exhibit two algebraic uses of this common family. For the block comparison, temporarily fix a shift $\sigma$ and parameters satisfying $$\Re\zeta_\sigma=\mu,
 \qquad \Re\zeta_{\sigma'}=0\quad(\sigma'\ne\sigma),
 \qquad \left\|\Im\zeta\right\|_2\le L^2.
 \label{hier:vertex-parameters}$$ We refer to this choice of real parts as the vertex corresponding to $\sigma$. Write $i_j=i_j^\sigma$ and $\rho_j=d(i_j)$. Thus $$G\le\frac{\rho_j}{\rho_{j+1}}\le B,
 \label{hier:block-ratio}$$ including the first, possibly shorter, block.

<a id="eq:src-18"></a>
For a nonempty $J\in\mathcal G_{i_j}^\vartheta$, call its nonempty descendants at level $i_{j+1}$ its *block children*. For such a block child $c$, all packets entering $A_c$ have the same transition factor from $J$ to $c$. Denote it by $\lambda_c=\prod_{i=i_j}^{i_{j+1}-1}w_{v_{i+1}}$, along that descendant path. Only the last transition of this block has a nonzero real exponent. Separating packets according to whether their stopping level precedes $i_{j+1}$ therefore gives the exact identity $$A_J=\sum_{\substack{c\subset J\\c\in\mathcal G_{i_{j+1}}^\vartheta}}
                 \lambda_c A_c+B_J,
 \qquad
 |\lambda_c|=\left(\frac{n_c}{m_c}\right)^\mu.
 \label{eq:src-18}$$ The remainder $B_J$ is precisely the sum in <a href="#eq:src-15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-15">[eq:src-15]</a> over $i_j\le k_s<i_{j+1}$. Its assigned tops need not lose eligibility at the end of the block. Thus residual packets and lost top mass are different parts of the block decomposition.

<a id="eq:src-19"></a>
For each nonempty $J$, we add the mass to the normalized $p$-th power of the amplitude. The resulting positive amount $V_J$ and its multiplicative comparison factor $\beta_J$ are $$V_J=n_J+n_J^{\alpha-p}\left\|A_J\right\|_{\mathcal H}^p,
 \qquad
 \beta_J=
 \frac{V_J}{n_J+\sum_c n_c^{\alpha-p}\left\|A_c\right\|_{\mathcal H}^p}.
 \label{eq:src-19}$$ We use $\beta_J^0$ for the same ratio with $B_J$ omitted only in the numerator. The mass baseline makes $V_J$ and every denominator positive, including when all its block-child amplitudes vanish. The sum is over the nonempty descendants at the end of the block. Equivalently, the denominator is the lost eligibility mass $n_J-\sum_c n_c$ plus the block-child amounts $\sum_c V_c$. This is the comparison later used in the exact path expansion.

<a id="eq:src-25"></a>
At any one spatial point, abbreviate $n=n_J$ and normalize the child amplitudes by $$l_c=\frac{n_c}{n},\qquad
 X_c=n_c^{\mu-1}A_c,\qquad
 \beta_J^0=
  \frac{1+\left\|\sum_c l_c^{1-\mu}\lambda_c X_c\right\|_{\mathcal H}^p}
       {1+\sum_c l_c\left\|X_c\right\|_{\mathcal H}^p}.
 \label{eq:src-25}$$ The ratio follows by dividing <a href="#eq:src-19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-19">[eq:src-19]</a> by $n$ and using $p\mu=\alpha-1$. Also $\sum_c l_c\le1$. This is a pointwise identity: the masses, scalar factors, and vectors still vary with the spatial point. The vector sum in its numerator is the weighted child sum whose phase motion we will compare with the weighted child energies in the denominator.

<a id="pred:retention-probability"></a>
The boundary mass $m_c$ is designed to pay for selecting separated children in that comparison. At the final transition of this block put $i=i_{j+1}-1$ and <a id="hier:child-separation"></a>$$Q=G^{-1/4}d(i)=G^{3/4}\rho_{j+1}.
 \label{hier:child-separation}$$ Fix one spatial point and choose any finite subfamily $\mathcal C$ of nonempty block children. Join distinct $c,c'\in\mathcal C$ when $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(c,c')<Q$. Give the children independent exponential clocks of rates $n_c$, and retain a child when its clock rings before those of all its neighbors. The rates are positive. The clocks have no ties with probability one, and the retained intervals have pairwise distance at least $Q$. The retention probability of $c$ is $$\pi_c=\int_0^\infty n_c e^{-n_ct}
              \prod_{c'\sim c}e^{-n_{c'}t}\,\,\mathrm dt
       =\frac{n_c}{n_c+\sum_{c'\sim c}n_{c'}}
       \ge\frac{n_c}{m_c}.
 \label{pred:retention-probability}$$ To see the inequality, let $b$ be the immediate parent of $c$. The eligible top sets of $c$ and its competitors inside $b$ are disjoint subsets of $\mathcal T(b)$, so their masses sum to at most $n_b$. Every competitor outside $b$ lies inside $J$ and within $Q=G^{-1/4}d(i)$ of $c$, so its mass is included among the additional terms of $m_c$ in <a href="#eq:src-14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-14">[eq:src-14]</a>. Thus $m_c$ bounds the entire competing mass even when only a subfamily of children is used.

<a id="hier:selection-cancellation"></a>
Multiplying the coefficient of a retained child by $\pi_c^{-1}$ reconstructs its original coefficient in expectation. The corresponding expected $r_*$-power cost is $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{c\text{ retained}\}}\pi_c^{-1}\right]=1,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{c\text{ retained}\}}\pi_c^{-r_*}\right]
       =\pi_c^{1-r_*}.$$ The coefficient exponent in <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a> matches this cost exactly. The identities $r_*(1-\mu)=1$ and $\mu r_*=r_*-1$ give $$\pi_c^{1-r_*}|\lambda_c|^{r_*}
   =\left(\frac{n_c/m_c}{\pi_c}\right)^{r_*-1}\le1.
 \label{hier:selection-cancellation}$$ The first identity also turns $l_c^{r_*(1-\mu)}$ into $l_c$, the weight in the denominator of <a href="#eq:src-25" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-25">[eq:src-25]</a>. The later snapshot construction will use this selection for the child vectors it retains and check the remaining hypotheses of the finite Fourier estimate.

<a id="eq:src-30"></a>
The same analytic family has a second algebraic use at the equal-coordinate parameter $$\zeta^{\mathrm c}=(\mu/K,\ldots,\mu/K).$$ Let $v$ be a root and let $s$ be a packet contributing to $A_v$, with $T=T(s)$. Apart from the factor $q_T\phi_s$, the coefficient of $s$ in $n_v^{\mu/K}A_v(\zeta^{\mathrm c},z)$ is $$n_v^{\mu/K}n_{v_{k_s}(T)}^{-\mu/K}
       \prod_{i=0}^{k_s-1}
        \left(\frac{n_{v_{i+1}(T)}}{m_{v_{i+1}(T)}}\right)^{\mu/K}.$$ Cancelling consecutive eligible masses identifies it as $$\label{eq:src-30}
 \kappa_s
   =\prod_{i=0}^{k_s-1}
       \left(\frac{n_{v_i(T)}}{m_{v_{i+1}(T)}}\right)^{\mu/K},
 \qquad T=T(s).$$ The empty product is one, and each factor lies in $(0,1]$ by <a href="#eq:src-14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-14">[eq:src-14]</a>. Thus for each root the equal-coordinate value produces its portion of the masked packet sum with only these boundary ratios in its coefficients. The remaining tasks are to estimate the vertex values, interpolate those estimates to $\zeta^{\mathrm c}$, and remove the boundary ratios by averaging the angular-grid translation.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Full-root variation and amplitude bounds

<a id="hier:positive-logs"></a>
We now leave the temporary vertex specialization and return to the general range $0\le\Re\zeta_\sigma\le\mu$ for every coordinate, with arbitrary imaginary parts. The following bounds control the endpoint weights without a loss proportional to the number of levels. Along one eligible top path, write $n_i=n_{v_i(T)}$ and $$P_i=\log\frac{n_i}{n_{i+1}},
 \qquad
 R_i=\log\frac{m_{v_{i+1}(T)}}{n_{i+1}}.
 \label{hier:positive-logs}$$ Both are nonnegative as long as the top remains eligible at level $i+1$.

<a id="stmt-hier-weight-variation"></a>
<div id="hier:weight-variation" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:full-root-telescope"></a>
<a id="eq:src-16"></a>
**Lemma 34** (Full-root variation bound). *Fix an eligible path from a root $v_0(T)$ through level $k$. At every spatial point, $$\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}R_i
 \le K\log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}}{n_{v_k(T)}},
 \qquad
 \sum_{i=0}^{k-1}P_i
 =\log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}}{n_{v_k(T)}}.
 \label{hier:full-root-telescope}$$ For a fixed starting level $h\le k_T$, define, for $h\le k\le k_T$, $$\omega_{T,h,k}(\zeta,z)
 =n_{v_k(T)}(z)^{-\mu/K}
   \prod_{i=h}^{k-1}w_{v_{i+1}(T)}(\zeta,z).$$ If $0\le\Re\zeta_\sigma\le\mu$ for every $\sigma$, then the supremum plus total variation of this sequence, with the endpoint $k$ varying, is bounded by $$\sup_{h\le k\le k_T}|\omega_{T,h,k}|
 +\sum_{k=h}^{k_T-1}
       |\omega_{T,h,k+1}-\omega_{T,h,k}|
 \le C h_T^{-\mu/K}
 \left[1+K(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)
                   \log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}}{h_T}\right].
 \label{eq:src-16}$$ The same bound holds upon restricting the endpoints to a subinterval.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-weight-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-weight-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a shift $\sigma$. For the transitions $i\equiv\sigma\pmod K$ with $i<k$, <a href="#hier:boundary-mass-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:boundary-mass-bounds">[hier:boundary-mass-bounds]</a> gives $$R_i\le\log\frac{n_{a(i)}}{n_{i+1}}.$$ For this fixed shift, the intervals of levels $[a(i),i+1]$ are successive blocks starting at zero. Their logarithms telescope. The endpoint of the last completed block is at most $k$, so the sum is at most $\log(n_0/n_k)$, by monotonicity of the masses. Summing over the $K$ shifts proves the first inequality in <a href="#hier:full-root-telescope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:full-root-telescope">[hier:full-root-telescope]</a>. The second is an ordinary telescope. This is a full-root bound. In particular, it remains applicable to weights occurring on a later segment even when another shift’s block starts before that segment.

<a id="hier:log-weight-increment"></a>
Set $a=\mu/K$, solely for this proof, and use the specified logarithm $$L_k=-a\log n_k-\sum_{i=h}^{k-1}\zeta_{i\bmod K}R_i,
 \qquad \omega_{T,h,k}=e^{L_k}.$$ Its increment is $$L_{k+1}-L_k=aP_k-\zeta_{k\bmod K}R_k.
 \label{hier:log-weight-increment}$$ Since $n_k\ge h_T$ and all real transition exponents are nonnegative, $|e^{L_k}|\le h_T^{-a}$. For any complex $A,B$, $$|e^B-e^A|
 \le |B-A|\int_0^1|e^{(1-t)A+tB}|\,\,\mathrm dt
 \le |B-A|\max(|e^A|,|e^B|).$$ Using this inequality for adjacent logarithms and then <a href="#hier:full-root-telescope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:full-root-telescope">[hier:full-root-telescope]</a> proves <a href="#eq:src-16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-16">[eq:src-16]</a>. Notice that the exponential of the sum of the absolute logarithmic increments has not been used. Such an exponential would be unsuitable when $h_T$ is very small. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-hier-amplitudes"></a>
<div id="hier:amplitudes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src-17"></a>
<a id="hier:individual-amplitude"></a>
<a id="hier:insignificant-amplitude"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Amplitude bounds). *On $Z_0$, for $0\le\Re\zeta_\sigma\le\mu$, $$\left\|A_v(\zeta,z)\right\|_{\mathcal H}
 \le L^C(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)n_v(z).
 \label{eq:src-17}$$ More precisely, the contribution of any one eligible top $T$ to $A_v$, also when restricted to any interval of packet lengths, is bounded by $$L^C(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)|q_T(z)|h_T(z)^4.
 \label{hier:individual-amplitude}$$ If all eligible tops of $v$ are insignificant at $z\in Z_0$, then $$\left\|A_v(\zeta,z)\right\|_{\mathcal H}
 \le L^C(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)e^{-L\log L}n_v(z).
 \label{hier:insignificant-amplitude}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-amplitudes"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-amplitudes</summary>

<a id="hier:summation-by-parts"></a>
*Proof.* For vectors $f_h,\ldots,f_k$ and scalars $\omega_h,\ldots,\omega_k$, summation by parts gives $$\left\|\sum_{i=h}^k\omega_i f_i\right\|
 \le
 \left(|\omega_k|+\sum_{i=h}^{k-1}|\omega_{i+1}-\omega_i|\right)
 \max_{h\le j\le k}\left\|\sum_{i=h}^j f_i\right\|.
 \label{hier:summation-by-parts}$$ Group the packets assigned to $T$ according to their stopping level. The resulting initial sums, or the sums obtained after restricting to an interval of packet lengths, are interval partial sums of the original top’s length sequence. By <a href="#eq:src-11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-11">[eq:src-11]</a> and the fact that the $K_0$-dilated weight is at least the corresponding $K_1$-dilated weight, their norm at $z\in Z_0$ is at most $L^C h_T(z)^8$. The mask $q_T(z)$ is common to this whole top. Apply <a href="#hier:summation-by-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:summation-by-parts">[hier:summation-by-parts]</a> and <a href="#eq:src-16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-16">[eq:src-16]</a>.

To see that the logarithmic factor can be absorbed uniformly, use <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a> and $\log N\le2L$. Since $n_{v_0(T)}\le n$ and $h_T\le C_3$, $$0\le\log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}}{h_T}
 \le C L+\log^+\frac1{h_T}.$$ Here and below $\log^+x=\max(0,\log x)$. Also $\mu/K<1$. The elementary boundedness of $x^\delta\log^q(1/x)$ on $(0,1]$, for fixed $\delta>0$ and fixed $q$, shows that $$h_T^{8-\mu/K}
 \left[1+K(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)
                    \log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}}{h_T}\right]
 \le L^C(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)h_T^4.$$ This proves <a href="#hier:individual-amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:individual-amplitude">[hier:individual-amplitude]</a>. Summing over the eligible tops and using $h_T^4\le C_3^3h_T$ proves <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a>. For insignificant tops, the mask estimate after <a href="#eq:src-8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-8">[eq:src-8]</a> gives $|q_T|\le e^{-L\log L}$. Applying the same summation proves <a href="#hier:insignificant-amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:insignificant-amplitude">[hier:insignificant-amplitude]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Spatial meshes for prediction

Return to a fixed shift $\sigma$ and a vertex parameter satisfying <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a>. Write $i_j=i_j^\sigma$ and $\rho_j=d(i_j)$ as in the block comparison above, and take $J\in\mathcal G_{i_j}^\vartheta$. Its block children and their factors are those in <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a>. Let $R$ be any top eligible in $J$, and parametrize a line of slope $s_R$ by $z(x)=(x,y_*+s_Rx)$. On this line use one nested dyadic grid in the parameter $x$, at lengths <a id="eq:src-20"></a>$$H_j=\frac{w}{\rho_{j+1}\sqrt G},
 \qquad H_{-1}=\frac{w}{\rho_0\sqrt G}.
 \label{eq:src-20}$$ These are dyadic lengths because $w,d(0),G$, and $\sqrt G$ are dyadic. They increase with $j$. The grids can, for example, have common origin zero. Each $H_j$ cell is a union of $H_{j-1}$ cells.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### Positive-mass motion and the locality of other shifts

<a id="stmt-hier-mass-motion"></a>
<div id="hier:mass-motion" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:motion-locality"></a>
<a id="hier:mass-log-derivative"></a>
<a id="hier:ratio-derivative"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Mass motion). *Fix $R,J,j$ as above and an interval $I$ of horizontal length $H_j$ on a line of slope $s_R$. Consider the masses $n_J,n_c,m_c$ in <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a>, the masses used in the factors $\lambda_c$, and those used in any endpoint multiplier of a packet contributing to $A_c$. Each such mass, and each of its positive subset-complement differences used below, is a sum of weights of tops $T$ satisfying $$|s_T-s_R|\le C\rho_j,
 \qquad d_T\le\rho_j/B^2.
 \label{hier:motion-locality}$$ There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, with $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}=C G^{-1/2}$, every nonzero such mass $a$ satisfies $$|\partial_x\log a|\le\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}
 \quad\text{almost everywhere on }I.
 \label{hier:mass-log-derivative}$$ If $r=a/(a+b)$ is one of the ratios of a submass to its containing mass, then $$|\partial_x\log r|
 \le\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}(1-r)
 \le\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}(-\log r).
 \label{hier:ratio-derivative}$$ When $b=0$, the ratio is identically one and its derivative is zero. In particular, nonzero $n_J-n_c$ and $m_c-n_c$ have the relative motion bound <a href="#hier:mass-log-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:mass-log-derivative">[hier:mass-log-derivative]</a>. All these masses vary by a factor $1+O(G^{-1/2})$ across $I$.*

<a id="hier:complex-transition-motion"></a>
*For the parameters <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a>, the block factors also satisfy, for $x,x_0\in I$, $$\left|\frac{\lambda_c(x)}{\lambda_c(x_0)}-1\right|
 \le G^{-c_0}
 \label{hier:complex-transition-motion}$$ for some absolute $c_0>0$ and all sufficiently large $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-mass-motion"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-mass-motion</summary>

*Proof.* All descendants of $J$ have slopes inside $J$, so their top slopes are within $\rho_j$ of $s_R$. Their eligibility cutoffs are at most $\rho_j/B^2$. A mass in an additional boundary term <a href="#eq:src-14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-14">[eq:src-14]</a> at a transition $i\ge i_j$ can come from an interval outside $J$, because the block associated to another shift can start before $i_j$. However, that interval is within $G^{-1/4}d(i)$ of the relevant child and has width $d(i+1)$. The relevant child is a descendant of $J$, and $d(i)\le\rho_j$. Hence every added slope is within $C\rho_j$ of $s_R$. The tops in an added interval have precision at most $d(i+1)/B^2$; those in the immediate-parent mass have precision at most $d(i)/B^2$. This proves <a href="#hier:motion-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:motion-locality">[hier:motion-locality]</a>, including for weights below the block. Taking a difference of the relevant nested top sets preserves the same property. This argument uses the neighbor radius at the immediate transition, rather than the possibly larger width of the other shift’s block ancestor.

In normalized coordinates of a top $T$, horizontal motion along $s_R$ has velocities $1/l_T$ and $(s_R-s_T)/W$. Differentiating <a href="#eq:src-5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-5">[eq:src-5]</a>, using the Lipschitz property of the absolute value at almost every point, gives $$|\partial_x\log h_T|
 \le C\left(\frac1{l_T}+\frac{|s_R-s_T|}{W}\right)
 \le C\left(\frac{\rho_j}{B^2w}+\frac{\rho_j}{mw}\right).$$ The factor $K_0^{-1}$ available from the dilation is not needed. Multiplying by $H_j$ and using <a href="#hier:block-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:block-ratio">[hier:block-ratio]</a> yields $$H_j|\partial_x\log h_T|
 \le\frac C{\sqrt G}
       \left(\frac{B}{B^2}+\frac Bm\right)
 \le C G^{-1/2}.$$ For a positive sum $a=\sum_T h_T$, its logarithmic derivative is the weighted average of the logarithmic derivatives of the summands. This proves <a href="#hier:mass-log-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:mass-log-derivative">[hier:mass-log-derivative]</a>, also for positive differences, which are such sums by their set interpretation.

<a id="hier:loss-motion"></a>
If $a,b>0$, direct differentiation gives $$\partial_x\log\frac a{a+b}
 =\frac b{a+b}\bigl(\partial_x\log a-\partial_x\log b\bigr).$$ This proves <a href="#hier:ratio-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:ratio-derivative">[hier:ratio-derivative]</a>; the inequality $1-r\le-\log r$ holds for $0<r\le1$. If the complementary top set is empty, $r=1$ everywhere. In particular, writing $q_r=-\log r\ge0$, $$|\partial_x q_r|\le\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}q_r,
 \qquad
 e^{-2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}q_r(x_0)
 \le q_r(x)\le e^{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}q_r(x_0)
 \quad(x,x_0\in I).
 \label{hier:loss-motion}$$ The inequalities remain valid for an identically zero loss.

Finally, a block factor is a product of at most $K$ transition factors. The simpler bound $|\partial_x\log r|\le2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}/H_j$ shows that its specified complex logarithm changes by at most $C K(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)G^{-1/2}$. For <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a> this is $L^C G^{-1/2}$ and tends to zero faster than any negative fixed power of $L$. Exponentiating proves <a href="#hier:complex-transition-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:complex-transition-motion">[hier:complex-transition-motion]</a> after decreasing $c_0$. This last argument does not require any bound on the size of a logarithmic loss itself. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Variation of moving endpoint weights

The next estimate makes explicit why the motion of the analytic weights can be controlled from a single point, even for very small $h_T$. For a finite scalar sequence $a=(a_k)$ let $$\left\|a\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 =\sup_k|a_k|+\sum_k|a_{k+1}-a_k|.$$ Only this elementary finite-sequence norm is used here.

<a id="stmt-hier-moving-weights"></a>
<div id="hier:moving-weights" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:moving-weight-bv"></a>
<a id="hier:moving-weight-difference"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Moving weights). *In the setting of [hier:mass-motion](#hier:mass-motion), fix a top $T$ contributing to $A_c$, and take endpoint weights $\omega_k=\omega_{T,i_{j+1},k}$. If $z_0\in I\cap Z_0$ and the parameters satisfy <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a>, then, uniformly for $z'\in I$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|(\omega_k(z'))_k\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 &\le L^C h_T(z_0)^{-4},
 \label{hier:moving-weight-bv}\\
 \left\|(\omega_k(z')-\omega_k(z_0))_k\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 &\le G^{-1/2}L^C h_T(z_0)^{-4}.
 \label{hier:moving-weight-difference}
\end{aligned}$$ Both conclusions hold for any interval of endpoints. Neither assumes that $z'$ belongs to $Z_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-moving-weights"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-moving-weights</summary>

<a id="hier:moving-loss-budget"></a>
*Proof.* Parametrize the interval by its horizontal coordinate $x$, and write $h=i_{j+1}$ and $a=\mu/K$ in this proof. Use $P_i,R_i$ from <a href="#hier:positive-logs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:positive-logs">[hier:positive-logs]</a> for $h\le i<k_T$. By <a href="#hier:loss-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:loss-motion">[hier:loss-motion]</a>, each of these nonnegative losses is comparable throughout $I$ to its value at $x_0$, with factor $e^{O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}})}$, and its absolute derivative is bounded by $C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}/H_j$ times itself. Define $$\mathcal B(x)=\sum_{i=h}^{k_T-1}
                    \bigl(aP_i(x)+|\zeta_{i\bmod K}|R_i(x)\bigr).$$ It follows that $\mathcal B(x)\le e^{C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}\mathcal B(x_0)$. By the full-root bound, $$\mathcal B(x_0)
 \le C K(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|_2)
                 \log\frac{n_{v_0(T)}(z_0)}{h_T(z_0)}.
 \label{hier:moving-loss-budget}$$ The full-root mass is used only at the snapshot in this inequality; its spatial derivative is not needed.

Let $L_k=\log\omega_k$ be the specified logarithm in the proof of [hier:weight-variation](#hier:weight-variation), and put $q_k=\partial_x L_k$. Equation <a href="#hier:log-weight-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:log-weight-increment">[hier:log-weight-increment]</a> and the mass derivative bound imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \sup_k|q_k|
 &\le\frac{C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}
                  \bigl(a+\mathcal B(x)\bigr),\\
 \sum_k|q_{k+1}-q_k|
 &\le\frac{C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}\mathcal B(x).
\end{aligned}$$ For example, the initial term is $q_h=-a\partial_x\log n_h$, and subsequent differences are the derivatives of $aP_i-\zeta_{i\bmod K}R_i$. At every point of $I$, $$\sup_k|\omega_k(x)|\le h_T(x)^{-a}
 \le e^{a\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}h_T(z_0)^{-a}.$$ The complex-exponential estimate used for <a href="#eq:src-16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-16">[eq:src-16]</a> therefore gives $$\left\|\omega(x)\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 \le C h_T(z_0)^{-a}\bigl(1+\mathcal B(x_0)\bigr).$$

<a id="hier:weight-derivative-bv"></a>
For finite sequences the product rule for differences gives $$\left\|ab\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 \le \sup_k|a_k|\left\|b\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
       +\sup_k|b_k|\sum_k|a_{k+1}-a_k|.$$ Using $\partial_x\omega_k=\omega_k q_k$ and the preceding estimates yields $$\left\|\partial_x\omega(x)\right\|_{\mathrm{BV}}
 \le\frac{C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}}}{H_j}
 h_T(z_0)^{-a}\bigl(1+\mathcal B(x_0)\bigr)^2.
 \label{hier:weight-derivative-bv}$$ The functions involved are absolutely continuous on the line. Integrate <a href="#hier:weight-derivative-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:weight-derivative-bv">[hier:weight-derivative-bv]</a> between $x_0$ and $x'$, over distance at most $H_j$, and use the triangle inequality for the finite-sequence norm. This proves the difference bound with right side $C\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mov}} h_T(z_0)^{-a}(1+\mathcal B(x_0))^2$.

At the snapshot, <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>, <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a>, and <a href="#hier:moving-loss-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:moving-loss-budget">[hier:moving-loss-budget]</a> bound the last expression by $G^{-1/2}L^C h_T(z_0)^{-4}$. Indeed the only possible nonpolynomial quantity is $\log^+(1/h_T(z_0))$, whose square is absorbed by the difference between exponents $a<1$ and $4$. The same argument proves <a href="#hier:moving-weight-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:moving-weight-bv">[hier:moving-weight-bv]</a>. There is no assumption that $G^{-1/2}\mathcal B(x_0)$ is small: very small $h_T$ is allowed, and only a polynomial in its logarithm has appeared. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Amplitude motion from one good snapshot

<a id="stmt-hier-amplitude-motion"></a>
<div id="hier:amplitude-motion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src-21"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Predictable amplitude motion). *Fix a block child $c$ of $J$, a path top $R$ eligible in $J$, and an $H_j$ cell on a line of slope $s_R$. Let $t_c$ be the angular center of $c$. If $z_0\in Z_0$ lies in that cell and $z'=z_0+(b,s_Rb)$ is any other point in the cell, then, for the parameters <a href="#hier:vertex-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:vertex-parameters">[hier:vertex-parameters]</a>, $$\left\|A_c(\zeta,z')-\mathcal D_c(b)A_c(\zeta,z_0)\right\|_{\mathcal H}
 \le G^{-c_0}n_c(z_0),
 \qquad
 \mathcal D_c(b)_{\nu\nu}
 =\exp\bigl(i(s_R-t_c)\eta_\nu b\bigr).
 \label{eq:src-21}$$ Here $c_0>0$ is absolute and may also be used, after decreasing it, in <a href="#hier:complex-transition-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:complex-transition-motion">[hier:complex-transition-motion]</a>. The estimates hold at all displaced points in the cell, not just its points in $Z_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-amplitude-motion"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-amplitude-motion</summary>

<a id="hier:predictable-length"></a>
*Proof.* Fix one top $T$ contributing to $A_c$. Its contributing packets have $k_s\ge i_{j+1}$, hence $$\ell_s\ge l_*:=\frac{B^2w}{\rho_{j+1}},
 \qquad l_T\ge l_*.
 \label{hier:predictable-length}$$ Also $s_T\in c$, so $|s_T-t_c|\le\rho_{j+1}/2$, while $|s_T-s_R|\le C\rho_j$.

Consider any interval partial sum $S$ of these unweighted packets on $T$. The good-snapshot estimates <a href="#eq:src-11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-11">[eq:src-11]</a>, including their demodulated vertical-derivative and vertical-neighborhood versions, bound $S$ and $W(\partial_y-i\eta_\nu)S_\nu$ by $L^Ch_T(z_0)^8$ throughout the vertical neighborhood of radius $W$ at $z_0$. Put $v=(s_R-s_T)b$ and first move to $z_1=z_0+(0,v)$. Since $$|v|\le C\rho_jH_j\le\frac{CBw}{\sqrt G}\ll W,$$ the fundamental theorem of calculus after componentwise demodulation gives $$\left\|S(z_1)-\operatorname{diag}(e^{i\eta_\nu v})S(z_0)\right\|
 \le L^Ch_T(z_0)^8\frac{|v|}{W}
 \le G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^8.$$ Next move from $z_1$ to $z'$ along slope $s_T$ by horizontal distance $b$. The nonexceptional horizontal derivative estimate in [conc:top-good](#conc:top-good), applied to lengths at least $l_*$, bounds this change by $$L^Ch_T(z_0)^8\frac{|b|}{l_*}
 \le G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^8.$$ To justify its spatial weight here, the two displacements change the normalized coordinates of $T$ by at most $C/(B^2\sqrt G)+CB/(m\sqrt G)$. Its weight at every intermediate point is therefore comparable to $h_T(z_0)$. If the derivative estimate is expressed using the smaller dilation $K_1$, that weight is bounded above by the $K_0$-dilated weight used here. No good-point estimate at $z_1$ or $z'$ has been invoked for this horizontal move.

<a id="hier:unweighted-motion"></a>
The replacement of $s_T$ by $t_c$ in the diagonal phase costs at most $$C|s_T-t_c|\frac{|b|}{w}\left\|S(z_0)\right\|
 \le G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^8,$$ because $\eta_\nu\asymp w^{-1}$ uniformly in $\nu$. This proves, uniformly over the interval partial sum, $$\left\|S(z')-\mathcal D_c(b)S(z_0)\right\|
 \le G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^8.
 \label{hier:unweighted-motion}$$

The same bound holds with the mask $q_T$ multiplying $S$ at its own evaluation point. Indeed $|q_T|\le1$, and its formula <a href="#eq:src-8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-8">[eq:src-8]</a>, together with boundedness of the derivative of sinc, gives $$|q_T(z')-q_T(z_0)|
 \le L^C\left(\frac{|b|}{l_T}
                    +\frac{|s_R-s_T|\,|b|}{W}\right)
 \le G^{-1/2}L^C.$$ Combining this with the snapshot bound for $S$ proves the masked version of <a href="#hier:unweighted-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:unweighted-motion">[hier:unweighted-motion]</a>. This estimate uses absolute motion of the mask and does not divide by $q_T$, which may vanish.

Group the masked packets by stopping level and denote their sums by $f_k(z)$. Their weighted contribution to the difference in <a href="#eq:src-21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-21">[eq:src-21]</a> is $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_k\omega_k(z')
       \bigl(f_k(z')-\mathcal D_c(b)f_k(z_0)\bigr)\\
 &\hspace{15mm}
 +\sum_k\bigl(\omega_k(z')-\omega_k(z_0)\bigr)
                        \mathcal D_c(b)f_k(z_0).
\end{aligned}$$ All interval partial sums of the first parenthesis have norm at most $G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^8$, by the masked version of <a href="#hier:unweighted-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:unweighted-motion">[hier:unweighted-motion]</a>. The snapshot partial sums in the second line have norm at most $L^Ch_T(z_0)^8$. Apply <a href="#hier:summation-by-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:summation-by-parts">[hier:summation-by-parts]</a> to both lines, then <a href="#hier:moving-weight-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:moving-weight-bv">[hier:moving-weight-bv]</a> and <a href="#hier:moving-weight-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:moving-weight-difference">[hier:moving-weight-difference]</a>. The result for this top is at most $G^{-1/2}L^Ch_T(z_0)^4$. Sum over the tops eligible in $c$. Since $h_T^4\le C_3^3h_T$, this is at most $G^{-1/2}L^Cn_c(z_0)$. Finally $\log G\asymp L^{0.7}$ absorbs every fixed power of $L$ into a smaller positive power of $G$, proving <a href="#eq:src-21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-21">[eq:src-21]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### One deterministic residual sequence for each path

We now refine $Z_0$ by a set which depends on the angular translation but not on $\zeta$. Its purpose is to make the later residual estimates available on most of the path weight. The subsets of packets used in its definition must be fixed before evaluating a spatial point or choosing a mass cutoff.

<a id="hier:reference-packets"></a>
For each possible path top $R$, let $$\mathcal P_R
 =\{s:\ell_s\le l_R,\quad
                    |\theta_s-s_R|\le C B^4d_s\},
 \label{hier:reference-packets}$$ where the absolute constant is large enough to cover the assigned-top comparisons below. Apply the main/error decomposition of [conc:line-moments](#conc:line-moments) with reference slope $s_R$ to this fixed collection. Denote the resulting positive error sum by $\mathcal E_R$ and positive main square function by $\mathcal S_R$. The absolute envelopes can use fixed decay exponents slow enough to dominate the norms of the unweighted synthesis packets. There is no loss in doing so because the packet bounds are available with arbitrarily large fixed decay exponents.

<a id="hier:deterministic-residual-subset"></a>
For each shift $\sigma$, define a second fixed collection $$\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}
 =\bigcup_{\substack{j\ge0\\ i_{j+1}^\sigma\le k_R}}
 \left\{s:
 \begin{array}{l}
 i_j^\sigma\le k_s<i_{j+1}^\sigma,\\
 T(s)\in\mathcal T\bigl(v_{i_{j+1}^\sigma}(R)\bigr)
 \end{array}\right\}.
 \label{hier:deterministic-residual-subset}$$ For blocks at whose endpoint $R$ is still eligible, these are the packets that stop within the block while their assigned tops remain eligible in the child $v_{i_{j+1}^\sigma}(R)$. The union takes all such blocks at once, fixing one packet collection before any spatial point or mass cutoff is chosen. Let $\mathcal V_{R,\sigma}$ be the $3$-variation of the sequence formed by summing $q_{T(s)}\phi_s$ from this collection at each packet length. It permits any finite list of disjoint intervals of that one length sequence. Subsequent choices of those intervals may depend on the evaluation point; the collection and its length sequence remain fixed.

Every packet in $\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}$ satisfies the hypotheses of [conc:line-moments](#conc:line-moments) with $t=s_R$ and $l=l_R$. Indeed, $k_s<i_{j+1}^\sigma\le k_R$ gives <a id="hier:residual-precision"></a>$$d_s>\frac{\rho_{j+1}}{B^2}\ge d_R,
 \qquad \ell_s<l_R.
 \label{hier:residual-precision}$$ Since $s_{T(s)}$ and $s_R$ are in the same block-child interval of width $\rho_{j+1}$, $$|\theta_s-s_R|
 \le |\theta_s-s_{T(s)}|+|s_{T(s)}-s_R|
 \le C d_s+\rho_{j+1}\le C B^2d_s\le C B^4d_s.$$ For later use, if a residual packet only lies in the larger path node $J=v_{i_j}(R)$ and has $\ell_s\le l_R$, then $|s_{T(s)}-s_R|\le\rho_j\le B\rho_{j+1}<B^3d_s$; thus it belongs to $\mathcal P_R$ as well. These facts explain both choices of fixed collections.

<a id="stmt-hier-good-paths"></a>
<div id="hier:good-paths" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:weighted-bad-paths"></a>
<a id="hier:good-set"></a>
**Lemma 39** (Good paths). *There is a fixed exponent $C_{\mathrm{path}}$, chosen independently of the mass cutoff below, such that the following construction is valid. Call $R$ bad at $z$ if any of $\mathcal E_R(z),\mathcal S_R(z),\mathcal V_{R,\sigma}(z)$, $0\le\sigma<K$, exceeds $L^{C_{\mathrm{path}}}$. Otherwise call it good at $z$. For every fixed angular translation, $$\sum_R\int_{\mathbb R^2}h_R(z)\mathbf 1_{\{R\text{ bad at }z\}}\,\,\mathrm dz
 \le S e^{-10L}.
 \label{hier:weighted-bad-paths}$$ The constant $10$ may be replaced by any prescribed absolute constant by increasing the fixed exponent. The measurable set $$Z=Z_\vartheta
 =\left\{z\in Z_0:
       \sum_{R\text{ bad at }z}h_R(z)\le e^{-L}\right\}
 \label{hier:good-set}$$ is independent of $\zeta$ and satisfies <a id="hier:good-set-budget"></a>$$|Z_0\setminus Z|\le S e^{-9L}
 \le L^Ce^{-5L}|F|.
 \label{hier:good-set-budget}$$ Its removal is therefore allowable in the target estimate <a href="#eq:src-7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-7">[eq:src-7]</a>, using the original adjoint sum’s $L^2$ bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-good-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-good-paths</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $R$, a line of slope $s_R$, and any horizontal interval $I$ of length $l_R$ on that line. By [conc:line-moments](#conc:line-moments), the normalized $L^b(I)$ norm of each indicated quantity is at most $C b^C(1+\log B)^C$. The same bound holds for $\mathcal V_{R,\sigma}$ because <a href="#hier:deterministic-residual-subset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:deterministic-residual-subset">[hier:deterministic-residual-subset]</a> is a fixed subset satisfying <a href="#eq:src-9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-9">[eq:src-9]</a>. As $\log B=K\log G$ is bounded by a fixed power of $L$, this bound is polynomial in $L$ when $b$ is a fixed positive multiple of $L$.

For clarity, fix an arbitrarily large absolute number $J_0$. Choose $b=\lceil L\rceil$ and then a fixed exponent $C_{\mathrm{path}}$ sufficiently larger than those in the moment bound. Markov’s inequality gives, for all large $L$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &|\{x\in I:\mathcal E_R(x)>L^{C_{\mathrm{path}}}\}|
  +|\{x\in I:\mathcal S_R(x)>L^{C_{\mathrm{path}}}\}|\\
 &\qquad
  +\sum_{\sigma=0}^{K-1}
   |\{x\in I:\mathcal V_{R,\sigma}(x)>L^{C_{\mathrm{path}}}\}|
  \le e^{-J_0L}|I|.
 \end{aligned}$$ The factor $K+2$ is polynomial and is absorbed by the same choice. Alternatively one may increase the fixed multiple in the choice of $b$; neither choice depends on the number of scales or tops.

Partition the whole reference line into intervals of length $l_R$. Along this line $h_R$ changes by at most an absolute factor on each such interval, directly from <a href="#eq:src-5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-5">[eq:src-5]</a>. Hence the preceding estimate implies $$\int_I h_R\mathbf 1_{\{R\text{ bad}\}}\,\,\mathrm dx
 \le C e^{-J_0L}\int_I h_R\,\,\mathrm dx.$$ Sum over the intervals and integrate in the transverse coordinate $y-s_Rx$; this change of coordinates has determinant one. Using <a href="#hier:top-weight-basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:top-weight-basic">[hier:top-weight-basic]</a> gives $$\int_{\mathbb R^2}h_R\mathbf 1_{\{R\text{ bad}\}}\,\,\mathrm dz
 \le L^C e^{-J_0L}|R_R|.$$ Sum over $R$ and take $J_0$ large enough to obtain <a href="#hier:weighted-bad-paths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:weighted-bad-paths">[hier:weighted-bad-paths]</a>. This establishes the estimate with its full spatial weight, not only on the central part of $R$.

Markov’s inequality applied to the nonnegative sum defining <a href="#hier:good-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:good-set">[hier:good-set]</a> gives its first measure bound. Since $S\le L^C|E|=L^CN^2|F|$ and $N\le e^{2L}$, the second follows. For example, Cauchy–Schwarz and the previously proved adjoint estimate give $$\int_{Z_0\setminus Z}\left\|P\right\|
 \le |Z_0\setminus Z|^{1/2}\left\|P\right\|_2
 \le L^Ce^{-5L/2}N|F|,$$ which is smaller than any required fixed negative power of $L$ times $N|F|$. The estimate is applied to the original sum $P$, as required for exceptional discards.

Finally, all packet collections used here depend only on the finite geometric data, the angular translation, and the shift. They do not depend on $z$, $\zeta$, significance, or a mass cutoff. Their finite length variations and the other thresholded quantities are measurable in $z$. They are measurable jointly with $\vartheta$ as well: at the finitely many relevant angular levels, membership of each of the finitely many top slopes in its interval changes at finitely many translation endpoints. On the complementary parameter intervals all the relevant membership data are fixed. This also proves the asserted independence from $\zeta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Mass stopping and the exact path expansion

<a id="hier:mass-cutoff"></a>
Choose a cutoff $$M=L^{A_M},
 \label{hier:mass-cutoff}$$ where the fixed exponent $A_M$ will be taken sufficiently large in the residual estimates. Every threshold and exponent in the preceding lemmas has already been fixed without reference to $A_M$. Statements below that use $M$ hold for each such fixed exponent after increasing the lower threshold for $L$.

<a id="hier:small-node-amount"></a>
For convenience set <a id="hier:stop-cost"></a>$$C_M=L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1},
 \label{hier:stop-cost}$$ with a fixed sufficiently large $C$. Equation <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> and $p\mu=\alpha-1$ imply that a node of mass $n_J\le M$ satisfies $$V_J\le C_M n_J.
 \label{hier:small-node-amount}$$ Indeed $V_J/n_J\le1+L^{Cp}n_J^{\alpha-1}$ for the parameters.

<a id="stmt-hier-path-expansion"></a>
<div id="hier:path-expansion" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:exact-expansion"></a>
<a id="hier:top-path-expansion"></a>
<a id="hier:good-path-domination"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Expansion into top paths). *Fix $z\in Z$, a root, a shift, and parameters as above. Starting from a node with $n_J>M$, expand $$V_J=\beta_J
      \left(n_J-\sum_c n_c+\sum_c V_c\right),
 \label{hier:exact-expansion}$$ and stop a branch on first reaching a node of mass at most $M$. At each expanding node, split $n_J-\sum_c n_c$ into the individual weights of the tops which do not remain eligible at the block endpoint. At a stopped node split $V_J$ proportionally among its eligible top weights. This gives exactly one terminal path for each eligible root top $R$, with a terminal amount $e_R$ satisfying $$0<e_R\le C_Mh_R,
 \qquad
 V_{\mathrm{root}}
 =\sum_R e_R\prod_{J\text{ active on }R\text{'s path}}\beta_J.
 \label{hier:top-path-expansion}$$ If $n_{\mathrm{root}}>M$, at least one half of this sum comes from tops which are good at $z$. Consequently, $$V_{\mathrm{root}}(z)
 \le 2C_M\sum_{\substack{R\text{ eligible at root}\\R\text{ good at }z}}
     h_R(z)\prod_{J\text{ active on }R\text{'s path}}\beta_J(z).
 \label{hier:good-path-domination}$$ If $n_{\mathrm{root}}\le M$, <a href="#hier:small-node-amount" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:small-node-amount">[hier:small-node-amount]</a> applies instead.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-path-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-path-expansion</summary>

*Proof.* Identity <a href="#hier:exact-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:exact-expansion">[hier:exact-expansion]</a> follows directly from <a href="#eq:src-19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-19">[eq:src-19]</a>, because $V_c-n_c=n_c^{\alpha-p}\left\|A_c\right\|^p$. The term $n_J-\sum_c n_c$ is a sum of top weights by <a href="#hier:mass-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:mass-loss">[hier:mass-loss]</a>, now between the endpoints of the whole block. Every top either remains eligible in exactly one block child or loses eligibility and terminates at $J$. The construction therefore branches by disjoint top sets, assigning each top exactly one path. It terminates after finitely many blocks even if no mass stop occurs, since no top is eligible beyond the final level. At such a final expanding node the block-child sum is empty and all tops terminate directly.

If $R$ terminates directly at an expanding node, its terminal amount is $h_R$. If it reaches a mass-stopped node $c$, the terminal amount is $(V_c/n_c)h_R$, which is at most $C_Mh_R$ by <a href="#hier:small-node-amount" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:small-node-amount">[hier:small-node-amount]</a>. Repeated use of <a href="#hier:exact-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:exact-expansion">[hier:exact-expansion]</a> proves the equality in <a href="#hier:top-path-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:top-path-expansion">[hier:top-path-expansion]</a>.

To verify the claim about bad paths, it is useful to normalize the expansion by actual probabilities. At an active node define $$D_J=n_J-\sum_c n_c+\sum_cV_c
     =n_J+\sum_c(V_c-n_c)>M.$$ Choose a block child $c$ with probability $V_c/D_J$, or a directly terminating top $R$ with probability $h_R/D_J$. These probabilities sum to one. At a mass-stopped node choose an eligible top with probability $h_R/n_c$. The probability of a given complete terminal path is exactly its summand in <a href="#hier:top-path-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:top-path-expansion">[hier:top-path-expansion]</a> divided by $V_{\mathrm{root}}$. In fact, for an active edge $J\to c$, $V_c/D_J=\beta_JV_c/V_J$, and at a direct terminal edge $h_R/D_J=\beta_Jh_R/V_J$. The successive $V_c$ factors cancel; at a mass stop the last factor $h_R/n_c$ leaves the amount $(V_c/n_c)h_R$. This proves the asserted probability identity without an independence assumption on the amplitudes.

For a directly terminating top, its last conditional probability is $h_R/D_J\le h_R/M\le h_R$. If a top terminates in the next node by a mass stop, the combined last choice of that child and then that top has conditional probability $$\frac{V_c}{D_J}\frac{h_R}{n_c}
 \le C_M\frac{h_R}{M}\le C_Mh_R.$$ Earlier conditional probabilities are at most one. Thus the unconditional probability of any specified terminal top is at most $C_Mh_R$. This estimate also covers tops which terminate after many active ancestors; it uses only the last active step. By <a href="#hier:good-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:good-set">[hier:good-set]</a>, the total probability of bad terminal tops is at most $$C_M\sum_{R\text{ bad at }z}h_R(z)\le C_Me^{-L}<\frac12$$ for all sufficiently large $L$. Here $\log C_M=O(p\log L+\log(1+M))=o(L)$ for each fixed $A_M$. The probability identity now implies that good tops carry at least half of the total amount, and the bound on $e_R$ proves <a href="#hier:good-path-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:good-path-domination">[hier:good-path-domination]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:src-22"></a>
The remaining path estimate, proved below in [res:path-product](#res:path-product), is $$\int_{Z\cap\{R\text{ good}\}}
       h_R\prod_{J\text{ active on }R\text{'s path}}\beta_J
 \le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C\int_{\mathbb R^2}h_R.
 \label{eq:src-22}$$ [pred:section](#pred:section), [res:section](#res:section) prove <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a> from the estimates established above. Once that estimate is available, <a href="#hier:good-path-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:good-path-domination">[hier:good-path-domination]</a> and <a href="#hier:top-weight-basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:top-weight-basic">[hier:top-weight-basic]</a> control the integral of $V_{\mathrm{root}}$ by summing over the eligible root tops.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### Path drops and marked spatial cells

<a id="hier:path-drops"></a>
Fix a top $R$ and one shift. Let $J_R$ be the last block index with $i_{J_R}\le k_R$. For $0\le j\le J_R$ put $$J_j=v_{i_j}(R),\qquad n_j=n_{J_j},\qquad
 n_{J_R+1}=0,\qquad
 h_j^*=n_j-n_{j+1},\qquad u_j=\frac{h_j^*}{n_j}.
 \label{hier:path-drops}$$ For $j<J_R$, the path block child is $v_{i_{j+1}}(R)$. For $j=J_R$ there is no path child: $R$ ceases to be eligible at the next block endpoint. Other tops in $J_j$ may still continue, and all their children are retained in <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a> and <a href="#eq:src-19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-19">[eq:src-19]</a>. Only the path notation sets the next mass to zero. In particular, the last possible path drop has $h_j^*=n_j$ and $u_j=1$.

The actual active indices form the prefix before the first mass at most $M$, equivalently the indices with $n_j>M$. A path which stops by mass need not reach its final possible block $J_R$. At the last active index of such a path we retain the actual eligible next mass in <a href="#hier:path-drops" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:path-drops">[hier:path-drops]</a>; it is not set to zero merely because the expansion stops there.

<a id="stmt-hier-marking"></a>
<div id="hier:marking" class="lemma">

<a id="hier:drop-budget"></a>
<a id="hier:missing-factor"></a>
**Lemma 41** (Drops and marking). *At a point of $Z_0$, the active path drops satisfy $$\sum_{j:\,n_j>M}u_j
 \le 1+\log^+\frac{n_0}{M}\le C L.
 \label{hier:drop-budget}$$ On each line of slope $s_R$, mark an $H_j$ cell $I$ when <a id="eq:src-23"></a>$$\inf_{z\in I}n_j(z)>M,
 \qquad
 \sup_{z\in I}h_j^*(z)\ge\frac12.
 \label{eq:src-23}$$ Every point of a marked cell is active and has $h_j^*\ge c$ for an absolute $c>0$. At an active point in an unmarked cell with $h_j^*\ge1$, one has $M<n_j<M+1$ for all sufficiently large $L$. There is at most one such index on each active path, and its factor is bounded at points of $Z_0$ by $$\beta_{J_j}\le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C.
 \label{hier:missing-factor}$$ If an index is inactive at a point, the cell containing that point is unmarked. Finally, even in the last possible block, <a id="hier:mesh-within-top"></a>$$H_j\le\frac{l_R}{B\sqrt G}.
 \label{hier:mesh-within-top}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-hier-marking"><summary>Proof of stmt-hier-marking</summary>

*Proof.* If there are no active indices there is nothing to prove in <a href="#hier:drop-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:drop-budget">[hier:drop-budget]</a>. Otherwise let $q$ be the last active index. For $j<q$, the next mass is also greater than $M$, and $$u_j=1-\frac{n_{j+1}}{n_j}
 \le\log\frac{n_j}{n_{j+1}}.$$ The last term $u_q$ is at most one, whether its next mass is positive or zero. Therefore the sum is at most $1+\log(n_0/n_q)\le1+\log(n_0/M)$. This argument deliberately does not telescope through a possibly tiny mass below the stopping cutoff. Equation <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a> gives the last inequality.

Both $n_j$ and $h_j^*$ are positive sums of weights of tops eligible at $J_j$, except that the difference can be identically zero. In the last possible block it is simply $n_j$. Thus [hier:mass-motion](#hier:mass-motion) gives relative variation $1+O(G^{-1/2})$ across the whole $H_j$ cell. A marked cell has positive supremum of $h_j^*$, so this difference is not identically zero there, and its infimum is at least $\tfrac12 e^{-CG^{-1/2}}\ge\tfrac14$ for large $L$. The first marking condition supplies activity everywhere on the cell.

Suppose instead that $z$ is active, its cell is unmarked, and $h_j^*(z)\ge1$. The second marking condition then holds, so the first one fails: the cell has infimum mass at most $M$. The relative-motion bound implies $$M<n_j(z)\le e^{CG^{-1/2}}M<M+1,$$ where the last inequality holds because $M$ is a fixed polynomial in $L$. Its drop of at least one gives $n_{j+1}(z)<M$ (or zero at the last possible block), and all subsequent masses are at most that mass. This is consequently the last active index and can occur at most once. At a point of $Z_0$, since the denominator of <a href="#eq:src-19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-19">[eq:src-19]</a> is at least $n_j$, <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> gives $$\beta_{J_j}
 \le\frac{V_{J_j}}{n_j}
 \le1+L^{Cp}n_j^{\alpha-1}
 \le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C,$$ proving <a href="#hier:missing-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:missing-factor">[hier:missing-factor]</a>. If $n_j(z)\le M$, its cell cannot have infimum greater than $M$, proving the assertion about inactive indices. This observation will justify assigning the value one to the auxiliary phase factor on every unmarked cell.

Finally, eligibility at the starting level gives $d_R\le\rho_j/B^2$, equivalently $l_R\ge B^2w/\rho_j$. Together with <a href="#hier:block-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:block-ratio">[hier:block-ratio]</a>, this yields $$\frac{H_j}{l_R}
 \le\frac{\rho_j}{B^2\rho_{j+1}\sqrt G}
 \le\frac1{B\sqrt G}.$$ This only uses eligibility at the start of the block, and therefore applies to the final possible block as well. ◻

</details>

All constructions above are finite and measurable. Their constants are uniform in the finite depth, so no lower bound on a nonzero top weight, or on the number of angular levels retained, is part of the argument. The limits from finite packet collections and the angular-translation average are taken only after the subsequent sections have obtained the uniform estimates.

<a id="pred:section"></a>
#### Prediction and averaging on marked cells

Fix a shift at a vertex of the analytic parameter simplex, with imaginary parameters of Euclidean norm at most $L^2$. Retain the block notation, path top $R$, and meshes from the preceding construction. Thus $J$ is the path interval at level $i_j$, its block children $c$ have width $\rho_{j+1}$, and $$G\rho_{j+1}\le\rho_j\le B\rho_{j+1},\qquad
 H_j=\frac{w}{\rho_{j+1}\sqrt G},\qquad
 H_{j-1}=\frac{w}{\rho_j\sqrt G}.$$ The first inequalities include the initial, possibly shorter, block. The path child is denoted by $a$ when it exists. At the last path block there is no path child, even when some other tops continue; in that case the relative path drop is $u_j=1$.

<a id="stmt-pred-marked-comparison"></a>
<div id="pred:marked-comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:src-24"></a>
**Proposition 42** (Averaging factors on marked cells). *There is an absolute $c_{\rm av}>0$ with the following property, for all sufficiently large $L$. On a fixed line of slope $s_R$, each marked $H_j$-cell $I$ meeting $Z$ admits a nonnegative function $\Gamma_j$ which is constant on the $H_{j-1}$-subcells of $I$, satisfies $|I|^{-1}\int_I\Gamma_j\le1$, and obeys $$\beta_J^0(z)\le
       \exp\bigl(G^{-c_{\rm av}}u_j(z)\bigr)\Gamma_j(z)
       \qquad (z\in I).
 \label{eq:src-24}$$ Set $\Gamma_j=1$ on unmarked cells and on cells not meeting $Z$. The functions can be chosen measurably on each fixed line. No choice jointly measurable in the family of parallel lines is needed.*

</div>

We first construct a normalized prediction $\widetilde\beta$ whose average is at most one for suitable probability measures. We compare it with $\beta_J^0$, then blend it with one to obtain $\mathcal B$, whose comparison error contains the relative path drop. Finally we pass to uniform cell measure, take the finer-cell supremum $\mathcal B^\sharp$, and normalize it to obtain $\Gamma_j$. The losses in these last operations also contain the relative path drop.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### Snapshot data and denominator shrinkage

<a id="pred:drop-comparability"></a>
Fix a marked cell $I$ meeting $Z$, and choose one snapshot $z_0\in I\cap Z$. Parameterize the line by horizontal displacement $b$ from $z_0$, and also write $I$ for the translated parameter interval. All unprimed masses and amplitudes below are evaluated at $z_0$; a prime denotes evaluation at $z_0+(b,s_Rb)$. Let $n=n_J(z_0)$ and $\upsilon=u_j(z_0)$. If the path child exists, then $\upsilon=1-n_a/n$; otherwise $\upsilon=1$. The positive-mass motion bounds give $$\tfrac12\upsilon\le u_j(z)\le2\upsilon\qquad(z\in I)
 \label{pred:drop-comparability}$$ for large $L$. Marking ensures $\upsilon>0$. Only nonempty children are used. Their masses are positive at every point, since the sets of eligible tops are fixed and their weights are strictly positive.

Use the pointwise normalization <a href="#eq:src-25" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-25">[eq:src-25]</a>, with $l_c$ and $X_c$ evaluated at the snapshot and the primed quantities evaluated at the displaced point. That identity expresses $\beta_J^0$ in either set of data. The next construction shrinks snapshot vectors to obtain one-sided denominator comparisons for those children. When $\upsilon<1/2$, the path vector will instead remain unchanged so that its numerator and denominator variations can be compared together. All vector norms in this section are in $\mathcal H$. The weights satisfy $\sum_c l_c\le1$, and, when there is a path child, $\sum_{c\ne a}l_c\le\upsilon$. When there is none, an off-path sum means the sum over all children.

<a id="pred:snapshot-size"></a>
Choose a fixed exponent $C_*$, large enough for the estimates below, and put <a id="pred:amplitude-scale"></a>$$K_*=L^{C_*}(1+N^\mu).
 \label{pred:amplitude-scale}$$ Equations <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a> and <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> imply, at the snapshot, $$\left\|X_c\right\|\le K_*,\qquad n^\mu\le K_*,\qquad
 \sum_{c\ne a}l_c^{1-\mu}\left\|X_c\right\|\le K_*\upsilon.
 \label{pred:snapshot-size}$$ Enlarging the fixed exponent absorbs absolute constants here and below. For the last estimate use $$l_c^{1-\mu}X_c=n^{\mu-1}A_c
              =n^\mu l_c(A_c/n_c).$$ The sum over all children in this estimate is at most $K_*$.

<a id="pred:scaled-motion"></a>
<a id="pred:numerator-motion"></a>
Let $\gamma>0$ be a sufficiently small fixed exponent below the motion exponent in [hier:amplitude-motion](#hier:amplitude-motion), and set $\varepsilon=G^{-\gamma}$. This choice is independent of the depth, dimension, and parameters $L,p,D$. Decreasing $\gamma$, if necessary, absorbs the fixed powers of $L$ in the following consequences of the motion estimates: $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|l_c'^{1/p}X_c'
       -\mathcal D_c(b)l_c^{1/p}X_c\right\|
       &\le\varepsilon l_c^{1/p}n_c^\mu,
       \label{pred:scaled-motion}\\
 \left\|n'^{\mu-1}\lambda_c'A_c'
       -n^{\mu-1}\lambda_c\mathcal D_c(b)A_c\right\|
       &\le\varepsilon K_*l_c.
       \label{pred:numerator-motion}
\end{aligned}$$ Both estimates hold throughout $I$, without a good-point hypothesis at the displaced point. For the scalar rescaling in the first one, write $l_c^{1/p}X_c=n^{-1/p}n_c^{\alpha/p-1}A_c$. The exponents $-1/p$ and $\alpha/p-1$ are absolutely bounded. Their factors have the stated relative mass motion; the error in $A_c$ is bounded by <a href="#eq:src-21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-21">[eq:src-21]</a>, and its snapshot size is at most $L^C n_c$. This proves <a href="#pred:scaled-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:scaled-motion">[pred:scaled-motion]</a>. For the second estimate use $n^{\mu-1}$, the relative complex motion of $\lambda_c$, $\left|\lambda_c\right|\le1$, and $n^{\mu-1}n_c=n^\mu l_c\le K_*l_c$.

<a id="pred:denominator-lower-bound"></a>
Shrink $A_c$ radially toward zero by a distance at most $2\varepsilon n_c$, choosing $$\widetilde A_c=
 \begin{cases}
   (1-2\varepsilon n_c/\left\|A_c\right\|)_+ A_c,& A_c\ne0,\\
   0,& A_c=0.
 \end{cases}$$ If no eligible top in $c$ is significant at $z_0$, set $\widetilde A_c=0$. This fits within the same error: [hier:amplitudes](#hier:amplitudes) and the mask suppression give $$\left\|A_c\right\|\le L^C e^{-L\log L}n_c\le\varepsilon n_c$$ for such a child. There is one exception: when $\upsilon<1/2$, leave the path vector unchanged, setting $\widetilde A_a=A_a$. Define $\widetilde X_c=n_c^{\mu-1}\widetilde A_c$. For every child to which shrinkage was applied, $$l_c'\left\|X_c'\right\|^p\ge l_c\left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^p
             \qquad(b\in I).
 \label{pred:denominator-lower-bound}$$ Indeed radial shrinkage reduces the norm of $l_c^{1/p}X_c$ by the smaller of its whole norm and $2\varepsilon l_c^{1/p}n_c^\mu$, whereas <a href="#pred:scaled-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:scaled-motion">[pred:scaled-motion]</a> permits a reduction of at most $\varepsilon l_c^{1/p}n_c^\mu$ at a displaced point.

<a id="pred:shrunk-numerator-motion"></a>
The bounds in <a href="#pred:snapshot-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:snapshot-size">[pred:snapshot-size]</a> continue to hold with $\widetilde X_c$. Including the shrinkage error in <a href="#pred:numerator-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:numerator-motion">[pred:numerator-motion]</a> gives $$\left\|l_c'^{1-\mu}\lambda_c'X_c'
       -l_c^{1-\mu}\lambda_c\mathcal D_c(b)\widetilde X_c\right\|
       \le C\varepsilon K_* l_c.
 \label{pred:shrunk-numerator-motion}$$

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
#### Exact averaging of the prediction

<a id="pred:model"></a>
On the whole parameter line, define $$F(b)=\sum_c l_c^{1-\mu}\lambda_c
                   \mathcal D_c(b)\widetilde X_c,\qquad
 \widetilde\beta(b)=
       \frac{1+\left\|F(b)\right\|^p}
            {1+\sum_c l_c\left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^p}.
 \label{pred:model}$$ The masses, scalar weights, and vectors are fixed snapshot data. Only the diagonal unitaries $\mathcal D_c(b)_{\xi\xi}=e^{i(s_R-t_c)\eta_\xi b}$ vary.

<a id="stmt-pred-fourier-average"></a>
<div id="pred:fourier-average" class="lemma">

<a id="pred:separation-bandwidth"></a>
**Lemma 43** (Fourier-supported averaging). *There is a fixed sufficiently small $\epsilon'>0$ such that, with $$Q=G^{3/4}\rho_{j+1},\qquad \delta=\epsilon' Q/w,
 \label{pred:separation-bandwidth}$$ every positive probability $\nu$ satisfying $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\widehat\nu\subset(-\delta,\delta)$ obeys $\int\widetilde\beta\,\,\mathrm d\nu\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pred-fourier-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-pred-fourier-average</summary>

*Proof.* Use only children for which $\widetilde X_c\ne0$; an empty collection gives the assertion immediately. Apply the clock selection from <a href="#pred:retention-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:retention-probability">[pred:retention-probability]</a> to this finite subfamily at the snapshot, with $Q$ as in <a href="#pred:separation-bandwidth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:separation-bandwidth">[pred:separation-bandwidth]</a>. The clock rates are fixed as $b$ varies. Each retained set has pairwise interval distance at least $Q$, and the retention probabilities satisfy $\pi_c\ge n_c/m_c$.

Every used child other than the possible unshrunk path child contains a significant eligible top at $z_0$. From each retained child other than that possible exception, select one such top. Their slopes are $Q$-separated, their precisions satisfy $$d_T\le\rho_{j+1}/B^2\le Q/B^2,$$ and their diameter is at most $\rho_j\le B\rho_{j+1}\le BQ$. The simultaneous count in [count:simultaneous](#count:simultaneous), with $D$ as chosen in [count:choice-D](#count:choice-D), bounds this list by $D/100$. Adding the possible retained path child leaves at most $D$ members. Clock exclusion separates that extra child too; it does not require a significant representative for that one child.

<a id="pred:weighted-phase-bound"></a>
In each Hilbert component, the retained carrier frequencies are separated by at least $c Q/w$, with $c>0$ absolute, since $\eta_\xi\asymp w^{-1}$. Choose $\epsilon'\le\epsilon_0c$. For a retained set $\mathcal S$, let $$F_{\mathcal S}(b)=
     \sum_{c\in\mathcal S}\pi_c^{-1}
       l_c^{1-\mu}\lambda_c\mathcal D_c(b)\widetilde X_c.$$ Expectation over the clocks gives $\mathbb E_{\mathcal S}F_{\mathcal S}=F$; no independence of retention indicators is asserted or needed. Put $r=r_*$. Minkowski in that expectation, <a href="#eq:src-13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-13">[eq:src-13]</a>, and Jensen for the concave function $t^{1/r}$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|F\right\|_{L^p(\nu;\mathcal H)}
 &\le \mathbb E_{\mathcal S}
       \left(\sum_{c\in\mathcal S}
          \pi_c^{-r}l_c\left|\lambda_c\right|^r
                           \left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^r\right)^{1/r}
       \notag\\
 &\le\left(\sum_c\pi_c^{1-r}l_c\left|\lambda_c\right|^r
                           \left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^r\right)^{1/r}
       \notag\\
 &\le\left(\sum_c l_c\left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^r\right)^{1/r}
 \le\left(\sum_c l_c\left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^p\right)^{1/p}.
 \label{pred:weighted-phase-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The power of $l_c$ uses $r(1-\mu)=1$. The third line uses the retention cancellation <a href="#hier:selection-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:selection-cancellation">[hier:selection-cancellation]</a> for the present child subfamily. The last inequality in <a href="#pred:weighted-phase-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:weighted-phase-bound">[pred:weighted-phase-bound]</a> is Hölder with weights of total mass at most one and $r\le p$; the missing mass can be placed at a zero vector. Raise to power $p$, add one, and divide by the constant denominator in <a href="#pred:model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:model">[pred:model]</a>. ◻

</details>

summarizes the horizontal scales used in the comparison and discretization below.

<figure id="fig:phase-scales">

<figcaption>The horizontal-length axis (schematic) for a marked block. Here $Q = G^{3/4}ρ_{j + 1}$ is the retained angular separation and $Gρ_{j + 1} ≤ ρ_{j} ≤ Bρ_{j + 1}$, including the initial block. The quantity $l_{*} = B^{2}w/ρ_{j + 1}$ is the lower bound for predictable packet lengths in <a href="#hier:predictable-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:predictable-length">[hier:predictable-length]</a>. On an interval of length $H_{j}$, the separated relative phases oscillate many times while the packet envelopes move little. The preceding mesh also satisfies $H_{j − 1}ρ_{j}/w = G^{−1/2}$, which controls the step supremums. Absolute frequency constants are suppressed.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-phase-scales"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:phase-scales`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\small,>=Stealth]
