<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
#### Comparison to the actual factor

<a id="pred:log-derivatives"></a>
On the underlying real Hilbert space put $\mathcal L(Y)=\log(1+\left\|Y\right\|^p)$. For $p\ge4$, $$\left\|\nabla\mathcal L(Y)\right\|\le p,\qquad
 \left\|D^2\mathcal L(Y)\right\|\le C p^2.
 \label{pred:log-derivatives}$$ Indeed its radial first derivative is $p r^{p-1}/(1+r^p)$, its radial second derivative is $$\frac{p(p-1)r^{p-2}}{1+r^p}
       -\frac{p^2r^{2p-2}}{(1+r^p)^2},$$ and each tangential Hessian eigenvalue is $p r^{p-2}/(1+r^p)$. Splitting into $r\le1$ and $r\ge1$ proves the bounds; the formulas extend continuously at zero. No dimension enters these estimates.

<a id="pred:balanced-comparison"></a>
If $\upsilon\ge1/2$, every child was shrunk. The denominator comparison <a href="#pred:denominator-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:denominator-lower-bound">[pred:denominator-lower-bound]</a>, the sum of <a href="#pred:shrunk-numerator-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:shrunk-numerator-motion">[pred:shrunk-numerator-motion]</a>, and <a href="#pred:log-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:log-derivatives">[pred:log-derivatives]</a> yield $$\log\beta_J^0(z)-\log\widetilde\beta(b)
       \le CpK_*\varepsilon\upsilon,\qquad \upsilon\ge1/2.
 \label{pred:balanced-comparison}$$ The factor $\upsilon$ here costs only an absolute factor of two.

<a id="pred:path-baseline"></a>
For $0<\upsilon<1/2$, retaining that factor requires the unshrunk path vector. Write $\sigma_a=\lambda_a/\left|\lambda_a\right|$ and remove $\mathcal D_a(b)$ and $\sigma_a$ from the predicted numerator. With $$\begin{aligned}
 Y&=l_a^{1/p}X_a,\\
 C_a&=l_a^{1-\mu-1/p}\left|\lambda_a\right|
      =l_a^{1-\alpha/p}(n_a/m_a)^\mu,\\
 Z_1(b)&=\sigma_a^{-1}\mathcal D_a(b)^{-1}
        \sum_{c\ne a}l_c^{1-\mu}\lambda_c
                         \mathcal D_c(b)\widetilde X_c,\\
 R_1&=\sum_{c\ne a}l_c\left\|\widetilde X_c\right\|^p,
\end{aligned}$$ the prediction becomes $$\widetilde\beta(b)=
       \frac{1+\left\|C_aY+Z_1(b)\right\|^p}
            {1+\left\|Y\right\|^p+R_1}.
 \label{pred:path-baseline}$$ Here $Y,C_a$, and the denominator are constant on the whole line. Since $n_a\le m_a\le n$, both $l_a$ and $n_a/m_a$ lie in $[1-\upsilon,1]$. As $1-\alpha/p\ge0$, we have <a id="pred:path-coefficient"></a>$$0<C_a\le1,\qquad 1-C_a\le C\upsilon,\qquad
 \left\|Z_1(b)\right\|\le K_*\upsilon.
 \label{pred:path-coefficient}$$

<a id="pred:path-errors"></a>
In the actual numerator remove $\mathcal D_a(b)$ and $\sigma_a'=\lambda_a'/\left|\lambda_a'\right|$. Define $$Y'=l_a'^{1/p}\mathcal D_a(b)^{-1}X_a',\qquad
 C_a'=l_a'^{1-\mu-1/p}\left|\lambda_a'\right|,\qquad
 R_1'=\sum_{c\ne a}l_c'\left\|X_c'\right\|^p,$$ and let $Z_1'$ be the remaining, similarly rotated off-path sum. The actual ratio has the form <a href="#pred:path-baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:path-baseline">[pred:path-baseline]</a> with primes. We have $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|Y'-Y\right\|&\le C\varepsilon K_*,
 &\left|C_a'-C_a\right|&\le C\varepsilon\upsilon,\notag\\
 \left\|Z_1'-Z_1\right\|&\le C\varepsilon K_*\upsilon,
 &R_1'&\ge R_1.
 \label{pred:path-errors}
\end{aligned}$$ The first and last statements follow from <a href="#pred:scaled-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:scaled-motion">[pred:scaled-motion]</a> and <a href="#pred:denominator-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:denominator-lower-bound">[pred:denominator-lower-bound]</a>. For the off-path sum, sum <a href="#pred:shrunk-numerator-motion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:shrunk-numerator-motion">[pred:shrunk-numerator-motion]</a> over $c\ne a$, whose weights sum to at most $\upsilon$. Changing the removed scalar phase costs at most $C\varepsilon$ times the off-path size, because $\left|\sigma_a'-\sigma_a\right|\le C\varepsilon$.

The extra $\upsilon$ in the coefficient estimate follows from the complementary-mass assertion of [hier:mass-motion](#hier:mass-motion). If $r=A/(A+B)$, with $A>0$, $B\ge0$, and the positive summands change relatively by at most $C\varepsilon$, then $$|r'-r|\le C\varepsilon r(1-r),\qquad
 |\log r'-\log r|\le C\varepsilon(1-r).$$ If $B=0$, the ratio is identically one. Apply this to $n_a/n$, with complement $n-n_a$, and to $n_a/m_a$, with complement $m_a-n_a$. Both values of $1-r$ are at most $\upsilon$. The bounded nonnegative powers defining $C_a$ give its claimed variation. This concerns the modulus of the path weight; its scalar phase has already been removed.

<a id="eq:src-26"></a>
Set $\mathfrak r=R_1/(1+\left\|Y\right\|^p+R_1)$. We claim $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\beta_J^0(z)-\log\widetilde\beta(b)
    &\le C p^2K_*^2\varepsilon\upsilon
           +C pK_*\varepsilon\mathfrak r,\notag\\
 -\log\widetilde\beta(b)
    &\ge \mathfrak r-CpK_*\upsilon.
 \label{eq:src-26}
\end{aligned}$$ Hold $Z_1=Z_1(b)$ fixed when comparing $Y$ with $Y'$. The derivative in $V$ of $\mathcal L(C_aV+Z_1)-\mathcal L(V)$, along that segment, is bounded by $$p|1-C_a|+Cp^2\bigl(|1-C_a|\left\|V\right\|+\left\|Z_1\right\|\bigr)
       \le Cp^2K_*\upsilon.$$ Equation <a href="#pred:path-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:path-errors">[pred:path-errors]</a> bounds its change by $Cp^2K_*^2\varepsilon\upsilon$. The changes in $C_a,Z_1$ fit the same bound by <a href="#pred:log-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:log-derivatives">[pred:log-derivatives]</a>.

For the residual denominator, set $$J(V)=-\log(1+R_1e^{-\mathcal L(V)}).$$ Its derivative has norm at most $pR_1/(e^{\mathcal L(V)}+R_1)$. On the segment from $Y$ to $Y'$, $\left|\mathcal L(V)-\mathcal L(Y)\right|\le CpK_*\varepsilon$. This tends to zero as $L$ increases, so that residual ratio is at most $2\mathfrak r$, and $J(Y')-J(Y)\le CpK_*\varepsilon\mathfrak r$. The actual residual $R_1'\ge R_1$ can only decrease the actual ratio. This proves the first inequality of <a href="#eq:src-26" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-26">[eq:src-26]</a>. For the second, use $C_a\le1$, <a href="#pred:log-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:log-derivatives">[pred:log-derivatives]</a>, and $\log(1+t)\ge t/(1+t)$: $$\begin{aligned}
 -\log\widetilde\beta
 &=\mathcal L(Y)-\mathcal L(C_aY+Z_1)
       +\log(1+R_1e^{-\mathcal L(Y)})\\
 &\ge-p\left\|Z_1\right\|+\mathfrak r
 \ge\mathfrak r-CpK_*\upsilon.
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
#### Absorbing the baseline error by blending

<a id="pred:subpower-amplitude"></a>
The parameter sizes give $$pK_* = G^{o(1)}\quad\text{as }L\longrightarrow\infty,
 \label{pred:subpower-amplitude}$$ with every preceding constant fixed. Indeed $\log N\le2L$, $\mu=O(L^{-1/2})$, and $\log G\asymp L^{7/10}$, so $\log(pK_*)=O(\sqrt L+\log L)=o(\log G)$. All the preceding smallness requirements consequently follow from a single sufficiently large lower bound on $L$.

<a id="pred:blended-domination"></a>
Set $\theta_{\mathrm{bl}}=\sqrt\varepsilon$ and blend the prediction with one: <a id="pred:blend"></a>$$\mathcal B(b)=(1-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}})\widetilde\beta(b)+\theta_{\mathrm{bl}}.
 \label{pred:blend}$$ It retains average at most one for every probability in [pred:fourier-average](#pred:fourier-average). Concavity of the logarithm gives $$\log \mathcal B-\log\widetilde\beta
       \ge-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}}\log\widetilde\beta.$$ For $\upsilon<1/2$, Equation <a href="#eq:src-26" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-26">[eq:src-26]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\beta_J^0-\log \mathcal B
 &\le\bigl(Cp^2K_*^2\varepsilon+CpK_*\theta_{\mathrm{bl}}\bigr)\upsilon
       +\bigl(CpK_*\varepsilon-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}}\bigr)\mathfrak r\\
 &\le\bigl(Cp^2K_*^2\varepsilon
                  +CpK_*\sqrt\varepsilon\bigr)\upsilon.
\end{aligned}$$ The last coefficient of $\mathfrak r$ is nonpositive for large $L$. For $\upsilon\ge1/2$, use <a href="#pred:balanced-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:balanced-comparison">[pred:balanced-comparison]</a> and $\log\widetilde\beta\le\mathcal L(F)\le p\left\|F\right\|\le CpK_*$ to obtain the same last bound. Therefore $$\beta_J^0(z)\le e^{a_L\upsilon}\mathcal B(b),\qquad
 a_L=Cp^2K_*^2\varepsilon+CpK_*\sqrt\varepsilon
       \le G^{-\gamma/3}
 \label{pred:blended-domination}$$ for sufficiently large $L$. The baseline has absorbed the term proportional to $\mathfrak r$; the remaining error is proportional to $\upsilon$.

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
#### Changing to uniform measure without a loss per level

<a id="pred:majorant-probability"></a>
Choose a fixed nonnegative Schwartz function $k$ of integral one with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\widehat k\subset[-1/4,1/4]$. For example, square the absolute value of the inverse Fourier transform of a nonzero smooth function supported in $(-1/8,1/8)$ and normalize its integral. Let $H=H_j$, $h=G^{-1/16}$, $s=HG^{-1/8}$, and $k_s(t)=s^{-1}k(t/s)$. Let $I^+$ be the concentric enlargement of $I$ of length $(1+h)H$, and define $$\,\mathrm d\nu_I(t)=
      \left(\frac{\mathbf 1_{I^+}}{|I^+|}*k_s\right)(t)\,\,\mathrm dt.
 \label{pred:majorant-probability}$$ This is a positive probability whose Fourier transform is supported in $[-1/(4s),1/(4s)]$. Since $H\delta=\epsilon'G^{1/4}$, this support lies strictly inside $(-\delta,\delta)$ for large $L$.

<a id="pred:measure-decomposition"></a>
Every point of $I$ is at distance at least $hH/2$ from the complement of $I^+$. The Schwartz tail beyond $(hH)/(2s)=G^{1/16}/2$ is $O(G^{-1/8})$, by a fixed tail estimate. Thus the density in <a href="#pred:majorant-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:majorant-probability">[pred:majorant-probability]</a> is at least $H^{-1}(1-CG^{-1/16})$ on $I$. Writing $\omega_I$ for uniform probability on $I$, choose $0<\eta\le CG^{-1/16}<1/2$ so that $$\nu_I\ge(1-\eta)\omega_I,\qquad
 \nu_I=(1-\eta)\omega_I+\eta\nu_{\rm rem}
 \label{pred:measure-decomposition}$$ for a positive probability $\nu_{\rm rem}$.

<a id="pred:negative-deviation"></a>
The bound $\int \mathcal B\,\,\mathrm d\nu_I\le1$ alone would lose an amount of order $\eta$ on changing measure. We need a bound proportional to $\upsilon$. If $\widetilde\beta\le1$ on the whole line, then $\mathcal B\le1$ and its uniform average is already at most one. Otherwise choose $b_0$ with $\widetilde\beta(b_0)>1$. When $\upsilon<1/2$, the denominator and path vector in <a href="#pred:path-baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:path-baseline">[pred:path-baseline]</a> are fixed. For all $b\in\mathbb R$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\widetilde\beta(b)
 &\ge\log\widetilde\beta(b_0)
          -p\left\|Z_1(b)-Z_1(b_0)\right\|\\
 &\ge-2pK_*\upsilon.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$(1-\widetilde\beta(b))_+\le CpK_*\upsilon
       \qquad(b\in\mathbb R).
 \label{pred:negative-deviation}$$ This does not assume an upper bound on $R_1$: its contribution cancels in the logarithmic difference. Equivalently, a value exceeding one already forces $R_1/(1+\left\|Y\right\|^p)\le e^{CpK_*\upsilon}-1$. For $\upsilon\ge1/2$, Equation <a href="#pred:negative-deviation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:negative-deviation">[pred:negative-deviation]</a> follows from nonnegativity since $pK_*\upsilon\ge1$. The blend inherits the same lower bound, as $(1-\mathcal B)_+=(1-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}})(1-\widetilde\beta)_+$.

<a id="pred:uniform-average"></a>
In the case where the prediction exceeds one somewhere, <a href="#pred:measure-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:measure-decomposition">[pred:measure-decomposition]</a> gives $$1\ge\int \mathcal B\,\,\mathrm d\nu_I
 \ge(1-\eta)\int \mathcal B\,\,\mathrm d\omega_I
           +\eta(1-CpK_*\upsilon).$$ Including the easier case $\mathcal B\le1$, this proves $$\int \mathcal B\,\,\mathrm d\omega_I\le1+C G^{-1/16}pK_*\upsilon.
 \label{pred:uniform-average}$$ The relative mass drop has survived the change of probability.

<a id="pm-h-58"></a>
#### Step supremums and normalization

The points $s_R,t_c$ lie in $J$, so all component frequencies of $\mathcal D_c$ have magnitude at most $C\rho_j/w$. For $\upsilon\ge1/2$, differentiating <a href="#pred:model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:model">[pred:model]</a> and using <a href="#pred:snapshot-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:snapshot-size">[pred:snapshot-size]</a> gives $\left\|\partial_b F(b)\right\|\le C(\rho_j/w)K_*$. For $\upsilon<1/2$, remove the path carrier as in <a href="#pred:path-baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:path-baseline">[pred:path-baseline]</a>. The off-path frequencies are $(t_a-t_c)\eta_\xi$, again of magnitude at most $C\rho_j/w$, so $\left\|\partial_b Z_1(b)\right\|\le C(\rho_j/w)K_*\upsilon$. In both cases, $$\left|\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm db}\log\widetilde\beta(b)\right|
       \le CpK_*\upsilon\frac{\rho_j}{w}.$$ Blending cannot increase the absolute logarithmic derivative, because $$\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm db}\log \mathcal B
   =\frac{(1-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}})\widetilde\beta}{\mathcal B}
               \frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm db}\log\widetilde\beta,\qquad
 0\le\frac{(1-\theta_{\mathrm{bl}})\widetilde\beta}{\mathcal B}\le1.$$

<a id="pred:step-supremum"></a>
<a id="pred:gamma-normalization"></a>
<a id="pred:normalization-cost"></a>
On each $H_{j-1}$-subcell $I'\subset I$, let $\mathcal B^\sharp$ equal $\sup_{b\in\overline{I'}}\mathcal B(b)$. The prediction is positive and continuous, and these compact-cell supremums are finite. The meshes are nested. Since $H_{j-1}\rho_j/w=G^{-1/2}$, $$\mathcal B\le \mathcal B^\sharp\le e^{b_L\upsilon}\mathcal B,\qquad
 b_L=CpK_*G^{-1/2}.
 \label{pred:step-supremum}$$ Use the fixed half-open mesh convention at endpoints; taking supremums over closed subcells preserves the inequalities. Define $$m_I=\max\left(1,\int \mathcal B^\sharp\,\,\mathrm d\omega_I\right),\qquad
 \Gamma_j=\mathcal B^\sharp/m_I\quad\text{on }I.
 \label{pred:gamma-normalization}$$ This function is nonnegative, constant on the finer cells, and has average at most one. Equations <a href="#pred:uniform-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:uniform-average">[pred:uniform-average]</a> and <a href="#pred:step-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:step-supremum">[pred:step-supremum]</a> yield $$\log m_I\le\bigl(b_L+CG^{-1/16}pK_*\bigr)\upsilon.
 \label{pred:normalization-cost}$$

<details id="proof-stmt-pred-fourier-average-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-pred-fourier-average</summary>

*Proof of [pred:marked-comparison](#pred:marked-comparison).* Perform the construction on each marked cell meeting $Z$. Equations <a href="#pred:blended-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:blended-domination">[pred:blended-domination]</a>, <a href="#pred:step-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:step-supremum">[pred:step-supremum]</a>, and <a href="#pred:gamma-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:gamma-normalization">[pred:gamma-normalization]</a> give $$\beta_J^0(z)\le e^{a_L\upsilon}\mathcal B(b)
       \le e^{a_L\upsilon}\mathcal B^\sharp(b)
       =e^{a_L\upsilon}m_I\Gamma_j(b).$$ By <a href="#pred:normalization-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:normalization-cost">[pred:normalization-cost]</a>, the exponent is at most $$\bigl(G^{-\gamma/3}+CpK_*G^{-1/2}
                    +CpK_*G^{-1/16}\bigr)\upsilon.$$ Equations <a href="#pred:subpower-amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:subpower-amplitude">[pred:subpower-amplitude]</a> and <a href="#pred:drop-comparability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:drop-comparability">[pred:drop-comparability]</a> bound this by $G^{-c_{\rm av}}u_j(z)$, for a fixed positive exponent: choose $c_{\rm av}<\min(\gamma/4,1/64)$ and then take $L$ sufficiently large. This proves <a href="#eq:src-24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-24">[eq:src-24]</a> with no error independent of the mass drop.

There are countably many cells on each fixed line and finitely many levels in the current truncation. Choose one snapshot in every relevant cell, use <a href="#pred:gamma-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pred:gamma-normalization">[pred:gamma-normalization]</a> there, and set $\Gamma_j=1$ elsewhere. This is a measurable step function of the line parameter. The proof of [res:path-product](#res:path-product) integrates the original integrand on lines where its slice is measurable, as holds for almost every transverse parameter. It therefore needs no jointly measurable snapshot choices across parallel lines. ◻

</details>

<a id="res:section"></a>
### Residual terms and integration along paths

<a id="res:small-mu"></a>
We work with a finite tile collection and a fixed angular translation. Fix a shift $\sigma$ and a parameter $\zeta$ whose real part is the corresponding vertex: $\Re\zeta_\sigma=\mu$, all other real parts vanish, and $\left\|\Im\zeta\right\|_2\le L^2$. All estimates in this section are uniform in these choices, the number of tiles, the angular depth, and $\dim\mathcal H$. We take $L$ sufficiently large that $$p>\alpha,\qquad 0<\mu\le\frac1{12}.
  \label{res:small-mu}$$ We retain the stopping threshold $M=L^{A_M}$ from <a href="#hier:mass-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:mass-cutoff">[hier:mass-cutoff]</a>; its fixed exponent will be chosen at the end of the residual estimates.

<a id="res:relative-drop-sum"></a>
Fix a path top $R$. We retain the block notation of the hierarchy: $J_j=v_{i_j}(R)$, $n_j=n_{J_j}$, and $A_j=A_{J_j}$. The last possible block is the last one whose starting node contains $R$ as an eligible top. At this last block there is no path child, even if other tops continue; the next path mass is defined to be zero. At every other block write $J_{j+1}$ for the path child and set $$h_j^*=n_j-n_{j+1},\qquad u_j=\frac{h_j^*}{n_j}.$$ An index is active if $n_j>M$. The active indices form a prefix. On $Z$, the mass bound <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a> and monotonicity give $$\sum_{j\text{ active}}u_j\le C L.
  \label{res:relative-drop-sum}$$ Indeed, except for the last active index, use $1-n_{j+1}/n_j\le\log(n_j/n_{j+1})$ and telescope down to a mass larger than $M$. The final summand is at most one. This proof also applies when the path stops by ineligibility.

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
#### Marked residuals

<a id="eq:src-27"></a>
For $n>0$ and $A\in\mathcal H$, put $$V(n,A)=n+n^{\alpha-p}\left\|A\right\|^{p}
        =n\bigl(1+\left\|n^{\mu-1}A\right\|^{p}\bigr).$$ The function $\mathcal L_p(Y)=\log(1+\left\|Y\right\|^{p})$ is globally $p$-Lipschitz on the underlying real Hilbert space. Away from zero its derivative has norm $p\left\|Y\right\|^{p-1}/(1+\left\|Y\right\|^{p})\le p$, and the assertion at zero follows by continuity. Since $\beta_J$ and $\beta_J^0$ have the same denominator, the decomposition <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a> therefore implies $$\log\beta_{J_j}-\log\beta_{J_j}^0
       \le Cp\,n_j^{-1+\mu}\left\|B_{J_j}\right\|.
  \label{eq:src-27}$$

<a id="stmt-res-block-square"></a>
<div id="res:block-square" class="lemma">

<a id="res:block-square-bound"></a>
**Lemma 44** (Square summation of residual blocks). *At a point $z\in Z$ where $R$ is a good path, $$\left(\sum_j\left\|B_{J_j}(z)\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}\le L^{C_b}.
  \label{res:block-square-bound}$$ Here the sum can be restricted to any subset of the possible blocks. The fixed exponent $C_b$ is independent of $M$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-block-square"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-block-square</summary>

<a id="res:residual-range"></a>
*Proof.* A residual packet in block $j$ has $$\frac{\rho_{j+1}}{B^2}<d_s\le\frac{\rho_j}{B^2},
  \qquad i_j\le k_s<i_{j+1}.
  \label{res:residual-range}$$ Because $\rho_j/\rho_{j+1}\le B$, this block contains at most $N_{\mathrm{len}}:=C(1+\log B)$ dyadic packet lengths. Also $s_{T(s)},s_R\in J_j$, so the top assignment and <a href="#res:residual-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:residual-range">[res:residual-range]</a> imply $$\left|\theta_s-s_R\right|
     \le C d_s+\rho_j
     \le C B^3 d_s\le C B^4d_s.$$ Thus the reference-line estimates apply whenever $\ell_s\le l_R$.

The coefficient multiplying $q_{T(s)}\phi_s$ in $B_{J_j}$ is a product of transition weights of modulus at most one, followed by $n_{v_{k_s}(T(s))}^{-\mu/K}$. The ending mass is at least $h_{T(s)}$. Consequently the modulus of the coefficient multiplying $\phi_s$ is at most $|q_{T(s)}|h_{T(s)}^{-\mu/K}\le C$. This last bound follows from the mask estimate <a href="#eq:src-8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-8">[eq:src-8]</a>: on the significant region $h_T\ge1$, while off that region the mask has faster decay than this fixed small negative power of $h_T$.

At $z$ let $e_\ell$ and $a_\ell$ be the sums of the absolute error and main envelopes, respectively, at length $\ell$ in the fixed family $\mathcal P_R$ of <a href="#hier:reference-packets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:reference-packets">[hier:reference-packets]</a>. The preceding bounds place every residual packet with $\ell_s\le l_R$ in this family, so its absolute envelopes are included in these positive sums. Goodness of $R$, as specified in [hier:good-paths](#hier:good-paths), gives $$\sum_{\ell\le l_R}e_\ell\le L^C,
  \qquad
  \left(\sum_{\ell\le l_R}a_\ell^2\right)^{1/2}\le L^C.$$ The contribution of such lengths to $\left\|B_{J_j}\right\|$ is bounded by $C\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal I_j}(e_\ell+a_\ell)$, where $\mathcal I_j$ is the set of residual lengths in that block. The sets $\mathcal I_j$ are disjoint. Positivity and Cauchy–Schwarz within each block therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\{\sum_j
    \left(\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal I_j,\,\ell\le l_R}
                         e_\ell\right)^2\right\}^{1/2}
       &\le\sum_{\ell\le l_R}e_\ell,\\
 \sum_j\left(\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal I_j,\,\ell\le l_R}
                         a_\ell\right)^2
       &\le N_{\mathrm{len}}\sum_{\ell\le l_R}a_\ell^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The factor $N_{\mathrm{len}}$ is polynomial in $L$.

Before the last possible block, $R$ remains eligible at the child level. Hence $d_R\le\rho_{j+1}/B^2<d_s$, and consequently $\ell_s<l_R$. Lengths larger than $l_R$ can occur only in the last possible block. At each of its at most $N_{\mathrm{len}}$ lengths, the total absolute coefficient envelope is bounded by a constant, by [conc:single-scale](#conc:single-scale), applied with reference length equal to that scale. To recall the reason this bound has no factor counting the available labels, the condition $|\theta_s-s_R|\le CB^4d_s$ makes the analyzing and synthesizing box envelopes comparable to common $s_R$-coordinate envelopes, since $W\gg B^4w$. Summing centers first produces a common positive convolution kernel of integral $O(1)$. At each source point only boundedly many selector labels are nonzero. Summing these labels then uses $\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal H}\le1$, and costs $O(1)$. The uniformly bounded multipliers just considered preserve this absolute estimate. The last block therefore contributes at most $C N_{\mathrm{len}}$ in norm. Combining the estimates proves <a href="#res:block-square-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:block-square-bound">[res:block-square-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="res:marked-cost"></a>
By [hier:marking](#hier:marking), at any marked point one has $h_j^*\ge c_m$ for a fixed $c_m>0$. Monotonicity of the masses therefore implies <a id="res:marked-shell-count"></a>$$\#\{j:\ j\text{ marked at }z,\ t\le n_j<2t\}
         \le C(1+t),\qquad t\ge M.
  \label{res:marked-shell-count}$$ For completeness, all but the final marked index in this shell account for disjoint mass drops of size at least $c_m$ while the mass lies in an interval of length at most $t$; the final index costs one even if its drop leaves the shell. Dyadic summation gives <a id="res:marked-weight-sum"></a>$$\sum_{j\text{ marked at }z}n_j^{-2+2\mu}
       \le C\sum_{r\ge0}(2^rM)^{-1+2\mu}
       \le C M^{-1+2\mu}.
 \label{res:marked-weight-sum}$$ Combining <a href="#eq:src-27" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-27">[eq:src-27]</a>, , and Cauchy–Schwarz yields $$\sum_{j\text{ marked at }z}
       \bigl(\log\beta_{J_j}-\log\beta_{J_j}^0\bigr)
       \le CpL^{C_b}M^{-1/2+\mu}.
 \label{res:marked-cost}$$

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
#### Unary segments and their energy telescope

Call an active index *unary* at $z$ if its cell is unmarked and $h_j^*(z)<1$. At such an index the path child exists: at the last possible block the drop would instead be $n_j>M$. A significant top has mass at least one. Thus every significant top eligible in $J_j$ must remain eligible in the path child, since the total mass removed from that child is $h_j^*<1$.

<a id="res:unary-decomposition"></a>
At a unary index write $$A_j=\lambda_j A_{j+1}+U_j+W_j.
  \label{res:unary-decomposition}$$ Here $\lambda_j$ is the path-child coefficient in <a href="#eq:src-18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-18">[eq:src-18]</a>; $U_j$ consists of the residual packets assigned to tops which remain eligible in the path child. These are exactly the packets of the fixed subset $\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}$ in <a href="#hier:deterministic-residual-subset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:deterministic-residual-subset">[hier:deterministic-residual-subset]</a> which stop in block $j$. The term $W_j$ collects all contributions of tops eligible in $J_j$ which do not continue in the path child, including their residual packets and, when present, their contributions through other children.

Those latter tops are all insignificant at $z$. Their contributions within $A_{J_j}$ are whole interval sums on individual tops, so [hier:amplitudes](#hier:amplitudes) bounds the contribution of each by $L^C(1+\left\|\zeta\right\|)|q_T|h_T^4$. Since $h_T$ is bounded above by an absolute constant and $|q_T|\le e^{-L\log L}$ there, this is at most $Ce^{-L}h_T$ for sufficiently large $L$. The masses of these tops sum to $h_j^*$. Therefore <a id="res:discarded-top-error"></a>$$\left\|W_j\right\|\le Ce^{-L}h_j^*.
  \label{res:discarded-top-error}$$ This estimate uses a sum of top masses, not the number of such tops.

<a id="res:segment-masses"></a>
Partition each consecutive run of unary indices into consecutive segments $[e,f)$. Starting at $e$, end a completed segment as soon as its accumulated drop is at least one. Each individual drop is less than one, so every completed segment has total drop in $[1,2)$. At the end of the run, retain the final remainder as one segment if it is nonempty. Hence every segment satisfies $$\Delta_{e,f}:=\sum_{e\le j<f}h_j^*=n_e-n_f<2,
  \qquad n_f>M-2,
  \qquad n_j\asymp n_e\quad(e\le j\le f).
  \label{res:segment-masses}$$ The endpoint remains an eligible path node, including when it is the node where the mass stopping rule first applies.

<a id="res:segment-shell-count"></a>
The number of segment starts in a mass shell obeys $$\#\{[e,f):\ t\le n_e<2t\}\le C(1+t),\qquad t\ge M.
  \label{res:segment-shell-count}$$ Completed segments account for disjoint drops of size at least one. For each remainder other than the final remainder on the path, charge it to the next interruption of a unary run. Such an interruption is either marked, with drop at least $c_m$, or is an active unmarked index with drop at least one. The mass at this interruption differs from the start mass of the remainder by less than two. Thus the interruptions charged by starts in $[t,2t)$ lie in the enlarged shell $[t-2,2t)$ and their fixed positive drops give $O(1+t)$ choices. The one possible final remainder adds one.

<a id="res:starred-subsolution"></a>
<a id="eq:src-28"></a>
For one segment define $$A_i^*=\left(\prod_{i\le j<f}\lambda_j\right)A_f,
 \qquad V_i^*=V(n_i,A_i^*),\qquad e\le i\le f,$$ with the empty product equal to one. Since $n_i\ge n_{i+1}$, $\alpha-p<0$, and $|\lambda_i|\le1$, we have $$V_i^*\le h_i^*+V_{i+1}^*,\qquad V_f^*=V_f.
  \label{res:starred-subsolution}$$ Indeed, the baseline terms differ by $h_i^*$, while the energy term on the left is at most $n_{i+1}^{\alpha-p}\left\|A_{i+1}^*\right\|^p$. The denominator of $\beta_{J_i}$ contains the energy of the path child and the nonnegative energies of all other children. It is therefore at least $h_i^*+V_{i+1}$. It follows that $$\sum_{e\le i<f}\log\beta_{J_i}
 \le \log V_e-\log V_f
       -\sum_{e\le i<f}\log(1+h_i^*/V_{i+1}).$$ On the other hand, <a href="#res:starred-subsolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:starred-subsolution">[res:starred-subsolution]</a> gives $$\log V_e^*-\log V_f
       \le\sum_{e\le i<f}\log(1+h_i^*/V_{i+1}^*).$$ Subtracting these inequalities produces the useful estimate $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{e\le i<f}\log\beta_{J_i}
 &\le \log(V_e/V_e^*)
       +\sum_{e\le i<f}
           \left[\log(1+h_i^*/V_{i+1}^*)
                 -\log(1+h_i^*/V_{i+1})\right] \\
 &\le Cp\, n_e^{-1+\mu}
            \max_{e\le i\le f}\left\|A_i-A_i^*\right\|.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:src-28}$$ To justify the last line, first use the Lipschitz estimate for $\mathcal L_p$ to obtain $$|\log V(n,A)-\log V(n,A')|
       \le p n^{\mu-1}\left\|A-A'\right\|.$$ For $h\ge0$, the derivative of $x\mapsto\log(1+he^{-x})$ has absolute value $h/(e^x+h)$. Along the interval between $\log V_{i+1}$ and $\log V_{i+1}^*$, both endpoint amounts, and therefore every intermediate amount, are at least $n_{i+1}$. The corresponding correction term is bounded by $$\frac{h_i^*}{n_{i+1}}
      p n_{i+1}^{\mu-1}\left\|A_{i+1}-A_{i+1}^*\right\|.$$ Now use <a href="#res:segment-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:segment-masses">[res:segment-masses]</a> and $\sum_{e\le i<f}h_i^*/n_{i+1}\le2/(M-2)$. This proves <a href="#eq:src-28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-28">[eq:src-28]</a>, including its endpoint case.

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
#### Common residual weights and variation

We give the coefficient calculation needed to apply the good-path variation estimate. It is important here that the same angular grid underlies all shifts.

<a id="res:common-weight"></a>
For integer levels $k$ along the path put $v_k=v_k(R)$ and $a_k=n_{v_k}$, so $a_{i_j}=n_j$. For a fixed segment $[e,f)$ and a block index $j\in[e,f)$, define $$\omega_j(k)=a_k^{-\mu/K}
     \prod_{r=i_j}^{k-1}
       \left(\frac{a_{r+1}}{m_{v_{r+1}}}\right)^{\zeta_{r\bmod K}},
 \qquad i_j\le k<i_f.
 \label{res:common-weight}$$ Here $i_j$ denotes the angular level at the start of block $j$.

Every packet in $U_b$, for $j\le b<f$, is assigned to a top in $v_{i_{b+1}}(R)$. It follows by nesting that its top lies in $v_k(R)$ at every intermediate level $k\le i_{b+1}$, in particular at its stopping level $k_s$. Multiplying its coefficient in $U_b$ by the path propagation factor $\prod_{j\le r<b}\lambda_r$ therefore gives exactly $\omega_j(k_s)q_{T(s)}\phi_s$. This identity holds for every contributing top, whether or not that top is significant. Thus the scalar coefficient depends only on the stopping level, rather than on the packet or top inside that level.

<a id="stmt-res-common-weight-variation"></a>
<div id="res:common-weight-variation" class="lemma">

<a id="res:common-weight-BV"></a>
**Lemma 45** (Variation of the common coefficients). *Uniformly over the segments and their starting indices, $$\sup_{i_j\le k<i_f}|\omega_j(k)|
    +\sum_{k=i_j}^{i_f-2}|\omega_j(k+1)-\omega_j(k)|
       \le L^{C_w}.
  \label{res:common-weight-BV}$$ The exponent $C_w$ is fixed before $M$ is chosen.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-common-weight-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-common-weight-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Every ending mass in this sequence is at least $a_{i_f}=n_f>M-2$. Since all real parts of $\zeta$ are nonnegative, $|\omega_j(k)|\le a_k^{-\mu/K}\le1$ when $M\ge3$. Using the real logarithms of positive masses, a logarithmic increment of the coefficient is $$\log\omega_j(k+1)-\log\omega_j(k)
 =\frac{\mu}{K}\log\frac{a_k}{a_{k+1}}
     -\zeta_{k\bmod K}\log\frac{m_{v_{k+1}}}{a_{k+1}}.$$ The logarithm here is the specified linear expression defining the coefficient, so no branch of a complex logarithm is being chosen. The first sum of absolute increments telescopes. For the second use [hier:weight-variation](#hier:weight-variation) on the entire root-to-endpoint path. All its summands are nonnegative, whence $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{k=i_j}^{i_f-2}
    |\log\omega_j(k+1)-\log\omega_j(k)|
 &\le \left(\frac{\mu}{K}
          +K\max_\sigma|\zeta_\sigma|\right)
            \log\frac{n_{\mathrm{root}}}{n_f} \\
 &\le L^{C_w}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last step uses $n_{\mathrm{root}}\le NL^C$ on $Z$, $\log N\le2L$, $n_f> M-2\ge1$, and $\max_\sigma|\zeta_\sigma|\le\mu+L^2$. The full-root bound is needed: a denominator $m_v$ belonging to a different shift may involve a block whose starting level precedes the segment. No estimate using only $\log(n_e/n_f)$ is asserted.

For two successive logarithmic values $z_0,z_1$ in this sequence, both $|e^{z_0}|$ and $|e^{z_1}|$ are at most one. Along the straight segment between them, $|e^{(1-t)z_0+tz_1}|\le1$. Integration of the derivative of the exponential consequently yields $|e^{z_1}-e^{z_0}|\le|z_1-z_0|$. The logarithmic-increment bound proves <a href="#res:common-weight-BV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:common-weight-BV">[res:common-weight-BV]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="res:segment-cube-sum"></a>
Let $\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}$ denote the fixed residual subset in <a href="#hier:deterministic-residual-subset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="hier:deterministic-residual-subset">[hier:deterministic-residual-subset]</a>. For each unary segment $[e,f)$ define $$\Lambda_{e,f}(z)=
    \max_{i_e\le a\le b<i_f}
       \left\|\sum_{\substack{s\in\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}\\
                              a\le k_s\le b}}
                       q_{T(s)}(z)\phi_s(z)\right\|.$$ An empty sum has value zero. Finite depth makes the maximum finite. The interval of stopping levels $[a,b]$ corresponds to an interval of dyadic packet lengths. The latter follows directly from the definition $k_s=\max\{k:d_s\le d(k)/B^2\}$, which is monotone in $\ell_s$. Since distinct unary segments have disjoint level intervals $[i_e,i_f)$, one may choose a maximizing interval in each segment and apply the single good-path $3$-variation bound to all those disjoint intervals. Thus $$\left(\sum_{[e,f)}\Lambda_{e,f}(z)^3\right)^{1/3}
       \le L^{C_v}.
  \label{res:segment-cube-sum}$$ The segments may depend on $z$; the variation is a supremum over all choices of disjoint intervals, so this dependence does not alter the inequality. The underlying packet subset itself is the single deterministic set $\mathcal Q_{R,\sigma}$.

<a id="res:segment-perturbation"></a>
Abel summation for a finite scalar sequence $(\omega_k)$ and vector sequence $(F_k)$ gives $$\left\|\sum_{k=a}^b\omega_k F_k\right\|
 \le \left(|\omega_b|+
             \sum_{k=a}^{b-1}|\omega_k-\omega_{k+1}|\right)
                \max_{a\le t\le b}
                       \left\|\sum_{k=a}^tF_k\right\|.$$ Applying this identity with <a href="#res:common-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:common-weight">[res:common-weight]</a>, and then iterating <a href="#res:unary-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:unary-decomposition">[res:unary-decomposition]</a>, yields $$\max_{e\le i\le f}\left\|A_i-A_i^*\right\|
    \le L^{C_w}\Lambda_{e,f}
           +Ce^{-L}\Delta_{e,f}.
 \label{res:segment-perturbation}$$ Indeed the propagated $W_j$ terms have modulus at most their unpropagated bounds because $|\lambda_j|\le1$. There are no residual terms beyond $i_f$ in this calculation: $U_j$ uses only $i_j\le k_s<i_{j+1}$ with $j<f$. When $f$ is a mass-stopping node it is still an eligible node and <a href="#res:segment-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:segment-masses">[res:segment-masses]</a> applies. A final block ending by ineligibility cannot be unary and is excluded from this calculation.

<a id="res:unary-cost"></a>
Using <a href="#res:segment-shell-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:segment-shell-count">[res:segment-shell-count]</a> and dyadic mass shells, we get <a id="res:segment-weight-sum"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \sum_{[e,f)}n_e^{(-1+\mu)3/2}
   &\le C\sum_{r\ge0}(2^rM)^{-1/2+3\mu/2}
    \le C M^{-1/2+3\mu/2},\\
 \left(\sum_{[e,f)}n_e^{(-1+\mu)3/2}\right)^{2/3}
   &\le C M^{-1/3+\mu}.
 \end{split}
 \label{res:segment-weight-sum}$$ Hölder with exponents $3/2$ and $3$, followed by <a href="#eq:src-28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-28">[eq:src-28]</a>, <a href="#res:segment-cube-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:segment-cube-sum">[res:segment-cube-sum]</a>, and <a href="#res:segment-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:segment-perturbation">[res:segment-perturbation]</a>, therefore bounds the sum of the unary log factors by $$\sum_{j\text{ unary at }z}\log\beta_{J_j}
       \le CpL^{C_u}M^{-1/3+\mu}
          +Cp e^{-L}\left(\max_{j\text{ active}}n_j^\mu\right)
                              \sum_{j\text{ active}}u_j.
 \label{res:unary-cost}$$ For the error term, use $n_e\ge n_j$ and $\mu-1<0$ within a segment to obtain $n_e^{\mu-1}h_j^*\le n_j^{\mu-1}h_j^*=n_j^\mu u_j$. By <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>, $\max n_j^\mu\le\exp(C\sqrt L)$. Together with <a href="#res:relative-drop-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:relative-drop-sum">[res:relative-drop-sum]</a>, this makes the second term in <a href="#res:unary-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:unary-cost">[res:unary-cost]</a> tend to zero.

<a id="res:choice-M"></a>
Choose a fixed exponent $C_{\mathrm{res}}$ larger than all polynomial exponents in <a href="#res:marked-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:marked-cost">[res:marked-cost]</a> and <a href="#res:unary-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:unary-cost">[res:unary-cost]</a>, and then choose $$A_M>4(C_{\mathrm{res}}+2),\qquad M=L^{A_M}.
  \label{res:choice-M}$$ By <a href="#res:small-mu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:small-mu">[res:small-mu]</a>, $-1/3+\mu\le-1/4$ and $-1/2+\mu\le-5/12$. Since $p=L^{1/2}$, this choice makes both principal residual costs bounded by an absolute constant for sufficiently large $L$. None of the good-path thresholds or the exponents used to select $A_M$ depended on $M$.

<a id="pm-h-63"></a>
#### The path product and its integral

<a id="stmt-res-path-product"></a>
<div id="res:path-product" class="theorem">

**Theorem 46** (Integrated path product). *With $M$ chosen by <a href="#res:choice-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:choice-M">[res:choice-M]</a>, the estimate <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a> holds for every path top $R$, every shift, and every vertex parameter with $\left\|\Im\zeta\right\|_2\le L^2$. Its constants are independent of the tile collection, angular depth, and Hilbert-space dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-path-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-path-product</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a line of slope $s_R$, and on that line use the functions $\Gamma_j$ constructed in [pred:marked-comparison](#pred:marked-comparison). At a point of $Z$ where $R$ is good, the active indices fall into the marked indices, the unary indices, and at most one active unmarked index with $h_j^*\ge1$. For the marked indices, combine <a href="#eq:src-24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-24">[eq:src-24]</a>, <a href="#res:marked-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:marked-cost">[res:marked-cost]</a>, and <a href="#res:relative-drop-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:relative-drop-sum">[res:relative-drop-sum]</a>. Their phase-domination losses have sum at most $CLG^{-c}$, which is bounded. The unary factors have bounded log product by <a href="#res:unary-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:unary-cost">[res:unary-cost]</a> and <a href="#res:choice-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:choice-M">[res:choice-M]</a>.

<a id="res:pointwise-product"></a>
At the possible remaining index, [hier:marking](#hier:marking) gives $M<n_j<M+1$. From <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> and the baseline $n_j$ in the denominator of $\beta_{J_j}$, $$\beta_{J_j}\le 1+L^{Cp}n_j^{\alpha-1}
       \le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C.$$ Here $C$ is fixed, since $\alpha$ stays in a fixed compact interval below three. We have therefore proved the pointwise domination $$\prod_{j\text{ active at }z}\beta_{J_j}(z)
       \le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C\prod_{j=0}^{J_R}\Gamma_j(z),
 \qquad z\in Z\cap\{R\text{ good}\},
 \label{res:pointwise-product}$$ where $J_R$ is the last possible block index for $R$. It is legitimate to include every possible index on the right: if $n_j(z)\le M$, its mesh cell cannot satisfy $\inf n_j>M$, so it is unmarked and $\Gamma_j(z)=1$. Unmarked active indices likewise have $\Gamma_j=1$.

<a id="res:mesh-average"></a>
Recall that $\Gamma_j$ is constant on the mesh of length $H_{j-1}$ and has average at most one on every cell of length $H_j$. The meshes are nested. On any cell $I$ of the largest mesh $H_{J_R}$, successive averaging from the smallest mesh upward therefore gives $$\int_I\prod_{j=0}^{J_R}\Gamma_j(x)\,\,\mathrm dx\le |I|.
  \label{res:mesh-average}$$ More explicitly, all later factors $\Gamma_k$, $k>j$, are constant on each $H_j$ cell. Integrating $\Gamma_j$ on those cells can thus only decrease the integral of the nonnegative product. Iterate this observation for $j=0,1,\ldots,J_R$.

The weight $h_R$ is uniformly comparable on $I$. This remains true for a terminal block and for the short first block: eligibility of $R$ at $i_j$ gives $\rho_j\ge B^2d_R$, and every block has $\rho_j/\rho_{j+1}\le B$. Thus <a id="res:mesh-versus-top"></a>$$H_j=\frac{w}{\rho_{j+1}\sqrt G}
        \le\frac{l_R}{B\sqrt G}\le l_R.
  \label{res:mesh-versus-top}$$ Along the line of slope $s_R$, the transverse normalized coordinate of $R$ is fixed and the horizontal normalized coordinate changes by at most $H_j/l_R$. The definition <a href="#eq:src-5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-5">[eq:src-5]</a> consequently gives $\sup_I h_R\le C\inf_Ih_R$, with an absolute constant depending only on the fixed decay exponent. By <a href="#res:mesh-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:mesh-average">[res:mesh-average]</a>, $$\int_I h_R\prod_{j=0}^{J_R}\Gamma_j\,\,\mathrm dx
     \le (\sup_Ih_R)|I|\le C\int_Ih_R\,\,\mathrm dx.$$ Sum over the largest-mesh cells and use <a href="#res:pointwise-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:pointwise-product">[res:pointwise-product]</a>. Whenever the original integrand in <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a> has a measurable slice on this line, we obtain $$\int_{Z\cap\{R\text{ good}\}\text{ on the line}}
       h_R\prod_{j\text{ active}}\beta_{J_j}\,\,\mathrm dx
       \le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C
                    \int_{\text{line}}h_R\,\,\mathrm dx.$$

The snapshots used to construct the $\Gamma_j$ need only be chosen on this one line. There are countably many mesh cells, so these choices give measurable step functions on that line. No jointly measurable choice of snapshots across different lines is required. The original integrand in <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a>, expressed entirely through $h_R$, the good sets, and the finite hierarchy factors $\beta_{J_j}$, is nonnegative and Lebesgue measurable. In the coordinates $(x,y-s_Rx)$, it has a measurable slice for almost every transverse parameter. The preceding line estimate therefore holds for almost every such parameter. Integrating it and applying Tonelli, using the Jacobian-one coordinate change, proves <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a>. ◻

</details>

All sums and products in this section were finite. The shell counts, Abel estimates, and mesh averaging bounds contain no factor equal to the number of scales, tiles, or hierarchy levels. They therefore apply with identical constants to every finite truncation required in the packet reduction.

<a id="assembly:section"></a>
### Interpolation, boundary averaging, and completion

We continue with a finite popular collection and its adjoint sum $P=\sum_s\phi_s$. Thus $N=(|E|/|F|)^{1/2}$, $L/2\le\log N\le2L$, and $S\lesssim L^C|E|$. All statements in this section are uniform in the finite collection, its depth, and the dimension of $\mathcal H$. We write $\vartheta$ for the translation of the angular grid. The set $Z_0$ and the functions $P,P_q,h_T,n$ do not depend on $\vartheta$, whereas the smaller set $Z=Z_\vartheta$, the angular masses, and the analytic sums do. Until the boundary-averaging step, we fix $\vartheta$ and suppress it in the notation.

<a id="pm-h-65"></a>
#### From the path estimate to analytic interpolation

<a id="stmt-assembly-vertices"></a>
<div id="assembly:vertices" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:src-29"></a>
**Proposition 47** (Vertex estimate). *For every nonempty root $v$, every shift $\sigma$, and every parameter with $$\Re\zeta=\mu e_\sigma,
 \qquad \|\Im\zeta\|_2\le L^2,$$ one has $$\label{eq:src-29}
 \big\|n_v^{\alpha/p-1}A_v(\zeta)\big\|_{L^p(Z;\mathcal H)}
       \le L^C S^{1/p}.$$ The constant and exponent are independent of the shift and the grid translation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-vertices"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-vertices</summary>

*Proof.* Recall the nonnegative amount $$V_v=n_v+n_v^{\alpha-p}\|A_v\|^p.$$ On the set where $n_v\le M$, Equation <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> gives $$V_v\le L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1}n_v.$$ Here and below the bound on the imaginary parts is absorbed into $L^{Cp}$. On the remaining set, apply Lemma <a href="#hier:path-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="hier:path-expansion">40</a>. At least half of the root amount is carried by good paths, and the terminal amount of a path ending at $R$ is at most $L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1}h_R$. Consequently, $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_Z V_v
 &\le L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1}\int n_v\\
 &\quad+2L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1}
   \sum_{R:\,s_R\in v}
   \int_{Z\cap\{\text{good }R\}}
        h_R\prod_{\text{active path of }R}\beta_J.
\end{aligned}$$ The root and its descendants contain only their eligible tops; sums over tops here and subsequently are understood with that convention. Theorem <a href="#res:path-product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="res:path-product">46</a>, namely Equation <a href="#eq:src-22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-22">[eq:src-22]</a>, bounds each integral in the second line by $L^{Cp}(1+M)^C\int h_R$. Moreover, $$\sum_R\int h_R\lesssim L^C S,$$ by the definition of the dilated decaying weights. We conclude that $$\int_Z V_v\le L^{Cp}(1+M)^C S.$$ Since $M$ is a fixed power of $L$, taking a $p$-th root and using $n_v^{\alpha-p}\|A_v\|^p\le V_v$ proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-assembly-interpolation"></a>
<div id="assembly:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 48** (Interpolation of the shift parameters). *Equation <a href="#eq:src-29" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-29">[eq:src-29]</a> also holds at $$\zeta^{\mathrm c}=(\mu/K,\ldots,\mu/K).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* Let $$\mathfrak S_\mu
 =\{a\in[0,\infty)^K:\ \textstyle\sum_\sigma a_\sigma=\mu\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal F_v(\zeta,z)=n_v(z)^{\alpha/p-1}A_v(\zeta,z).$$ Every nonempty mass is positive. For each fixed $z$, the defining sum is a finite sum of exponentials of linear functions of $\zeta$, with real logarithms of positive ratios as coefficients. In particular it is entire in $\zeta$.

<a id="assembly:crude-analytic-bound"></a>
We first give a uniform bound on the tube over $\mathfrak S_\mu$, without using the vertex estimate. From Equations <a href="#eq:src-17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-17">[eq:src-17]</a> and <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>, and from $\int n_v\le\int n\lesssim L^C S$, we have, for $a\in\mathfrak S_\mu$ and $\eta\in\mathbb R^K$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|\mathcal F_v(a+i\eta)\|_{L^p(Z)}
 &\le L^C(1+\mu+\|\eta\|_2)
           \left(\int_Z n_v^\alpha\right)^{1/p}\notag\\
 &\le L^C(1+\|\eta\|_2)
          (NL^C)^{(\alpha-1)/p}S^{1/p}.
 \label{assembly:crude-analytic-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The factor involving $N$ is at most $\exp(C\sqrt L)$, since $\log N\le2L$, $p=L^{1/2}$, and $2<\alpha<3$ for sufficiently large $L$.

Multiply the analytic family by $$\mathcal D(\zeta)=\exp\left(\sum_\sigma\zeta_\sigma^2\right).$$ On the tube under consideration, $$|\mathcal D(a+i\eta)|
       =\exp(\|a\|_2^2-\|\eta\|_2^2)
       \le\exp(\mu^2-\|\eta\|_2^2).$$ At a vertex, Proposition <a href="#assembly:vertices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:vertices">47</a> controls $\mathcal D\mathcal F_v$ whenever $\|\eta\|_2\le L^2$. For $\|\eta\|_2>L^2$, Equation <a href="#assembly:crude-analytic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:crude-analytic-bound">[assembly:crude-analytic-bound]</a> gives the same bound: indeed $$(1+\|\eta\|_2)
  \exp(\mu^2-\|\eta\|_2^2+C\sqrt L)$$ is bounded by an absolute constant in this range, after increasing the fixed lower threshold for $L$. Thus the damped family has norm at most $L^C S^{1/p}$ at every vertex, uniformly in all imaginary parts.

Here are details that justify applying scalar three-lines to this family. Test against any element $b$ of the dual unit ball $L^{p'}(Z;\mathcal H)$. One may first restrict the pairing to a bounded set on which every nonempty mass used in the finite family is bounded below by a positive constant. Such sets exhaust $Z$, and the restriction does not increase the norm of $b$. On each such set, integration defines an entire scalar function; logarithms of the finitely many masses are bounded there. The crude bound above and the damping make this scalar function bounded on every interpolating strip. If two real vectors $a_0,a_1$ have established uniform bounds for all imaginary parts, apply the bounded scalar three-lines lemma (Hajłasz 2012, Lemma 3.3, p. 57) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Hajlasz2012](#ref-Hajlasz2012)] to $$z\longmapsto
 \int_Z
  \mathcal D\big((1-z)a_0+za_1+i\eta\big)
  \big\langle
   \mathcal F_v\big((1-z)a_0+za_1+i\eta,\cdot\big),b
  \big\rangle\,\,\mathrm dx,$$ with the indicated preliminary restriction understood. The boundary lines have the required bounds because their imaginary vectors are still arbitrary. The real parts throughout the strip remain in the simplex. Starting with its vertices and successively taking convex combinations proves the same bound at their barycenter, with no factor depending on $K$. Exhaustion of $Z$, followed by duality, removes the preliminary restriction. Finally $\mathcal D(\zeta^{\mathrm c})=\exp(\mu^2/K)$ is an absolute factor. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-66"></a>
#### The central weight and the exponent budget

The central coefficient identity <a href="#eq:src-30" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-30">[eq:src-30]</a> gives weights $\kappa_s\in(0,1]$. Define $$\mathcal W_\vartheta
       =\sum_s\kappa_s q_{T(s)}\phi_s.$$ By that identity, its portion in root $v$ is $n_v^{\mu/K}A_v(\zeta^{\mathrm c})$.

<a id="assembly:central-estimate"></a>
Put $\gamma=1-\alpha/p+\mu/K$, which is positive for the values of $L$ under consideration. Interpolation and Hölder’s inequality give, for each root, $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_Z\big\|n_v^{\mu/K}A_v(\zeta^{\mathrm c})\big\|
 &\le
  \|n_v^{\alpha/p-1}A_v(\zeta^{\mathrm c})\|_{L^p(Z)}
  \left(\int_Z n_v^{\gamma p'}\right)^{1/p'}\\
 &\le L^C S^{1/p}|F|^{1-1/p}(NL^C)^\gamma.
\end{aligned}$$ Only an absolute number of roots meet the bounded set of top slopes. Since $S\lesssim L^C|E|=L^C N^2|F|$, summing over them proves $$\label{assembly:central-estimate}
 \int_{Z_\vartheta}\|\mathcal W_\vartheta\|
       \le L^C|F|N^{\,1+(2-\alpha)/p+\mu/K}.$$

<a id="assembly:central-power-gain"></a>
We record explicitly why this improves by every fixed negative power of $L$. The net decrease of the exponent of $N$ is $$\delta_L
   =\frac{\alpha-2}{p}-\frac\mu K
   =\frac1p\left(\alpha-2-\frac{\alpha-1}{K}\right).$$ The separated-count bound is a fixed power of $L$, so the definition of $\alpha$ gives $\alpha-2\ge c_1/\log L$ with a fixed $c_1>0$. Also $\alpha-1\le2$ and $K\ge(\log L)^2$. For sufficiently large $L$, therefore, $$\delta_L\ge\frac{c_1}{2\sqrt L\log L},
 \qquad
 \delta_L\log N\ge\frac{c_1\sqrt L}{4\log L}.$$ In particular, for every fixed $Q>0$, after increasing the absolute lower threshold for $L$, Equation <a href="#assembly:central-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:central-estimate">[assembly:central-estimate]</a> implies, uniformly in $\vartheta$, $$\label{assembly:central-power-gain}
 \int_{Z_\vartheta}\|\mathcal W_\vartheta\|
           \le L^{-Q}N|F|.$$ This conclusion absorbs any fixed polynomial losses in the preceding estimates; none of their exponents is changed here.

<a id="pm-h-67"></a>
#### Boundary averaging with the eligibility condition

We now restore the dependence on the uniformly translated angular grid. The next estimate is pointwise on the fixed set $Z_0$.

<a id="stmt-assembly-boundary-loss"></a>
<div id="assembly:boundary-loss" class="lemma">

<a id="assembly:expected-error"></a>
**Lemma 49** (Expected boundary loss). *Let $\epsilon=4G^{-1/4}$. For $z\in Z_0$, $$\label{assembly:expected-error}
 \mathbb E_\vartheta\|P_q(z)-\mathcal W_\vartheta(z)\|
                    \le L^C\epsilon n(z).$$ Here the expectation is normalized Lebesgue measure for the common translation modulo $d(0)$; independent translations at different levels are neither assumed nor needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-boundary-loss"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-boundary-loss</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $z\in Z_0$ and a top $T$, and suppress $z$ from the masses. For $i<k_T$ let $$r_i(\vartheta)
       =\log\frac{m_{v_{i+1}(T)}}{n_{v_i(T)}}\ge0,
 \qquad
 M_i(b)=\sum_{\substack{U:\,|s_U-s_T|\le b\,d(i)\\
                              d_U\le d(i)/B^2}}h_U.$$ Unlike $r_i$, the latter mass does not depend on the grid translation. Each mass in this display is at least $h_T$ when $i<k_T$, and is at most $n$.

<a id="assembly:one-boundary-loss"></a>
Let $b_i(\vartheta)\in[0,1/2]$ be the distance from $s_T$ to the nearer boundary of its level-$i$ interval, divided by $d(i)$. An extra top counted in $m_{v_{i+1}(T)}-n_{v_i(T)}$ has slope within $\epsilon d(i)$ of $s_T$. Indeed the neighbor distance in Equation <a href="#eq:src-14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-14">[eq:src-14]</a> is $G^{-1/4}d(i)$, and the two level-$(i+1)$ interval lengths add at most $2d(i)/G$. Such a top is eligible at level $i+1$, hence also at level $i$. It lies outside the immediate parent. It follows that $r_i=0$ if $b_i>\epsilon$. If $0<b_i\le\epsilon$, the immediate parent contains every top counted by $M_i(b_i)$, whereas its extra mass is disjoint from these inner tops and is at most $M_i(\epsilon)-M_i(b_i)$. Consequently $$\label{assembly:one-boundary-loss}
 r_i(\vartheta)
       \le\log\frac{M_i(\epsilon)}{M_i(b_i(\vartheta))}.$$ Endpoint coincidences can be omitted: for the finite set of slopes and levels they form a null set of translations.

<a id="assembly:mass-inclusion"></a>
<a id="assembly:boundary-telescope"></a>
For a fixed number $0<b<\epsilon$, set $r=\lceil\log_G(\epsilon/b)\rceil$. The inclusion $$\label{assembly:mass-inclusion}
 M_{i+r}(\epsilon)\le M_i(b)$$ uses both conditions in the definition of $M_i$: the radius satisfies $\epsilon d(i+r)\le b d(i)$, and the precision threshold $d(i+r)/B^2$ is no larger than $d(i)/B^2$. To handle the end of the path without losing a term, define $$a_i=\begin{cases}
       \log(M_i(\epsilon)/h_T),&0\le i<k_T,\\
       0,&i\ge k_T.
      \end{cases}$$ If $i+r<k_T$, Equation <a href="#assembly:mass-inclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:mass-inclusion">[assembly:mass-inclusion]</a> applies. If $i+r\ge k_T$, use $M_i(b)\ge h_T$ instead. In both cases $$\log\frac{M_i(\epsilon)}{M_i(b)}\le a_i-a_{i+r}.$$ Summing and cancelling gives $$\label{assembly:boundary-telescope}
 \sum_{i<k_T}\log\frac{M_i(\epsilon)}{M_i(b)}
 \le\sum_{0\le i<\min(r,k_T)}a_i
 \le r\log\frac n{h_T}.$$ This argument also covers $k_T=0$, with empty sums.

Because $d(0)/d(i)=G^i$ is an integer, the common uniform translation is uniform modulo every $d(i)$. Thus each $b_i$ has density $2$ on $[0,1/2]$. Linearity of expectation, Equation <a href="#assembly:one-boundary-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:one-boundary-loss">[assembly:one-boundary-loss]</a>, and Equation <a href="#assembly:boundary-telescope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:boundary-telescope">[assembly:boundary-telescope]</a> imply <a id="assembly:total-boundary-loss"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\vartheta\sum_{i<k_T}r_i(\vartheta)
 &\le2\int_0^\epsilon
       \sum_{i<k_T}\log\frac{M_i(\epsilon)}{M_i(b)}\,\,\mathrm db\notag\\
 &\le2\log(n/h_T)
          \int_0^\epsilon\lceil\log_G(\epsilon/b)\rceil\,\,\mathrm db
 \le C\epsilon\log(n/h_T).
 \label{assembly:total-boundary-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ For completeness the integral in the last line equals $\epsilon/(1-G^{-1})$: express the ceiling as a sum of indicators of $b<\epsilon G^{-j}$, $j\ge0$, and integrate. The dependence of the different $b_i$’s is absent from this calculation.

On the fixed top $T$, the value of $\kappa_s$ depends only on the stopping level $k_s$. As packet length increases, $k_s$ increases; Equation <a href="#eq:src-30" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-30">[eq:src-30]</a> shows that these values are nonincreasing. Their deficits from one are nondecreasing and satisfy $$0\le1-\kappa_s
       \le\frac\mu K\sum_{i<k_T}r_i(\vartheta),$$ using $k_s\le k_T$ and $1-e^{-x}\le x$. Group the packets by stopping level. Every interval of these levels is an interval of packet lengths, so Equation <a href="#eq:src-11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-11">[eq:src-11]</a> bounds its unweighted sum by $L^C h_T^8$. Summation by parts against the monotone deficits, and $|q_T|\le1$, therefore give $$\left\|\sum_{s:T(s)=T}(1-\kappa_s)q_T\phi_s\right\|
  \le L^C h_T^8\frac\mu K\sum_{i<k_T}r_i(\vartheta).$$ For example, write the deficits as sums of their nonnegative increments and use the bound on the corresponding suffix sums; the sum of these increments is their largest value. Taking expectations, using $\mu/K\le1$, and summing over tops proves $$\mathbb E_\vartheta\|P_q-\mathcal W_\vartheta\|
       \le L^C\epsilon\sum_T h_T^8\log(n/h_T).$$ The weights have a fixed absolute upper bound. For every such weight, $$h_T^8\log(n/h_T)
       \le C h_T(1+\log_+ n),$$ because $h^7\log_+(1/h)$ is bounded on a fixed bounded interval. Equation <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a> gives $1+\log_+n\lesssim L$ on $Z_0$. This proves Equation <a href="#assembly:expected-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:expected-error">[assembly:expected-error]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-68"></a>
#### Recovering the original adjoint sum

<a id="stmt-assembly-improved-model"></a>
<div id="assembly:improved-model" class="theorem">

**Theorem 50** (Improvement for the popular model). *The popular adjoint sum satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:src-7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-7">[eq:src-7]</a>; namely, $$\int_F\|P\|\lesssim L^{-20}N|F|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-improved-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-improved-model</summary>

<a id="assembly:fixed-exceptions"></a>
*Proof.* We make the exceptional-set comparison explicit because the good set depends on the grid. Write $\|P\|_2\le L^{C_P}\sqrt{|E|}$, as supplied by Equation <a href="#eq:src-4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-4">[eq:src-4]</a>. Proposition <a href="#conc:mask-removal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conc:mask-removal">29</a> and Proposition <a href="#count:simultaneous" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="count:simultaneous">30</a> allow the fixed polynomial thresholds to be chosen so that $$\label{assembly:fixed-exceptions}
 \int_{F\setminus Z_0}\|P\|\le L^{-30}N|F|,
 \qquad
 \int_{Z_0}\|P-P_q\|\le L^{-30}N|F|.$$ Indeed a discarded area at most $L^{-2C_P-60}|F|$ has the stated first bound by Cauchy–Schwarz. The exponentially small concentration and count exceptions meet this area bound for sufficiently large $L$.

<a id="assembly:moving-exceptions"></a>
For each fixed translation the good-path construction gives $$|Z_0\setminus Z_\vartheta|\le S e^{-9L}.$$ This is Markov’s inequality applied to the weighted bad-path mass: its integral is at most $S e^{-10L}$, and the deletion threshold is $e^{-L}$. Since $S\lesssim L^C N^2|F|$ and $N^2\le e^{4L}$, $$\label{assembly:moving-exceptions}
 \int_{Z_0\setminus Z_\vartheta}\|P\|
       \le L^{C_P}\sqrt{|E|}\,(S e^{-9L})^{1/2}
       \le L^C e^{-5L/2}N|F|
       \le L^{-30}N|F|.$$ These are bounds for each translation; no union over translations is taken.

<a id="assembly:original-decomposition"></a>
For every $\vartheta$, the triangle inequality gives the following decomposition, whose left side is independent of $\vartheta$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_F\|P\|
 &\le \int_{F\setminus Z_\vartheta}\|P\|
      +\int_{Z_\vartheta}\|P-P_q\|
      +\int_{Z_\vartheta}\|P_q-\mathcal W_\vartheta\|
      +\int_{Z_\vartheta}\|\mathcal W_\vartheta\|.
 \label{assembly:original-decomposition}
\end{aligned}$$ The first two terms are controlled by Equations <a href="#assembly:fixed-exceptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:fixed-exceptions">[assembly:fixed-exceptions]</a> and <a href="#assembly:moving-exceptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:moving-exceptions">[assembly:moving-exceptions]</a>. The final term is controlled by Equation <a href="#assembly:central-power-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:central-power-gain">[assembly:central-power-gain]</a> with $Q=30$. Average the remaining term over translations. It is nonnegative and $Z_\vartheta\subset Z_0$, so Tonelli’s theorem and Lemma <a href="#assembly:boundary-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:boundary-loss">49</a> yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\vartheta\int_{Z_\vartheta}
       \|P_q-\mathcal W_\vartheta\|
 &\le\int_{Z_0}\mathbb E_\vartheta
       \|P_q-\mathcal W_\vartheta\|\\
 &\le L^C\epsilon\int_{Z_0}n
 \le L^C\epsilon N|F|
 \le L^{-30}N|F|.
\end{aligned}$$ The penultimate inequality uses Equation <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>; the last uses $\epsilon=4G^{-1/4}$ and $\log G\asymp L^{.7}$. All functions being averaged are measurable: for finitely many tops, packets, and levels, the angular assignments change only at finitely many translation values, and the good sets were defined by measurable quantities. Thus this averaging is legitimate even though $Z_\vartheta$ and $\mathcal W_\vartheta$ use the same translation. Averaging Equation <a href="#assembly:original-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:original-decomposition">[assembly:original-decomposition]</a> now proves the asserted estimate. In particular, on every discarded set we have estimated the original sum $P$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-69"></a>
#### Choice of parameters and completion of the reductions

We collect the order of the absolute choices. First fix the packet adaptation, separation, and overlap constants and the fixed geometric enlargements used with [red:kakeya](#red:kakeya). Fix the local slope bound $C_{\rm Lip}$, strip length $b_0$, and outer length $a=a_*$ as in <a href="#red:local-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:local-operator">[red:local-operator]</a>, with the required room for those enlargements. Choose the low-density exponent $C_2$ by <a href="#tile:eq-improvement-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tile:eq-improvement-loss">[tile:eq-improvement-loss]</a>, before constructing the popular forest.

Next fix the decay exponent in the top-count weight and the concentration orders that determine $K_1$. Choose the fixed mask-removal and exceptional-area powers, then choose $A$ using the count at dilation $K_1$, whose constants do not depend on $A$. The count at the larger dilation $K_0=L^{A+2}$ is used only after this comparison. Fix the threshold in <a href="#eq:src-12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-12">[eq:src-12]</a>, the separated-count exponent defining $D$, and the good-path thresholds. These choices determine the polynomial powers in the motion, prediction, and residual estimates.

Now choose $M=L^{A_M}$ as in <a href="#res:choice-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:choice-M">[res:choice-M]</a>. For large $L$, $\mu<1/12$, so this fixed exponent controls both $$pL^C M^{-1/2+\mu}
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 pL^C M^{-1/3+\mu}$$ in the marked and unary residual estimates. The good-path thresholds were fixed before $A_M$, and their required proportion remains valid: $$e^{-L}L^{Cp}(1+M)^{\alpha-1}
       =\exp\big(-L+O(\sqrt L\log L)+O(\log L)\big)<\tfrac12$$ for sufficiently large $L$.

Finally choose $L_0$ after the outer length and all fixed exponents. It ensures $\tau<a$, the geometric smallness requirements, and all the inequalities used above, because $$\log B=O(L^{.7}(\log L)^2)=o(L^{.9}),\qquad
 \log(N^\mu)=O(\sqrt L)=o(\log G),$$ and, for every fixed exponent $C$, $$\log(m^C\tau)=O(L^{.9})-\Theta(L^{.97})\longrightarrow-\infty.$$ It also enforces the saving in <a href="#assembly:central-power-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="assembly:central-power-gain">[assembly:central-power-gain]</a>. All choices are independent of the depth, tile count, and dimension.

<a id="stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates"></a>
<div id="assembly:all-model-estimates" class="theorem">

**Theorem 51** (The three model estimates). *For the packet forms in <a href="#eq:src-3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-3">[eq:src-3]</a>, let $u$ be a bounded measurable real slope, let $F,E\subset\mathbb R^2$ be measurable sets of positive finite measure, and suppose $\|f\|_{\mathcal H}\le\mathbf 1_F$ and $\|g\|_{\mathcal H}\le\mathbf 1_E$ pointwise. The following estimates hold uniformly over every finite subcollection.*

1.  *For $m\ge1$, $$|\Lambda(f,g)|\lesssim(1+\log(2m))^C\sqrt{|F||E|}.$$*

2.  *Suppose $L\ge L_0$, with $\tau,m,W$ chosen by <a href="#red:small-time-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:small-time-parameters">[red:small-time-parameters]</a>, and $L/2\le\log\sqrt{|E|/|F|}\le2L$. If $\left|u\right|\le1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(u)\le C_{\rm Lip}$, and $F,E\subset I\times\mathbb R$ for an interval $I$ of length at most $b_0$, then $$|\Lambda(f,g)|\lesssim L^{-20}\sqrt{|F||E|}.$$*

3.  *For the scalar model with $m=1$ and $|E|\le|F|$, there is an absolute $c>0$ such that $$|\Lambda(f,g)|\lesssim
           \sqrt{|F||E|}\left(\frac{|E|}{|F|}\right)^c.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* The first and third assertions are precisely the ordinary estimates proved with <a href="#eq:src-4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-4">[eq:src-4]</a>. For the second, the fixed deletion of tiles with $D_E(s)\le L^{-C_2}$ in [forest:section](#forest:section) contributes at most $CL^{-40}\sqrt{|F||E|}$, by <a href="#tile:eq-improvement-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tile:eq-improvement-loss">[tile:eq-improvement-loss]</a>. Assign the remaining tiles to the popular tops as already constructed. Their adjoint sum is $P$, and the coefficient convention in Equation <a href="#eq:src-3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-3">[eq:src-3]</a> gives $$|\Lambda_{\mathrm{popular}}(f,g)|
       \le\int_F\|P\|.$$ Theorem <a href="#assembly:improved-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:improved-model">50</a> bounds this by $L^{-20}N|F|=L^{-20}\sqrt{|F||E|}$. Adding the deleted contribution proves the second assertion. All preceding arguments apply to arbitrary finite tile sets and thus give the claimed uniformity. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-all-model-estimates</summary>

*Completion of the proofs of [thm:band-restricted](#thm:band-restricted), [thm:main](#thm:main).* Theorem <a href="#assembly:all-model-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:all-model-estimates">51</a> supplies exactly the three hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#red:model-suffices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:model-suffices">15</a>. That proposition therefore proves the band restricted estimate <a href="#eq:src-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src-1">[eq:src-1]</a>. The dependence here is forward: the reductions established the implication from the model estimates, which have now been proved without using the band restricted theorem.

All estimates were obtained first for finite packet collections and positive inner truncations, with constants independent of both. The summable smooth expansions and translated-lattice averages in the packet reduction consequently pass to their limits as specified in Proposition <a href="#red:model-suffices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:model-suffices">15</a>. The amplitude summation, vertical Littlewood–Paley reassembly, and spatial localization proved in the reductions give a uniform strong $L^2$ estimate for the original fixed-field transform with a positive inner cutoff. For a Schwartz input its symmetric principal value exists pointwise; Fatou’s lemma gives the same strong bound for the principal-value operator, which extends by density. For the already chosen outer length $a=a_*$, Chebyshev’s inequality gives the weak $(2,2)$ assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The constants are independent of the vector field, the input, and the level in that inequality. The dilation calculation following [thm:main](#thm:main) gives the outer length $a_*/\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(v)$ for positive Lipschitz seminorm; constant directions have already been handled by the one-dimensional theorem. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "Shifted blocks and boundary masses, schematically. The upper diagram uses $K = 3$. Each shift starts with $1 + σ$ transitions and then uses blocks of $K$ transitions; colored arrows are the final transitions for that shift. At a final transition $i → i + 1$, the mass $m_{v}$ includes all of $n_{b}$ and the masses of neighboring children outside $b$ but inside $J$. The neighbor radius is determined by the immediate scale $d(i)$, even when $J$ begins much earlier. Neither diagram is to scale.",

\\node[anchor=west] at (7.05,-2.27) {\\(\\sim G^{-1/4}d(i)\\)};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (4.9,-2.27) {local neighbor radius};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "The upper diagram illustrates discrete levels numbered 0 through 9 along horizontal axes for three shifts $\\sigma = 0$, $\\sigma = 1$, and $\\sigma = 2$. Each shift consists of sequences of transitions grouped into blocks of length $K=3$ (preceded by $1+\\sigma$ initial transitions), with final transitions within each block marked by colored arrows (blue for $\\sigma = 0$, green for $\\sigma = 1$, orange for $\\sigma = 2$) and gray arrows for non-final transitions. The lower diagram shows interval containment and boundary neighbors: a broad horizontal interval labeled \"block ancestor $J$\" contains an interval labeled \"immediate parent $b$\" (shaded in blue with vertical subdivisions), which contains an element $v$ indicated by an upward arrow. Adjacent to $b$ are interval segments labeled \"neighbors $v'$\" (shaded in orange with vertical subdivisions). A horizontal bidirectional arrow beneath indicates the \"local neighbor radius $\\sim G^{-1/4}d(i)$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The horizontal-length axis (schematic) for a marked block. Here $Q = G^{3/4}ρ_{j + 1}$ is the retained angular separation and $Gρ_{j + 1} ≤ ρ_{j} ≤ Bρ_{j + 1}$, including the initial block. The quantity $l_{*} = B^{2}w/ρ_{j + 1}$ is the lower bound for predictable packet lengths in [hier:predictable-length]. On an interval of length $H_{j}$, the separated relative phases oscillate many times while the packet envelopes move little. The preceding mesh also satisfies $H_{j − 1}ρ_{j}/w = G^{−1/2}$, which controls the step supremums. Absolute frequency constants are suppressed.",

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,>=Stealth]\n \\draw[->,thick] (0,0)--(11.7,0);\n \\foreach \\xx in {.7,3.8,6.75,10.5}\n   \\draw[thick] (\\xx,-.12)--(\\xx,.12);\n \\node[above,align=center] at (.7,.2)\n   {\\(H_{j-1}\\)\\\\[-1pt]\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{w}{\\rho_j\\sqrt G}\\)};\n \\node[above,align=center] at (3.8,.2)\n   {phase scale\\\\[-1pt]\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{w}{Q}\\)};\n \\node[above,align=center] at (6.75,.2)\n   {\\(H_j\\)\\\\[-1pt]\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{w}{\\rho_{j+1}\\sqrt G}\\)};\n \\node[above,align=center] at (10.5,.2)\n   {\\(l_*\\)\\\\[-1pt]\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{B^2w}{\\rho_{j+1}}\\)};\n \\draw[<->,blue!65!black] (3.8,-.65)--(6.75,-.65);\n \\node[below,blue!65!black] at (5.275,-.68)\n   {\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{QH_j}{w}=G^{1/4}\\)};\n \\draw[<->,green!40!black] (6.75,-1.35)--(10.5,-1.35);\n \\node[below,green!40!black] at (8.625,-1.38)\n   {\\(\\displaystyle\\frac{l_*}{H_j}=B^2\\sqrt G\\)};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
